Q01 Bouma Sequence & Turbidite Current Dynamics
Sedimentology & Stratigraphy / Question 1
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · Sedimentology · Chapter Masterclass

Turbidite Dynamics, The Bouma Sequence & Sole Marks

Comprehensive Textbook Study Guide & Complete Solution for DGMS 2019 Exam (Question 1)
Standard Reference: Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Edition, 2006)
PART I
Theoretical Foundation & Direct Textbook Analysis
Sediment gravity flows, hydrodynamics of waning turbidity currents, the Bouma sequence, and sole mark diagnostics
1. Sediment Gravity Flows vs. Fluid Gravity Flows

In deep-marine and lacustrine basins, sediment transport is predominantly driven by sediment gravity flows—flows in which sediment is moved down-slope under the direct pull of gravity, dragging the interstitial fluid with it (Middleton & Hampton, 1973; Boggs, 2006, Chapter 2).

"Sediment gravity flows are distinguished from fluid gravity flows (such as rivers and ocean surface currents) because the sediment moves under the direct action of gravity and the fluid is dragged along with the sediment, rather than the fluid moving under gravity and carrying the sediment along... Four fundamental mechanisms of sediment support operate in sediment gravity flows: (1) fluid turbulence (turbidity currents), (2) upward escape of pore fluid (liquefied/fluidized flows), (3) grain-to-grain dispersive pressure (grain flows), and (4) matrix strength of cohesive mud (debris flows)." — Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Ed., 2006), pp. 54–57

A turbidity current is a density current in which sediment is held in suspension by fluid turbulence. As a turbidity current surges downslope through a submarine canyon or across a basin floor, its velocity progressively decreases due to slope reduction, friction, and fluid entrainment. This waning flow regime results in characteristic vertical grading and sedimentary structures.

2. Hydrodynamics of Waning Flow: The Bouma Sequence (Ta – Te)

Arnold Bouma (1962) recognized that deposits of deceleration-dominated turbidity currents exhibit an idealized vertical succession of five structural subdivisions, universally known as the Bouma Sequence:

"Bouma (1962) proposed an ideal turbidite sequence, now commonly called a Bouma sequence. This ideal sequence consists of five structural units (Fig. 2.8) that include the characteristics of turbidites. These structural subdivisions record the decay of flow strength of a turbidity current with time at a given point on the sea floor (waning flow) as current-flow velocity wanes through upper flow regime into lower flow regime." — Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Ed., 2006), p. 58
  • Ta (Massive or Graded Sandstone): Coarsest division at the base. Rapid, catastrophic sedimentation from high-concentration suspension in the upper flow regime. Internal structure is massive or displays normal grading (fining upward).
  • Tb (Lower Plane-Laminated Sandstone): Medium to fine sandstone displaying parallel, horizontal laminations formed by traction transport under upper flow regime plane-bed conditions.
  • Tc (Ripple Cross-Laminated Sandstone): Fine to very fine sandstone exhibiting current ripple cross-laminations, climbing ripples, or convolute lamination, reflecting deposition in the lower flow regime as current velocity wanes.
  • Td (Upper Plane-Laminated Siltstone): Faint, parallel-laminated silt and clay deposited by settling from dilute suspension under weak traction.
  • Te (Pelagic / Hemipelagic Mud): Structureless hemipelagic mud deposited by slow suspension settling of ocean background sediment.
Figure 2.8 (Boggs, Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy, 2006, p. 58): The ideal Bouma sequence showing the five structural divisions (Ta through Te) deposited by a decelerating turbidity current, illustrating upward fining from massive coarse sand to pelagic mud.
3. Sole Marks: Flute Casts as Downslope Turbidity Current Indicators

Sole marks are positive-relief structures preserved on the underside (sole) of sandstone beds overlying muddy strata. Among sole marks, flute casts are the diagnostic hallmark of high-energy turbidity currents.

"Flute casts are elongated, scoop-shaped structures that occur on the soles of turbidite beds. The genesis of these structures involves localized erosion of cohesive mud bottoms by turbulent eddies generated in the high-velocity head of a turbidity current (Fig. 4.35). As the current wanes, sand fills the scoured depressions, preserving positive casts on the underside of the sandstone bed (Fig. 4.36). The bulbous, deepest end of a flute cast points up-current, and the flared, shallower end points down-current, providing an unambiguous paleocurrent indicator." — Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Ed., 2006), pp. 116–117
Figure 4.35 (Boggs, 2006, p. 117): Diagrammatic representation of flute mark genesis by localized turbulent eddy scouring into cohesive mud bottom, followed by sand infilling.
Figure 4.36 (Boggs, 2006, p. 117): Flute casts on the base of a turbidite sandstone bed. The bulbous, pointed ends point in the up-current direction, establishing flow toward the upper right.
4. Contour Currents vs. Turbidity Currents: Key Distinctions

A classic trap in sedimentary petrology is distinguishing turbidites from contourites:

  • Flow Geometry: Turbidity currents flow downslope (perpendicular to bathymetric contours), driven by gravitational instability. Contour currents flow along-slope (parallel to bathymetric contours), driven by deep-water thermohaline ocean circulation and deflected by the Coriolis force.
  • Internal Layering: Turbidites feature the characteristic upward-fining, sequential Bouma subdivisions (Massive $\rightarrow$ Plane-Laminated $\rightarrow$ Ripple-Laminated). Contourites are typically thin, intensely bioturbated, winnowed silt/mud layers lacking the Bouma sequence.
  • Sole Marks: Flute casts require rapid, catastrophic downslope turbulent eddy erosion. Contour currents are semi-continuous, low-velocity traction currents that do not generate basal flute casts.
PART II
DGMS 2019 Examination: Question & Worked Solution
Rigorous option-by-option critical analysis and definitive textbook-grounded answer
❓ Original Question — DGMS 2019 (Junior Scientific Officer)

Q.1. A normal graded bed of sandstone is noticed with flute cast at its base and internally made up of massive, plane-laminated and ripple-laminated subdivisions, in order of superposition. The sandstone bed is interpreted as a product of

(a)   Debris flow
(b)   Grain flow
(c)   Turbidity current
(d)   Contour current
✔
Correct Answer: (c) Turbidity current
The combination of basal flute casts, normal grading (fining upward), and the upward succession of massive (Ta) ➔ plane-laminated (Tb) ➔ ripple-laminated (Tc) subdivisions is the exact definition of a classic Bouma-sequence turbidite bed.
🔍 Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
✗  (a) Debris flow INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: Debris flows are supported by matrix cohesive strength and buoyant raft pressure. They produce debrites—chaotic, poorly sorted, matrix-supported diamictites. They lack traction structures (no plane or ripple laminations), do not display normal Bouma grading, and rarely form flute casts because their laminar, plug-like flow does not generate turbulent eddy scours.

✗  (b) Grain flow INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: Grain flows are supported purely by dispersive pressure arising from grain-to-grain collisions (Bagnold, 1954). In subaqueous settings, grain flows are physically restricted to steep slopes exceeding the angle of repose (~30°). They produce very thin units (<5 cm), are massive to reverse-graded (due to kinetic sieving), and completely lack tractional ripple laminations and basal flute marks.

✔  (c) Turbidity current CORRECT

Why it is correct: As described in Boggs (2006, pp. 58, 116–117), a decelerating turbidity current deposits the diagnostic Bouma sequence:
• Flute casts at base: Scoured by turbulent eddies in the fast, dense head.
• Massive division (Ta): Rapid sedimentation from suspension.
• Plane-laminated division (Tb): Upper flow-regime traction transport.
• Ripple-laminated division (Tc): Lower flow-regime current ripple migration.
The description in the question matches the classic Ta–c turbidite profile with 100% fidelity.

✗  (d) Contour current INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: Contour currents flow parallel to bathymetric contours along the continental slope, driven by thermohaline circulation. They are low-velocity, semi-continuous traction flows that lack catastrophic downslope surges, do not generate the Bouma sequence, and do not produce basal flute casts pointing downslope.

PART III
Synthesis Reference Tables & High-Yield Practice Questions
Quick-reference diagnostic tables and 8 curated exam questions for DGMS, UPSC GSI, and IIT-JAM
📊 Synthesis Tables: Gravity Flows & The Bouma Divisions

Table 1: Classification of Subaqueous Sediment Gravity Flows

Flow Type Support Mechanism Depositional Structure Grading Style Sole Marks
Turbidity Current Fluid turbulence Bouma sequence (Ta–Te) Normal grading (fining upward) Flute casts, groove casts
Debris Flow Matrix strength (cohesive mud) Chaotic, unstratified diamictite Ungraded or reverse graded Rare / absent
Grain Flow Dispersive pressure (grain collisions) Massive, thin sheets (<5 cm) Reverse grading (kinetic sieving) Absent
Fluidized Flow Upward escape of pore fluid Dish structures, fluid escape pipes Poorly graded Absent

Table 2: Divisions of the Ideal Bouma Sequence (Boggs, 2006)

Division Name Sedimentary Structure Flow Regime Grain Size
Ta Massive / Graded Structureless or normally graded Upper flow regime (rapid dump) Coarse to medium sand
Tb Lower Plane Bed Parallel horizontal laminations Upper flow regime plane bed Medium to fine sand
Tc Rippled / Convolute Current ripple & convolute laminae Lower flow regime (ripples) Fine to very fine sand / silt
Td Upper Plane Bed Faint parallel laminations Lower flow regime (suspension) Silt to clay
Te Pelagic Mud Structureless mud / shale Hemipelagic background settling Clay / pelagic biogenic mud
🎯 Probable High-Yield Exam Questions (UPSC GSI, DGMS, IIT-JAM & GATE)
  • Q1. In the Bouma sequence, the division that represents the transition into the lower flow regime is:
    (a) Ta     (b) Tb     (c) Tc     (d) Td
    → Correct Answer: (c) Tc
    Explanation: Ta and Tb represent upper flow regime conditions (rapid suspension dumping and upper plane bed). Tc represents current ripple cross-laminations, which form strictly under lower flow regime conditions.
  • Q2. In a flute cast, the paleocurrent direction is determined by:
    (a) The pointed, bulbous end points down-current
    (b) The pointed, bulbous end points up-current, and the flared end points down-current
    (c) Flutes are symmetrical and give only the trend, not the direction
    (d) Flute marks are perpendicular to current direction
    → Correct Answer: (b)
    Explanation: Turbulent eddy vortices scour deepest at the up-current impact point, creating a bulbous beak that tapers and flares out in the down-current flow direction (Boggs, 2006, p. 117).
  • Q3. Reverse grading in coarse clastic sedimentary beds is characteristic of:
    (a) Turbidity currents     (b) Grain flows     (c) Low-density suspension     (d) Contour currents
    → Correct Answer: (b) Grain flows
    Explanation: Grain flows are dominated by dispersive pressure from grain collisions. Large clasts experience greater dispersive pressure and migrate to the top of the shearing flow, while smaller clasts fall between interstices (kinetic sieving), producing reverse grading.
  • Q4. Which of the following sedimentary structures is NOT typical of the base of turbidite sandstones?
    (a) Flute cast     (b) Groove cast     (c) Desiccation crack     (d) Prod mark
    → Correct Answer: (c) Desiccation crack
    Explanation: Desiccation cracks (mud cracks) are subaerial exposure structures formed in tidal flats or playa lakes. Turbidites are deep subaqueous marine/lacustrine gravity flows.
  • Q5. How do contourites differ fundamentally from turbidites in deep-sea basins?
    (a) Contourites flow downslope; turbidites flow along-slope
    (b) Contourites flow parallel to bathymetric contours; turbidites flow downslope
    (c) Contourites always display the complete Bouma sequence
    (d) Contourites are unsorted debris deposits
    → Correct Answer: (b)
    Explanation: Contour currents are geostrophic ocean bottom currents flowing parallel to bathymetric contours, whereas turbidity currents are catastrophic density flows moving downslope perpendicular to contours.
  • Q6. The Tb division of the Bouma sequence is characterized by:
    (a) Convolute bedding
    (b) Plane-parallel laminations formed under upper flow regime
    (c) Climbing ripples
    (d) Dish structures
    → Correct Answer: (b)
    Explanation: Tb is the lower plane-laminated division, formed by traction transport in the upper flow regime plane-bed phase.
  • Q7. Dish structures and fluid escape pipes are most characteristic of which sediment gravity flow deposit?
    (a) Turbidite     (b) Fluidized / Liquefied flow     (c) Grain flow     (d) Mud flow
    → Correct Answer: (b) Fluidized / Liquefied flow
    Explanation: As pore fluid escapes upward through loose, settling sand, it drags clay and fine silt upward, creating curved, subhorizontal concave-upward "dish" laminae and vertical escape pillars.
  • Q8. The primary mechanism supporting sediment in a turbidity current is:
    (a) Upward escape of pore water     (b) Fluid turbulence     (c) Dispersive pressure     (d) Matrix cohesion
    → Correct Answer: (b) Fluid turbulence
    Explanation: In turbidity currents, sediment grains are maintained in suspension by upward turbulent eddies within the moving fluid.

Sedimentology & Stratigraphy / Question 2

DGMS 2019 · Carbonate Sedimentology · Chapter Masterclass

Carbonate Diagenesis, Cement Fabrics & Syntaxial Overgrowths

Comprehensive Textbook Study Guide & Complete Solution for DGMS 2019 Exam (Question 2)
Standard Reference: Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Edition, 2006)
PART I
Theoretical Foundation & Direct Textbook Analysis
Carbonate diagenetic environments, cement morphologies, optical continuity, and crystallographic overgrowth mechanisms
1. Carbonate Diagenetic Environments & Cement Precipitation

Carbonate sediments are extraordinarily susceptible to post-depositional physical and chemical alteration (diagenesis) because calcium carbonate minerals (aragonite, high-Mg calcite, low-Mg calcite) are chemically reactive and metastable in natural waters (Boggs, 2006, Chapter 6).

"Carbonate diagenesis takes place in three major realms: (1) the marine environment, (2) the meteoric environment (subdivided into the vadose unsaturated zone and the phreatic saturated zone), and (3) the deep-burial environment. Cementation is the primary process responsible for the lithification of loose carbonate grains into rigid limestone. The mineralogy and morphology of carbonate cements are diagnostic of the chemistry, saturation state, and hydrologic flow of pore waters." — Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Ed., 2006), pp. 204–207

The morphology of carbonate cement crystals ranges widely:

  • Acicular / Aragonite Needles & Fibrous Cements: Common in the marine phreatic zone (high Mg/Ca ratio favors aragonite and high-Mg calcite).
  • Meniscus & Pendant (Gravitational) Cements: Confined to the meteoric vadose zone, where capillary forces draw pore water into contact points between grains (meniscus) or beneath grains (stalactitic droplets).
  • Equant / Blocky Calcite Cements: Interlocking crystals of equal dimensions, characteristic of freshwater meteoric phreatic and deep burial realms.
  • Syntaxial Rim Overgrowths: Single-crystal cement jackets precipitated in crystallographic and optical continuity around monocrystalline host grains.
Figure 6.15 (Boggs, Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy, 2006, p. 208): Morphology and distribution of carbonate cement types. Note the distinctive Syntaxial rim cement surrounding a monocrystalline echinoderm grain, alongside meniscus, pendant, isopachous, and blocky mosaic fabrics.
2. The Mechanism of Syntaxial Overgrowth & Optical Continuity

The word syntaxial derives from the Greek syn (together) and taxis (order/arrangement). In sedimentary petrography, it defines cement growth that shares the exact crystal lattice orientation of the substrate.

"In the water-saturated phreatic zone, cements are isopachous, blocky, or syntaxial rim cements. Syntaxial rims form by precipitation of optically continuous calcite cement on single-crystal echinoderm fragments. Because syntaxial overgrowths are optically continuous with the original crystal, the host grain and the cement extinguish simultaneously under crossed polars on the petrographic microscope." — Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Ed., 2006), pp. 141, 208

Echinoderm plates (crinoid ossicles, echinoid spines) are unique in paleontology because each individual skeletal element behaves as a single giant crystal of high-Mg calcite. When pore waters become supersaturated with respect to low-Mg calcite:

  • The echinoderm surface acts as an ideal, energetically favored seed template (epitactic nucleation site).
  • Calcite precipitates without needing to nucleate new crystallographic centers, growing outward as an optical continuation of the host lattice.
  • Under a petrographic microscope between crossed polars, when the stage is rotated, both the core echinoderm fragment and the surrounding syntaxial cement extinguish at the exact same angle.
  • The boundary between the original grain and the syntaxial overgrowth is typically demarcated only by a thin "dust line" of micro-inclusions or iron oxide coating.
3. Comparative Terminology: Syntaxial, Antitaxial, Blocky & Rim

Competitive examinations frequently test precise terminology differentiating overgrowth types:

  • Syntaxial Overgrowth: Mineral growth where cement continues the lattice and optical orientation of the substrate (crystal axis [c-axis] is identical). Diagnostic of echinoderm-bearing limestones (encrinites) and syntaxial quartz overgrowths on detrital quartz grains in quartz arenites.
  • Antitaxial Growth: A term strictly from structural geology and vein mechanics (Durney & Ramsay, 1973). Antitaxial veins are fibrous veins where fibrous mineral crystals grow outward from a central median suture line towards the vein walls (crystals grow at the vein wall-rock contact). It does not describe carbonate grain cementation.
  • Blocky / Equant Cement: A mosaic of randomly oriented calcite crystals that have approximately equal dimensions and vary randomly in their optical extinction angles.
  • Rim Cement (Generic): An isopachous or microcrystalline crust coating a grain. While syntaxial overgrowth forms a "rim", calling it merely "rim overgrowth" fails to specify the critical defining condition: optical continuity with the single crystal substrate.
PART II
DGMS 2019 Examination: Question & Worked Solution
Rigorous option-by-option critical analysis and definitive textbook-grounded answer
❓ Original Question — DGMS 2019 (Junior Scientific Officer)

Q.2. In carbonate diagenesis, if calcite cement is overgrown on a single calcite crystal in same optical orientation, it is referred to as

(a)   Rim overgrowth
(b)   Antitaxial overgrowth
(c)   Blocky overgrowth
(d)   Syntaxial overgrowth
✔
Correct Answer: (d) Syntaxial overgrowth
When cement precipitates around a monocrystalline grain (such as an echinoderm fragment or quartz grain) in identical crystallographic and optical orientation, it is defined scientifically as a syntaxial overgrowth (or syntaxial rim cement).
🔍 Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
✗  (a) Rim overgrowth INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: "Rim overgrowth" or "isopachous rim" is a generic descriptive term denoting any cement layer that coats a grain boundary. A rim can be composed of randomly oriented microcrystalline calcite, isopachous fibrous aragonite needles, or tangential coatings, none of which share the optical orientation of the host grain. It misses the specific crystallographic relationship defined in the question.

✗  (b) Antitaxial overgrowth INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: "Antitaxial" is a structural geology term describing fibrous vein-fill dynamics where fibers grow outward from a median line towards the wall rock (Durney & Ramsay, 1973). In diagenetic sedimentology, cement growing outward from a detrital grain into pore space is syntaxial, never antitaxial.

✗  (c) Blocky overgrowth INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: Blocky cement (equant spar) consists of equant, polygonal calcite crystals that nucleate randomly in pore spaces without crystallographic alignment to the host grain. Each crystal in a blocky mosaic has its own independent optical orientation.

✔  (d) Syntaxial overgrowth CORRECT

Why it is correct: As defined verbatim in Boggs (2006, pp. 141, 208), precipitation of mineral cement on a single-crystal substrate (e.g., echinoderm ossicle or quartz grain) where the new cement adopts the exact same optical and crystallographic orientation as the host crystal is termed syntaxial overgrowth. Both host and cement extinguish simultaneously under crossed polars.

PART III
Synthesis Reference Tables & High-Yield Practice Questions
Quick-reference diagnostic tables and 8 curated exam questions for DGMS, UPSC GSI, and IIT-JAM
📊 Synthesis Tables: Carbonate Cements & Overgrowth Fabrics

Table 1: Carbonate Cement Fabrics and Diagenetic Environments

Cement Morphology Mineralogy Diagenetic Environment Diagnostic Feature
Syntaxial Rim Overgrowth Low-Mg Calcite Meteoric Phreatic & Burial Optical continuity with single-crystal host (echinoderm)
Isopachous Fibrous Rim Aragonite / High-Mg Calcite Marine Phreatic Uniform isopachous coating of needles perpendicular to grain
Meniscus Cement Low-Mg Calcite Meteoric Vadose Blunt, curved meniscus bridges at grain contacts
Pendant (Microcodium/Gravitational) Low-Mg Calcite Meteoric Vadose Thickens on undersides of grains due to hanging droplets
Blocky / Equant Mosaic Low-Mg Calcite Meteoric Phreatic & Burial Equant polygonal crystals increasing in size towards pore center
Poikilotopic Spar Calcite / Ferroan Calcite Deep Burial Very large calcite crystals enclosing multiple detrital grains

Table 2: Comparison of Growth Terms in Geology

Term Discipline Growth Substrate & Direction Optical Property
Syntaxial Sedimentology / Petrology Grows outward from host grain into open pore space Identical optical extinction to host grain
Antitaxial Structural Geology Vein fibers grow outward from median line to wall rock Independent fiber optical axes
Epitaxial Mineralogy / Materials Overgrowth of mineral A onto mineral B with lattice match Distinct optical properties from substrate
🎯 Probable High-Yield Exam Questions (UPSC GSI, DGMS, IIT-JAM & GATE)
  • Q1. Syntaxial calcite overgrowths in limestones are most commonly developed around:
    (a) Ooids     (b) Echinoderm fragments     (c) Peloids     (d) Intraclasts
    → Correct Answer: (b) Echinoderm fragments
    Explanation: Each echinoderm skeletal element (crinoid plate, echinoid spine) is a single, optically uniform crystal of calcite, serving as an ideal template for syntaxial rim precipitation.
  • Q2. Under crossed polars, a syntaxial overgrowth and its host grain:
    (a) Show 90° difference in extinction angles
    (b) Extinguish simultaneously at the exact same orientation
    (c) Display undulose versus uniform extinction
    (d) Never extinguish
    → Correct Answer: (b) Extinguish simultaneously at the exact same orientation
    Explanation: Optical continuity means the crystallographic c-axes of both host and cement are parallel, resulting in simultaneous extinction.
  • Q3. Meniscus and pendant cement fabrics in carbonate rocks are diagnostic indicators of:
    (a) Deep marine phreatic environment
    (b) Meteoric vadose environment
    (c) Hydrothermal burial realm
    (d) Anoxic deep marine basin
    → Correct Answer: (b) Meteoric vadose environment
    Explanation: Vadose environments contain both air and water in pores; surface tension holds water in menisci at grain contacts or hanging beneath grains, depositing meniscus and pendant cements.
  • Q4. Syntaxial overgrowths in sandstones are most prominently exhibited by which mineral?
    (a) Plagioclase     (b) Quartz     (c) Biotite     (d) Tourmaline
    → Correct Answer: (b) Quartz
    Explanation: In quartz arenites, silica cement precipitates around detrital quartz grains in optical continuity, forming syntaxial quartz overgrowths with euhedral crystal faces.
  • Q5. The "dust line" frequently visible between a host grain and its syntaxial rim represents:
    (a) A zone of high strain
    (b) Impurities, iron oxides, or micro-inclusions on the original grain surface
    (c) Chemical replacement by dolomite
    (d) Fracturing during compaction
    → Correct Answer: (b)
    Explanation: Prior to cementation, the detrital grain surface accumulates micro-coatings of clay, iron oxides, or fluid inclusions, which remain preserved as a dark line separating the host from the new cement.
  • Q6. Isopachous fibrous aragonite needle cement is typical of:
    (a) Subaerial caliche crusts
    (b) Warm, shallow marine phreatic environments
    (c) Deep burial diagenesis
    (d) Meteoric phreatic lenses
    → Correct Answer: (b) Warm, shallow marine phreatic environments
    Explanation: Marine waters have high Mg/Ca ratios that inhibit calcite growth and promote precipitation of needle-like aragonite or high-Mg calcite as uniform rims around grains.
  • Q7. In structural geology, fibrous veins that grow from a central median suture outwards towards the wall rocks are termed:
    (a) Syntaxial     (b) Antitaxial     (c) Composite     (d) Ataxial
    → Correct Answer: (b) Antitaxial
    Explanation: Antitaxial veins grow at the vein/wall-rock interface with the oldest crystal material in the center, contrasting with syntaxial veins where crystals grow inward toward the vein center.
  • Q8. A limestone dominated by crinoid fragments cemented by syntaxial rim spar is termed:
    (a) Micrite     (b) Encrinite     (c) Oolite     (d) Travertine
    → Correct Answer: (b) Encrinite
    Explanation: Encrinites are crinoidal packstones or grainstones where vast volumes of syntaxial calcite cement interlock around abundant crinoidal debris.

Sedimentology & Stratigraphy / Question 3
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · Carbonate Sedimentology · Chapter Masterclass

Dunham Carbonate Classification: Depositional Fabrics & Grainstones

Comprehensive Textbook Study Guide & Complete Solution for DGMS 2019 Exam (Question 3)
Standard Reference: Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Edition, 2006)
PART I
Theoretical Foundation & Direct Textbook Analysis
Depositional textures, mud- vs. grain-support criteria, matrix winnowing, and the Embry & Klovan reef expansion
1. The Philosophy of Carbonate Classification: Texture vs. Composition

In carbonate petrology, two seminal classification schemes predominate: Robert L. Folk's (1959, 1962) compositional classification (based on allochems and matrix type: micrite vs. sparite) and Robert J. Dunham's (1962) textural classification.

"Dunham (1962) devised a classification of limestones based on the depositional texture of the rocks rather than the identity of different kinds of carbonate grains. Dunham's classification is based on whether the components of the rock were bound together during deposition (boundstone) or were not bound together. Rocks whose components were not bound together are divided on the basis of whether the rock is matrix-supported (mudstone, wackestone) or grain-supported (packstone, grainstone). The presence or absence of lime mud (< 20 μm) serves as the primary indicator of depositional energy." — Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy (4th Ed., 2006), p. 188

Dunham's system is universally favored for hand-specimen, core, and thin-section description because it directly translates into hydrodynamic energy conditions at the time of deposition:

  • Presence of Lime Mud (Micrite): Indicates calm, low-energy waters (lagoons, protected bays, deep marine below wave base) where fine carbonate mud can settle from suspension.
  • Absence of Lime Mud (Washed Clean): Indicates turbulent, high-energy settings (shoals, barrier bars, beaches, tidal passes) where waves and currents vigorously winnow away the fine mud, leaving only a grain-supported framework that is later cemented by sparry calcite cement.
2. Dunham's (1962) Core Classes & Distinctions

Dunham established four primary classes for non-bound carbonate rocks based on the ratio of mud to grains and framework support:

  • Mudstone: Mud-supported fabric containing less than 10% grains (>90% lime mud matrix). Reflects ultra-quiet, stagnant or sheltered water.
  • Wackestone: Mud-supported fabric containing more than 10% grains, but grains do not touch to support one another; they "float" in a matrix of lime mud.
  • Packstone: Grain-supported framework containing lime mud matrix in the interstitial spaces between grains. Represents moderate energy or rapid dumping where mud was not completely winnowed.
  • Grainstone: Grain-supported framework that contains almost no lime mud matrix (<1% mud). Interstitial voids are completely free of mud and are filled by sparry calcite cement. Reflects persistent, high-energy wave or current winnowing.
  • Boundstone: Carbonate components were bound together organically during deposition by colonial organisms (corals, stromatoporoids, encrusting algae, bryozoans).
Table 6.3 (Boggs, Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy, 2006, p. 189): Table 6.3A: The original Dunham (1962) classification of carbonate rocks according to depositional texture. Notice the explicit definition of Grainstone: "Grain-supported, Lacks mud". Table 6.3B: The Embry and Klovan (1971) modification expanding boundstones and coarse (>2 mm) carbonate gravels.
3. The Embry & Klovan (1971) Reef & Megagrain Expansion

Embry and Klovan (1971) expanded Dunham's system to better classify organic reef rocks and coarse bioclastic carbonates containing particles larger than 2 mm (Boggs, 2006, pp. 188–189):

  • Autochthonous Reef Rocks (Bound in Life):
    • Framestone: Supported by a rigid organic framework (e.g., massive branching corals, stromatoporoids).
    • Bindstone: Supported by tabular or encrusting organisms that bind and laminate sediment (e.g., microbial mats, encrusting red algae).
    • Bafflestone: Supported by stalk-like organisms that baffle current flow and trap sediment (e.g., upright bryozoans, sea grasses).
  • Allochthonous Coarse Carbonates (>10% grains > 2 mm):
    • Floatstone: Matrix-supported carbonate gravel (grains > 2 mm float in a fine matrix).
    • Rudstone: Grain-supported carbonate gravel (grains > 2 mm form the self-supporting framework).
PART II
DGMS 2019 Examination: Question & Worked Solution
Rigorous option-by-option critical analysis and definitive textbook-grounded answer
❓ Original Question — DGMS 2019 (Junior Scientific Officer)

Q.3. A carbonate rock that contains almost no mud and shows grain-supported framework is named as

(a)   Grainstone
(b)   Packstone
(c)   Floatstone
(d)   Bindstone
✔
Correct Answer: (a) Grainstone
By Dunham's (1962) universal classification, a carbonate rock possessing a self-supporting grain framework and devoid of lime mud (matrix-free) is definitively named a Grainstone.
🔍 Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
✔  (a) Grainstone CORRECT

Why it is correct: As defined in Table 6.3A of Boggs (2006, p. 189) and Dunham (1962), a grainstone is a grain-supported carbonate rock that "lacks mud" (contains almost no micrite). The intergranular pore spaces are typically filled by clear, sparry calcite cement that precipitated during diagenesis after deposition.

✗  (b) Packstone INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: While a packstone is grain-supported, it is defined by the presence of lime mud in its interstitial pore spaces ("grain-supported, contains mud"; Boggs, p. 189). Because the question explicitly specifies "contains almost no mud", packstone is eliminated.

✗  (c) Floatstone INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: In the Embry & Klovan (1971) classification, a floatstone is a coarse carbonate rock containing >10% grains larger than 2 mm, but it is matrix-supported (the large clasts float in a finer matrix). It is neither mud-free nor grain-supported.

✗  (d) Bindstone INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: A bindstone is an autochthonous reef carbonate where organisms (such as tabular encrusting red algae or cyanobacterial mats) bound the sediment together during life. The question describes an un-bound clastic depositional framework.

PART III
Synthesis Reference Tables & High-Yield Practice Questions
Quick-reference diagnostic tables and 8 curated exam questions for DGMS, UPSC GSI, and IIT-JAM
📊 Synthesis Tables: Dunham & Embry-Klovan Framework

Table 1: Dunham (1962) Classification of Carbonates

Class Support Fabric Lime Mud Content Depositional Energy Typical Environment
Mudstone Mud-supported > 90% mud (< 10% grains) Very Low Protected lagoons, deep basinal marine
Wackestone Mud-supported < 90% mud (> 10% grains) Low to Moderate Shallow shelf lagoon, quiet embayment
Packstone Grain-supported Contains mud matrix Moderate Sub-wave-base flank, tempestite dump
Grainstone Grain-supported Lacks mud (< 1%) High (winnowed) Ooid shoals, tidal inlets, beach barrier
Boundstone Organically bound in life Variable High to Moderate Biogenic reef core, organic framework

Dunham Class Primary Characteristic Equivalent Folk (1959) Term (Ooid example) Equivalent Folk (1959) Term (Bioclast example)
Grainstone Grain-supported, spar cement, mud-free Oosparite Biosparite
Packstone Grain-supported, micrite matrix present Packed Oomicrite Packed Biomicrite
Wackestone Mud-supported, > 10% grains Sparse Oomicrite Sparse Biomicrite

🎯 Probable High-Yield Exam Questions (UPSC GSI, DGMS, IIT-JAM & GATE)
  • Q1. A carbonate rock consisting of 60% ooids and 40% sparry calcite cement with no lime mud is classified in Folk's and Dunham's schemes respectively as:
    (a) Oomicrite / Packstone
    (b) Oosparite / Grainstone
    (c) Oosparite / Wackestone
    (d) Oomicrite / Grainstone
    → Correct Answer: (b) Oosparite / Grainstone
    Explanation: In Folk's system, ooids + spar = Oosparite. In Dunham's system, grain-supported + mud-free = Grainstone.
  • Q2. In the Embry & Klovan (1971) classification, a reef limestone supported by organisms that acted as sediment baffles during life is termed:
    (a) Bindstone     (b) Bafflestone     (c) Framestone     (d) Rudstone
    → Correct Answer: (b) Bafflestone
    Explanation: Bafflestones are formed by upright, stalked organisms (e.g., bryozoans, crinoids, sea grasses) that trap and baffle current-borne sediment.
  • Q3. A limestone consisting of 30% fossil shells floating in a lime mud matrix is named in Dunham's classification as:
    (a) Mudstone     (b) Wackestone     (c) Packstone     (d) Grainstone
    → Correct Answer: (b) Wackestone
    Explanation: A wackestone is mud-supported and contains >10% grains (here 30%). If it contained <10% grains, it would be a mudstone.
  • Q4. How does a Rudstone differ from a Floatstone in the Embry & Klovan scheme?
    (a) Rudstone is matrix-supported; Floatstone is grain-supported
    (b) Rudstone is grain-supported; Floatstone is matrix-supported
    (c) Rudstone contains < 2 mm grains; Floatstone contains > 2 mm grains
    (d) Rudstone is organically bound; Floatstone is unbound
    → Correct Answer: (b) Rudstone is grain-supported; Floatstone is matrix-supported
    Explanation: Both rocks have >10% gravel-size (>2 mm) carbonate particles, but Rudstones have a grain-supported framework, whereas Floatstones are matrix-supported.
  • Q5. The boundary grain size separating lime mud (micrite) from sand-sized carbonate allochems in Dunham's classification is:
    (a) 2 μm     (b) 20 μm     (c) 63 μm     (d) 2 mm
    → Correct Answer: (b) 20 μm
    Explanation: Dunham (1962) defined lime mud as carbonate particles finer than 20 μm, encompassing micrite (<4 μm) and microspar (4–20 μm).
  • Q6. The presence of a Grainstone facies in a stratigraphic succession indicates:
    (a) Deep marine euxinic basin
    (b) High-energy agitated shallow water shoal or beach
    (c) Stagnant restricted coastal lagoon
    (d) Glaciomarine dropstone setting
    → Correct Answer: (b) High-energy agitated shallow water shoal or beach
    Explanation: Grainstones require strong, persistent wave or tidal currents to continuously wash and winnow away fine lime mud.
  • Q7. An encrusting stromatoporoid or red algal framework that binds sediment is categorized as a:
    (a) Bafflestone     (b) Bindstone     (c) Floatstone     (d) Grainstone
    → Correct Answer: (b) Bindstone
    Explanation: Organisms that encrust and bind sheet-like organic crusts form bindstones.
  • Q8. Packstone differs from Grainstone primarily in:
    (a) Grain size of the allochems
    (b) Presence versus absence of lime mud in the matrix
    (c) Mineralogy (calcite vs aragonite)
    (d) Degree of dolomitization
    → Correct Answer: (b) Presence versus absence of lime mud in the matrix
    Explanation: Both are grain-supported; packstones contain lime mud matrix, whereas grainstones are completely winnowed clean of mud.

Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · METAMORPHIC PETROLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Barrovian Metamorphic Zones, Isograd Concepts & Pelitic Facies

Authoritative Reference: John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapters 21 & 28)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Metamorphism of Pelitic Sediments
Barrow's Classical Zones, The Physical Concept of Isograds, AKF/AFM Topologies & Devolatilization Reactions
1. Historical Foundations: George Barrow & The Scottish Highlands

The systematic zoning of regional metamorphic terranes originated with the pioneering field work of George Barrow (1893, 1912) in the Dalradian Supergroup of the Grampian Highlands, Scotland. Barrow mapped a sequence of systematic mineralogical transformations occurring within metamorphosed pelitic rocks (shales, mudstones, and siltstones) progressing outward from unmetamorphosed or low-grade sediments toward deeper, hotter orogenic infrastructure.

"In a pioneering study of regional metamorphism, George Barrow (1893, 1912) made one of the first and most lasting contributions to our understanding of the progressive nature of metamorphism... Barrow noted significant and systematic mineralogical changes in the pelitic rocks as he progressed from the coastal slates to the more deeply eroded interior of the metamorphic belt. He recognized six progressive mineral zones, each based on the first appearance of a distinctive new index mineral." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 21, p. 486)

The classical sequence of index minerals recognized by Barrow defines what is now universally termed the Barrovian metamorphic series (representing typical medium P/T regional orogenic metamorphism):

  1. Chlorite Zone: Very low to low grade; rocks are slates and phyllites. Typical assemblage: Chlorite + Muscovite + Quartz + Albite.
  2. Biotite Zone: Low grade (greenschist facies); phyllites and fine schists. Typical assemblage: Biotite + Chlorite + Muscovite (phengitic) + Quartz + Albite.
  3. Garnet Zone: Low-medium grade (upper greenschist to epidote-amphibolite); schists characterized by almandine-rich garnet. Assemblage: Garnet + Biotite + Muscovite + Chlorite + Quartz + Plagioclase.
  4. Staurolite Zone: Medium grade (lower amphibolite facies); schists marked by the appearance of staurolite.
  5. Kyanite Zone: Upper-medium grade (middle amphibolite facies); schists bearing kyanite, indicating substantial lithostatic overburden (medium to high pressure, typical of crustal thickening).
  6. Sillimanite Zone: High grade (upper amphibolite to granulite facies); rocks contain fibrous sillimanite (fibrolite) or prismatic sillimanite, coexisting with biotite, muscovite, quartz, and plagioclase (and at the highest grades, partial melting begins via muscovite breakdown).
2. The Physical Meaning of an Isograd & Modern Chemographic Analysis

In 1925, Cecil Edgar Tilley introduced the term isograd (from the Greek iso = equal, gradus = grade or step) to describe the mappable boundary that separates two metamorphic zones. In classical mapping:

  • An isograd represents the surface of first appearance of an index mineral in a rock of appropriate bulk composition (most notably, pelites).
  • Because temperature and pressure vary continuously across three-dimensional space, the mapped isograd line on a geological map represents the intersection of an isogradic surface with the Earth's topographic surface.
  • When crossing an isograd in the field (e.g., crossing the biotite isograd), one enters the corresponding zone (the biotite zone). The zone shares the name of its defining isograd.
"Because classic isograds are based on the first appearance of a mineral, rather than its complete stability range, the index mineral of a lower zone may still be stable in higher grade zones. Chlorite, for example, is still stable in the biotite zone (and even up into the staurolite zone). Thus, crossing an isograd does not necessarily signify that earlier minerals disappear, but rather that a new phase has become thermodynamically stable." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 21, p. 487)
3. Detailed Petrology of the Biotite Isograd & AKF/AFM Topology

In low-grade pelitic rocks (slates and phyllites of the chlorite zone), the primary sheet silicates are chlorite and phengitic muscovite (a white mica rich in the celadonite component, where Al in octahedral and tetrahedral sites is substituted by (Mg, Fe) + Si: AlVI + AlIV ↔ (Mg, Fe)VI + SiIV).

As temperature increases into the range of approximately 300°C to 400°C at crustal pressures of 2 to 6 kbar, the first brown biotite flakes nucleate in the rock. The specific reaction responsible for the appearance of biotite depends on the bulk potassium content and whether K-feldspar or phengite acts as the potassium reservoir:

Reaction 1: The Discontinuous Biotite-In Reaction (K-Feldspar Bearing Pelites)
In pelites containing detrital or diagenetic K-feldspar, biotite forms at the lowest temperatures by the univariant reaction in the KFASH/KMASH system: 3 Chlorite + 8 K-feldspar = 5 Biotite + 3 Muscovite + 9 Quartz + 4 H₂O

This reaction consumes K-feldspar completely in most normal aluminous pelites, explaining why K-feldspar is typically absent from intermediate-grade Barrovian pelites until the high-grade second-sillimanite isograd is reached.

Reaction 2: The Continuous Phengite Breakdown Reaction (K-Feldspar Free Pelites)
In common K-feldspar-free shales, the potassium is supplied by the phengite component of white mica. As temperature rises, the phengitic muscovite sheds its Fe-Mg component to react with chlorite: Chlorite + Phengitic Muscovite = Biotite + Muscovite (less phengitic) + Quartz + H₂O

Because this is a continuous multi-component reaction involving Fe-Mg-Al solid solutions, chlorite does not disappear abruptly at the isograd; instead, chlorite and biotite coexist across the entire width of the biotite zone!

Therefore, in the biotite zone of a Barrovian sequence, the classic equilibrium mineral assemblage is:

Biotite + Chlorite + Phengitic Muscovite + Quartz + Albite (± epidote)
II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 4
Question Statement
Q.4: In a Barrovian zone of metamorphism, presence of biotite + chlorite + phengitic muscovite + quartz + albite assemblage marks the:
(a) Albite zone
(b) Biotite zone
(c) Zeolite zone
(d) Chlorite zone
✓
Correct Answer: (b) Biotite zone
The first appearance of metamorphic biotite in equilibrium with chlorite, phengitic muscovite, quartz, and albite definitively marks the entry into the Barrovian Biotite Zone.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(b) Biotite zone CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In the classic Barrovian progressive metamorphic sequence established by Barrow (1893, 1912) and codified in petrology textbooks (Winter, 2010, p. 486; Yardley, 1989; Bucher & Grapes, 2011), the incoming of brown biotite in pelites defines the biotite isograd and the beginning of the biotite zone. Because the reaction forming biotite in common pelites involves the reaction of chlorite with the phengitic component of muscovite (or K-feldspar), chlorite does not disappear immediately but persists alongside newly formed biotite. Therefore, the diagnostic 5-phase equilibrium assemblage of the biotite zone is specifically biotite + chlorite + phengitic muscovite + quartz + albite.
(a) Albite zone INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: There is no such division as an "Albite zone" in the Barrovian metamorphic series. While albite is the stable sodic plagioclase feldspar throughout low-grade metamorphism (persisting across both the chlorite and biotite zones up to the peristerite solvus around 450°C in the oligoclase/garnet zone), it is an ubiquitous matrix phase rather than a defining index mineral. Using albite as an index mineral name violates standard IUGS metamorphic nomenclature.
(c) Zeolite zone INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: The Zeolite facies (or zeolite zone) represents very low-grade burial metamorphism and diagenesis (temperatures below ~200°C–250°C and pressures < 3 kbar), characterized by hydrated calcium-sodium aluminosilicates such as heulandite, analcime, and laumontite in volcaniclastic rocks, or smectite/illite in pelites. Biotite is completely unstable under zeolite facies conditions; true biotite requires thermal conditions above ~300°C to nucleate in pelitic compositions.
(d) Chlorite zone INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: In the Chlorite zone (the lowest-grade zone of the Barrovian sequence), the rock is composed exclusively of chlorite + muscovite (illite/phengite) + quartz + albite. By strict definition, the chlorite zone lies below the biotite isograd; biotite is completely absent. The moment the very first grains of biotite appear in the thin section or hand specimen, the rock has transitioned across the isograd into the biotite zone!
III
Synthesis Reference Tables, Diagnostic Criteria & High-Yield Practice
Complete Facies Equivalences, Field Recognition & 8 Exam-Grade Multiple-Choice Questions
Barrovian Metamorphic Sequence: Mineral Zones & Facies Equivalents
Barrovian Zone Defining Isograd Reaction (in Pelites) Diagnostic Equilibrium Assemblage Approx. T (°C) Metamorphic Facies
Chlorite Zone Smectite/Illite → Chl + Ms Chlorite + Muscovite + Albite + Quartz ± Epidote < 300°C Sub-greenschist to Lower Greenschist
Biotite Zone Chl + Phengite / Kfs → Bt + Ms + Qtz + H₂O Biotite + Chlorite + Muscovite + Quartz + Albite 300 – 420°C Middle Greenschist Facies
Garnet Zone Chl + Ms + Qtz → Grt (Alm) + Bt + H₂O Almandine + Biotite + Muscovite + Quartz + Oligoclase 420 – 520°C Upper Greenschist to Epidote-Amphibolite
Staurolite Zone Grt + Chl + Ms → St + Bt + Qtz + H₂O Staurolite + Biotite + Almandine + Muscovite + Quartz + Plagioclase 520 – 580°C Lower Amphibolite Facies
Kyanite Zone St + Chl + Ms → Ky + Bt + Qtz + H₂O Kyanite + Staurolite + Biotite + Almandine + Muscovite + Quartz + Plagioclase 580 – 650°C Middle Amphibolite Facies
Sillimanite Zone Ky → Sil (polymorphic) & Ms + Qtz → Sil + Kfs + Melt Sillimanite (fibrolite/prismatic) + Biotite + Garnet + Plagioclase + Quartz ± K-feldspar > 650°C Upper Amphibolite to Granulite Facies
Diagnostic Petrographic Criteria: Identifying the Biotite Zone
  • Microscopic Texture: Rocks of the biotite zone are typically fine-grained phyllites or very fine schists. Brown pleochroic biotite flakes (often sub-millimeter in size) lie along the continuous cleavage planes ($) defined by aligned muscovite and chlorite.
  • Coexistence of Chlorite and Biotite: Green chlorite flakes remain abundant and intimately intergrown with brown biotite, demonstrating chemical equilibrium across the divariant AFM field.
  • Phengite Substitution: Muscovite shows elevated Si:Al ratios (phengitic character), reflecting moderate-to-high pressure conditions during orogenic thickening.
  • Plagioclase Composition: Plagioclase remains strictly pure albite ($ to $). Calcification across the peristerite solvus into oligoclase occurs only at higher grades near the garnet isograd.
8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Pelitic Metamorphism & Barrovian Zones
1. In the Dalradian rocks of the Scottish Highlands, who first introduced the concept of an "isograd" to delineate metamorphic zones?
(a) George Barrow    (b) C. E. Tilley    (c) Pentti Eskola    (d) Arthur Holmes
Answer: (b) C. E. Tilley — While George Barrow (1893) first mapped the mineral zones, Cecil Edgar Tilley (1925) coined and defined the term "isograd" as a line of equal metamorphic grade based on the first appearance of an index mineral.
2. Which of the following is the correct ascending order of Barrovian metamorphic zones in pelitic rocks?
(a) Chlorite → Biotite → Staurolite → Garnet → Kyanite → Sillimanite
(b) Chlorite → Biotite → Garnet → Staurolite → Kyanite → Sillimanite
(c) Chlorite → Garnet → Biotite → Staurolite → Sillimanite → Kyanite
(d) Biotite → Chlorite → Garnet → Kyanite → Staurolite → Sillimanite
Answer: (b) — The standard Barrovian sequence is Chlorite → Biotite → Garnet → Staurolite → Kyanite → Sillimanite.
3. Why is K-feldspar typically absent in intermediate-grade Barrovian pelitic schists (biotite to kyanite zones)?
(a) Potassium is insoluble in metamorphic fluids
(b) K-feldspar is consumed at the biotite isograd by reaction with chlorite to form biotite + muscovite
(c) K-feldspar only forms in basaltic rocks
(d) K-feldspar transforms into albite at low temperatures
Answer: (b) — The reaction 3 Chl + 8 Kfs = 5 Bt + 3 Ms + 9 Qtz + 4 H₂O completely consumes all modal K-feldspar in normal pelites at the onset of the biotite zone.
4. The Buchan (or Abukuma) metamorphic series differs from the Barrovian series because it develops under:
(a) Higher pressure at similar temperature    (b) Lower pressure and higher geothermal gradient    (c) Ultra-high pressure subduction    (d) Anhydrous conditions
Answer: (b) Lower pressure and higher geothermal gradient — Buchan facies series is characterized by low P/T conditions where andalusite and cordierite develop instead of kyanite and almandine.
5. In pelitic rocks, the change in plagioclase composition from albite (<An₅) to oligoclase (An₁₅–An₂₅) across the peristerite solvus typically coincides with the:
(a) Chlorite isograd    (b) Biotite isograd    (c) Garnet isograd    (d) Second sillimanite isograd
Answer: (c) Garnet isograd — The jump across the peristerite solvus from albite to oligoclase occurs at the transition from upper greenschist to epidote-amphibolite/amphibolite facies, near the garnet isograd.
6. In the AFM projection (Thompson, 1957) for pelitic rocks, the phases from which the composition is projected are:
(a) Albite, Quartz, and CO₂    (b) Muscovite (or K-feldspar), Quartz, and H₂O    (c) Calcite, Dolomite, and Quartz    (d) Garnet, Biotite, and Staurolite
Answer: (b) Muscovite (or K-feldspar), Quartz, and H₂O — The AFM projection simplifies the 6-component KFMASH system into a 2D ternary diagram by projecting from Muscovite, Quartz, and H₂O (or K-feldspar at higher grades).
7. Phengitic muscovite in low-grade pelitic rocks is characterized by:
(a) Depletion in silicon and enrichment in sodium
(b) Substitution of (Mg, Fe) + Si for Al in both octahedral and tetrahedral sites: AlVI + AlIV ↔ (Mg,Fe)VI + SiIV
(c) Complete replacement of K by Ca
(d) High fluorine content
Answer: (b) — Phengite is a solid solution between muscovite and celadonite, enriched in Si (up to 3.5 per formula unit) and (Mg, Fe) at higher pressures and lower temperatures.
8. The "second sillimanite isograd" in high-grade pelitic rocks represents:
(a) The polymorphic transformation of andalusite to sillimanite
(b) The dehydration breakdown of muscovite: Muscovite + Quartz = Sillimanite + K-feldspar + H₂O (or melt)
(c) The breakdown of garnet to staurolite
(d) The first appearance of fibrolite from kyanite
Answer: (b) — The second sillimanite isograd marks the upper stability limit of muscovite in the presence of quartz, producing K-feldspar + sillimanite and initiating anatectic melting.
Metamorphic Petrology / Question 5

DGMS 2019 · METAMORPHIC PETROLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Granulite Facies Metamorphism, Two-Pyroxene Equilibria & Mafic Metabasites

Authoritative Reference: John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 25: Metamorphic Facies and Metamorphosed Mafic Rocks)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Metamorphic Facies & Mafic Rocks
The Eskola Facies System, Hornblende Dehydration Breakdown, ACF Chemographics & Two-Pyroxene Granulites
1. The Metamorphic Facies Concept & Mafic Protoliths

The concept of metamorphic facies was formulated by the Finnish petrologist Pentti Eskola (1915, 1920, 1939) to provide a rigorous, chemical-thermodynamic framework for correlating metamorphic rocks of widely differing bulk compositions. Eskola recognized that while pelites, carbonates, and basalts yield vastly different minerals under the same physical conditions, all rocks that equilibrated under identical temperature and pressure ranges belong to the same metamorphic facies.

"A metamorphic facies is a set of metamorphic mineral assemblages, repeatedly associated in time and space and showing a regular relationship between mineral composition and bulk chemical composition, such that they were formed under similar ranges of temperature and pressure... Because mafic rocks (basalts and gabbros) are chemically sensitive to P and T across almost the entire spectrum of crustal conditions, their mineral assemblages serve as the definitive standard for naming and defining metamorphic facies." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 25, pp. 568–571)
2. The Amphibolite-to-Granulite Transition: Hornblende Breakdown

In mafic protoliths (metabasites), the lower-grade amphibolite facies is characterized by the essential hydrous mineral assemblage hornblende + plagioclase (oligoclase–andesine) ± quartz ± garnet.

As temperature rises beyond approximately 700°C to 750°C in the deep continental crust, hydrous amphibole becomes thermodynamically unstable and undergoes prograde dehydration-devolatilization (and dehydration-melting if quartz and plagioclase are present). The breakdown of hornblende produces anhydrous pyroxenes:

Dehydration Breakdown of Hornblende (Low to Moderate Pressure Granulites)
At moderate crustal pressures (3–8 kbar), hornblende reacts with quartz to generate orthopyroxene, clinopyroxene, and calcic plagioclase: Hornblende + Quartz = Orthopyroxene + Clinopyroxene + Plagioclase + H₂O (or melt)

This reaction generates the classic two-pyroxene granulite (orthopyroxene + clinopyroxene + plagioclase), marking the definitive boundary of the granulite facies.

Dehydration Breakdown of Hornblende (High Pressure Granulites)
At higher lithostatic pressures (> 8–12 kbar in thickened orogenic roots), garnet joins the product assemblage: Hornblende + Plagioclase + Quartz = Clinopyroxene + Orthopyroxene + Garnet + H₂O (or melt)

High-pressure mafic granulites are thus characterized by the 5-phase anhydrous paragenesis: Clinopyroxene + Orthopyroxene + Plagioclase + Garnet + Quartz.

3. ACF Chemographic Topology of Granulite Facies Mafic Rocks

In Eskola's ACF diagram (where A = Al₂O₃ + Fe₂O₃ - (Na₂O + K₂O), C = CaO, F = FeO + MgO + MnO), projected from quartz and fluid, the granulite facies metabasites occupy the central three-phase and four-phase sub-triangles connecting Plagioclase (Anorthite), Diopside (Cpx), Enstatite-Ferrosilite (Opx), and Almandine-Pyrope (Grt).

Key petrological features of granulite facies metabasites:

  • Coexistence of Two Pyroxenes: The essential defining hallmark of granulite facies metabasites is the stable coexistence of orthopyroxene (hypersthene/enstatite) and clinopyroxene (diopside/augite) with plagioclase.
  • Anhydrous Character: Hydrous minerals (hornblende, biotite, epidote) are absent or present only as minor retrograde/relict phases or F/Cl-enriched accessory crystals.
  • Plagioclase Composition: Plagioclase is intermediate to calcic (andesine to labradolite/bytownite). At even higher pressures (transitioning to eclogite), plagioclase breaks down completely into omphacite + garnet + kyanite + quartz.
II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 5
Question Statement
Q.5: Which one of the following is a characteristic assemblage of granulite facies metamorphism of mafic protolith?
(a) Hornblende + Plagioclase + Quartz + Garnet
(b) Forsterite + Orthopyroxene + Clinopyroxene + Epidote
(c) Clinopyroxene + Orthopyroxene + Plagioclase + Garnet + Quartz
(d) Forsterite + Orthopyroxene + Clinopyroxene + Staurolite
✓
Correct Answer: (c) Clinopyroxene + Orthopyroxene + Plagioclase + Garnet + Quartz
The stable coexistence of two pyroxenes (Cpx + Opx) with plagioclase, garnet, and quartz represents the quintessential high-pressure/high-temperature anhydrous assemblage of mafic granulites.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(c) Clinopyroxene + Orthopyroxene + Plagioclase + Garnet + Quartz CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In metamorphic petrology (Winter, 2010, Table 25-1, p. 571; Yardley, 1989; Bucher & Grapes, 2011), the entry into the granulite facies in mafic rocks is universally defined by the dehydration of hornblende to produce orthopyroxene (hypersthene) in the presence of clinopyroxene and plagioclase. At medium to high pressures, almandine-pyrope garnet and quartz coexist stably with both pyroxenes. This anhydrous 5-phase assemblage (Cpx + Opx + Plag + Grt + Qtz) is the textbook definition of a mafic "two-pyroxene granulite."
(a) Hornblende + Plagioclase + Quartz + Garnet INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: This assemblage is the classic paragenesis of the Amphibolite Facies (specifically, garnet amphibolite). Hornblende is a hydrous amphibole whose upper thermal stability limit defines the boundary between amphibolite and granulite facies. When a mafic rock enters the granulite facies, hornblende breaks down completely by dehydration reactions to generate pyroxenes. Therefore, an assemblage dominated by hornblende cannot represent the granulite facies.
(b) Forsterite + Orthopyroxene + Clinopyroxene + Epidote INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: This option contains two severe petrological impossibilities for mafic granulites:
  1. Forsterite (Mg-olivine): Mafic rocks (basalts/gabbros) are silica-saturated to oversaturated; olivine cannot coexist in equilibrium with quartz-bearing mafic granulites. Forsterite is restricted to silica-undersaturated ultramafic rocks (peridotites) or siliceous dolomitic marbles.
  2. Epidote: Epidote is a hydrous Ca-Al sorosilicate characteristic of low-to-medium grades (greenschist and epidote-amphibolite facies, T < 500°C–550°C). It completely decomposes at temperatures hundreds of degrees below the onset of granulite facies conditions.
(d) Forsterite + Orthopyroxene + Clinopyroxene + Staurolite INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: This assemblage is a chemically contradictory mixture:
  1. Staurolite: Staurolite is strictly confined to highly aluminous, Fe-rich pelitic protoliths (metamorphosed shales) at amphibolite facies (520°C–650°C). It never occurs in mafic bulk compositions because basalts lack sufficient Al₂O₃ relative to Ca, Mg, and Fe.
  2. Forsterite + Staurolite incompatibility: Forsterite represents extreme silica-undersaturation and ultramafic compositions, whereas staurolite requires high Al and excess silica. They are chemically mutually exclusive in nature.

Zeolite Laumontite/Heulandite + Chlorite + Albite + Quartz None Albite T < 250°C, Low P
Prehnite-Pumpellyite Prehnite + Pumpellyite + Chlorite + Albite + Quartz None Albite 250 – 350°C, Low-Mod P
Greenschist Actinolite + Chlorite + Epidote + Albite + Quartz Actinolite Albite (<An₅) 350 – 500°C, Mod P

Amphibolite Hornblende + Plagioclase (An₁₅–An₅₀) + Quartz ± Garnet Hornblende Oligoclase–Andesine 550 – 750°C, Mod-High P
Granulite Clinopyroxene + Orthopyroxene + Plagioclase ± Garnet ± Quartz Two Pyroxenes (Opx + Cpx) Andesine–Bytownite > 700 – 900°C, Mod-High P
Blueschist Glaucophane + Lawsonite/Epidote + Jadeite + Quartz Glaucophane (Na-amphibole) Plagioclase Unstable (Jadeite + Qtz) 200 – 450°C, High P (>6–8 kbar)
Eclogite Omphacite + Pyrope-Almandine Garnet ± Kyanite ± Quartz Omphacite (Na-Ca pyroxene) Plagioclase Completely Absent > 500°C, Very High P (>12–15 kbar)
Diagnostic Petrographic Criteria: Identifying Mafic Granulites
  • Presence of Orthopyroxene: The definitive petrographic criterion separating granulite facies from amphibolite facies is the appearance of hypersthene/orthopyroxene (distinguished by faint pink-to-green pleochroism, parallel extinction, and low birefringence).
  • Two-Pyroxene Geothermometry: Granulite metabasites preserve equilibrium compositions of coexisting clino- and orthopyroxenes that record peak metamorphic temperatures between 750°C and 950°C.
  • Granoblastic Polygonal Texture: Mafic granulites typically display a granoblastic polygonal (or granoblastic mosaic) texture with straight grain boundaries meeting at ~120° triple junctions, indicating prolonged static annealing at high temperature.
  • Plagioclase Twinning: Plagioclase exhibits well-developed albite and pericline twinning, frequently showing antiperthitic exsolution lamellae of K-feldspar.
8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Granulite Facies & Mafic Metamorphism
1. The critical mineral whose first appearance in metabasites defines the onset of the granulite facies is:
(a) Almandine garnet    (b) Orthopyroxene (hypersthene)    (c) Diopside    (d) Sillimanite
Answer: (b) Orthopyroxene (hypersthene) — In mafic rocks, the transition from amphibolite to granulite facies is definitively marked by the dehydration of hornblende to produce orthopyroxene in the presence of clinopyroxene and plagioclase.
2. Which reaction represents the dehydration breakdown of hornblende at the amphibolite-to-granulite facies boundary?
(a) Hornblende + Quartz = Orthopyroxene + Clinopyroxene + Plagioclase + H₂O
(b) Actinolite + Epidote = Hornblende + Albite + H₂O
(c) Glaucophane + Quartz = Jadeite + Talc + H₂O
(d) Tremolite + Calcite + Quartz = Diopside + CO₂
Answer: (a) — Reaction (a) is the fundamental devolatilization reaction responsible for the classic two-pyroxene granulite assemblage.
3. Mafic granulites are often called "two-pyroxene granulites" because they contain:
(a) Diopside and Augite
(b) Orthopyroxene (hypersthene/enstatite) and Clinopyroxene (augite/diopside)
(c) Omphacite and Aegirine
(d) Wollastonite and Hedenbergite
Answer: (b) — Two-pyroxene granulites are defined by the coexistence of an orthopyroxene (Ca-poor) and a clinopyroxene (Ca-rich).
4. Why is epidote completely absent from equilibrium granulite facies mafic rocks?
(a) Epidote only forms in granite pegmatites
(b) Epidote is a hydrous mineral that breaks down via dehydration at T > 550°C–600°C
(c) Epidote requires CO₂ to be stable
(d) Epidote is converted directly to chlorite
Answer: (b) — Epidote is thermally limited to greenschist and epidote-amphibolite facies; it dehydrates to form anorthite-rich plagioclase, grossular, and hornblende well before reaching granulite temperatures.
5. At ultra-high temperatures (UHT metamorphism, T > 900°C–1000°C) in pelitic granulites, which diagnostic anhydrous assemblage is recognized?
(a) Sapphirine + Quartz    (b) Chlorite + Muscovite    (c) Glaucophane + Jadeite    (d) Prehnite + Pumpellyite
Answer: (a) Sapphirine + Quartz — The coexistence of sapphirine + quartz or osumilite + quartz is the definitive indicator of ultra-high temperature (UHT) granulite metamorphism.
6. In mafic rocks, the transition from granulite facies to eclogite facies at high pressure is characterized by the disappearance of:
(a) Garnet    (b) Plagioclase    (c) Clinopyroxene    (d) Quartz
Answer: (b) Plagioclase — At high lithostatic pressures (> 12–15 kbar), plagioclase breaks down completely into jadeite/omphacite + grossular + kyanite + quartz, defining the eclogite facies.
7. Who first established the concept of metamorphic facies based on mineral assemblages in mafic rocks?
(a) George Barrow    (b) Pentti Eskola    (c) N. L. Bowen    (d) F. J. Turner
Answer: (b) Pentti Eskola — Eskola (1915, 1920) pioneered the metamorphic facies classification based on his study of Orijärvi and Oslo rocks.
8. What texture is most typical of mafic granulites that have undergone extensive static thermal annealing in the deep crust?
(a) Nematoblastic    (b) Slaty cleavage    (c) Granoblastic polygonal    (d) Poikilitic
Answer: (c) Granoblastic polygonal — High-temperature static recrystallization (annealing) drives grain-boundary migration to minimize interfacial surface energy, producing equant grains with ~120° dihedral angles.

DGMS 2019 · METAMORPHIC PETROLOGY & STRUCTURAL GEOLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Pressure Shadows, Strain Heterogeneity & Pressure Solution Microstructures

Authoritative References: John D. Winter (Ch 23: Structures & Textures) & Van der Pluijm & Marshak (Ch 9 & 11: Pressure Solution & Cleavage)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Metamorphic Textures & Stress Heterogeneity
Rigid Clasts in Ductile Matrices, Pressure Solution Transfer, Fiber Growth & Strain Shadows
1. Stress Shielding & Generation of Pressure Shadows Around Rigid Inclusions

When a deforming rock contains heterogeneous components—specifically rigid grains or porphyroblasts (such as pyrite, garnet, magnetite, or quartz pebbles) embedded within a more ductile, flow-prone matrix (such as calcite, phyllosilicates, or fine-grained quartz)—the local stress field is severely perturbed:

  • Stress Concentration: On grain boundaries perpendicular to the maximum compressive stress ($\sigma_1$), compressive stress reaches peak intensity. The ductile matrix impinges against the rigid barrier, creating zones of extreme mean stress.
  • Stress Shielding: Adjacent to the flanks of the rigid clast (in the extension direction $\sigma_3$, or parallel to foliation), the rigid crystal supports the compressive load, mechanically shielding the matrix behind it. This creates local zones of significantly reduced mean stress (low pressure).
"Pre-kinematic crystals, when clearly older than the deformation, commonly exhibit microstructures resulting from high localized strain... Pressure shadows (Figure 34c) occur when solution transfer dissolves a mineral (usually a matrix mineral) from high-stress areas and precipitates it in relatively low-stress areas adjacent to a porphyroblast. In addition, the external foliation usually wraps around a pre-kinematic porphyroblast, a result of flattening in the matrix." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 23, p. 527)
2. Pressure Solution Mass Transfer (Diffusive Mass Transfer)

The formation of pressure shadows is an integral part of the process of pressure solution (also called diffusive mass transfer or wet diffusion), which is one of the most prominent low-to-medium temperature deformation mechanisms in the crust (operating from ~150°C to 450°C):

  1. Dissolution: Soluble minerals (primarily quartz and calcite) have chemical potentials that increase with compressive stress. Under elevated normal stress along grain boundaries, quartz or calcite dissolves into thin intergranular fluid films: $$\mu = \mu_0 + \sigma_n \cdot V_m$$ where $\mu$ is the chemical potential, $\sigma_n$ is normal stress, and $V_m$ is molar volume.
  2. Transport: Dissolved chemical species diffuse through the fluid phase down the chemical potential gradient (away from high-stress surfaces toward regions of lower stress).
  3. Precipitation: Ions reach the low-stress pressure shadows adjacent to rigid clasts (or extensional tension gashes), where the fluid is supersaturated relative to the local low stress, precipitating new mineral grains or fibrous aggregates.
Fibrous Growth Fabrics in Pressure Shadows: Syntaxial vs. Antitaxial
Mineral fibers that precipitate in pressure shadows provide valuable kinematic records of the deformation history: Syntaxial Fringes: Fibers grow outward from the rigid porphyroblast; oldest material is at the core clast. Antitaxial Fringes: Fibers grow inward at the boundary with the porphyroblast; oldest material is at the outer tip.

Curved fibers in pressure shadows record progressive rotation of the incremental strain axes during non-coaxial (shear) deformation.

II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 6
Question Statement
Q.6: When flattening occurs in rocks that contain rigid grains, zones of relatively low pressure develop on the sides of the crystals that are normal to the maximum compressive stress. These zones are called:
(a) Solution cleavage
(b) Pressure shadows
(c) Pressure solution
(d) Stylolites
✓
Correct Answer: (b) Pressure shadows
These sheltered, low-stress zones that develop adjacent to rigid porphyroblasts during flattening are universally defined as pressure shadows (or strain shadows).
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(b) Pressure shadows CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In metamorphic petrology and structural geology (Winter, 2010, p. 527; Van der Pluijm & Marshak, 2004, pp. 236, 291; Ramsay & Huber, 1983; Passchier & Trouw, 2005), pressure shadows (also known as strain shadows or pressure fringes) are explicitly defined as the sheltered, low-mean-stress domains developed on the protected flanks of rigid crystals during tectonic flattening. Because the rigid clast bears the compressive load, the fluid in these protected areas has lower chemical potential, driving the precipitation of fibrous or granular quartz, calcite, or phyllosilicates.
(a) Solution cleavage INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: Solution cleavage (or pressure-solution cleavage / disjunctive cleavage) refers to a spaced foliation fabric formed by the dissolution and removal of soluble minerals, leaving behind planar seams concentrated in insoluble residues (clays, iron oxides, organic carbon). Solution cleavage is the planar dissolution fabric itself, not the localized low-pressure depositional domain adjacent to a rigid crystal.
(c) Pressure solution INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: Pressure solution is the physical deformation mechanism (the process of stress-induced dissolution, diffusion, and reprecipitation), whereas the question asks specifically for the name of the zones of relatively low pressure that develop adjacent to rigid grains. The process is pressure solution; the resulting low-pressure zone is the pressure shadow.
(d) Stylolites INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: Stylolites are jagged, serrated, tooth-like dissolution surfaces that form in regions of maximum compressive stress where extensive volume loss has occurred. Stylolites represent the exact physical opposite of pressure shadows: stylolites are the high-pressure dissolution source, whereas pressure shadows are the low-pressure precipitation sink!

Synthesis Reference Tables, Diagnostic Criteria & High-Yield Practice
Comparative Deformation Structures & 8 Exam-Grade Multiple-Choice Questions
Comparison of Pressure Solution Microstructures
Microstructure / Term Stress Regime Dominant Process Typical Mineralogy / Composition Diagnostic Field / Petrographic Form
Pressure Shadow (Strain Shadow) Low Mean Stress (Sheltered Flank) Precipitation of dissolved ions Fibrous or granular Quartz, Calcite, Chlorite, Mica Wings/fringes flanking rigid clasts (pyrite, garnet)
Stylolite Maximum Compressive Stress ($\sigma_1$) Stress-induced Dissolution (Volume Loss) Insoluble residue: Illite, Smectite, Hematite, Bitumen Serrated, tooth-like interpenetrating seams
Solution Cleavage Maximum Compressive Stress ($\sigma_1$) Pervasive Dissolution creating Spaced Fabric Alternating Qtz/Cal microlithons & phyllosilicate cleavage domains Parallel to anastomosing dark spaced cleavage seams
Extensional Vein (Tension Gash) Least Principal Stress ($\sigma_3$, Extension) Fracture opening and mineral precipitation Coarse fibrous or blocky Quartz, Calcite Lenticular, en-échelon, or planar vein arrays
Kinematic Significance of Pressure Shadows
  • Foliation Wrapping: Foliation planes ($S_1$) bend smoothly around the rigid porphyroblast and its flanking pressure shadows, creating an eye-shaped (augen) geometry.
  • Sense of Shear Determination: In ductile shear zones, asymmetric pressure shadows (such as $\sigma$-type and $\delta$-type mantle/tail geometries) provide unequivocal indicators of the rotational sense of shear (dextral vs. sinistral).
  • Deformation Timing: If pressure shadows contain unstrained fibers that truncate the matrix foliation, the fringe growth outlasted the main phase of foliation development (post-kinematic to syn-kinematic transition).
8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Pressure Shadows & Microstructures
1. In a deformed rock subjected to pure shear flattening, pressure shadows typically develop:
(a) On faces of rigid grains perpendicular to the maximum compressive stress ($\sigma_1$)
(b) Along the sides of rigid grains parallel to the extension direction ($\sigma_3$)
(c) Inside the rigid grain along cleavage fractures
(d) Exclusively along fault gouge seams
Answer: (b) — Pressure shadows develop in the low-pressure sheltered domains oriented parallel to the extension direction ($\sigma_3$) and perpendicular to $\sigma_1$.
2. Which mechanism is primarily responsible for mass transfer during the formation of pressure shadows at low-to-medium metamorphic grades?
(a) Dislocation glide    (b) Pressure solution diffusion    (c) Partial melting    (d) Cataclastic flow
Answer: (b) Pressure solution diffusion — Dissolution at high-stress grain boundaries and diffusion through intergranular pore fluid toward low-stress sheltered shadows.
3. In an "antitaxial" pressure fringe, where is the youngest mineral growth located?
(a) At the outer contact between the fringe and the rock matrix
(b) At the inner contact directly adjoining the rigid porphyroblast clast
(c) Uniformly distributed across the entire fringe
(d) At the center of the porphyroblast
Answer: (b) — In antitaxial growth, new fibers precipitate at the boundary between the rigid core and the fringe, pushing older fiber segments outward.
4. Stylolites differ fundamentally from pressure shadows because stylolites represent sites of:
(a) Maximum precipitation of quartz
(b) Maximum stress-induced dissolution and volume loss
(c) Magmatic crystallization
(d) Brittle tensile fracture opening
Answer: (b) — Stylolites are jagged dissolution seams formed at points of highest compressive stress where material dissolves, whereas pressure shadows are sites of mineral precipitation in low-stress domains.
5. What causes the curved or sigmoidal shape of mineral fibers commonly observed in pressure shadows?
(a) Rapid cooling of the metamorphic terrane
(b) Progressive rotation of the rigid porphyroblast and principal strain axes during non-coaxial shear
(c) Chemical reaction between quartz and feldspar
(d) Sudden change in metamorphic fluid salinity
Answer: (b) — As non-coaxial (rotational) deformation proceeds, the incremental extension direction rotates relative to the clast, causing newly grown fiber segments to precipitate at changing angles, producing curved fibers.
6. Which mineral commonly forms prominent, easily recognizable cubic porphyroblasts with fibrous quartz pressure shadows in slates?
(a) Halite    (b) Pyrite    (c) Gypsum    (d) Biotite
Answer: (b) Pyrite — Rigid pyrite cubes ($FeS_2$) embedded in fine-grained slate matrices are textbook examples that develop spectacular quartz/chlorite pressure fringes.
7. In shear sense indicators, a rigid clast flanked by wedge-shaped tails of recrystallized material that are stair-stepped across the clast without a central embayment is termed a:
(a) $\delta$-type clast    (b) $\sigma$-type clast    (c) Stylolitic nodule    (d) Mullion
Answer: (b) $\sigma$-type clast — Sigma ($\sigma$) porphyroclast systems have wedge-shaped tails that do not cross the reference median line, indicating non-coaxial shear.
8. Pressure solution creep becomes the dominant deformation mechanism under which typical crustal conditions?
(a) Completely dry conditions at > 900°C
(b) Low-to-moderate temperatures (150°C–400°C) in the presence of an intergranular fluid phase
(c) High-strain-rate impact shock events
(d) Temperatures below 0°C in permafrost
Answer: (b) — Pressure solution requires the presence of an intergranular aqueous fluid film and operates efficiently at low-to-moderate crustal temperatures (150°C–400°C).
Metamorphic Petrology / Question 7
