DGMS 2019 · METAMORPHIC PETROLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Classification & Genesis of Metamorphic Rocks: Blueschist, Pseudotachylite, Hornfels & Skarn

Authoritative References: John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapters 21, 22, 23 & 30)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Metamorphic Settings & Rock Nomenclature
Subduction High-P Metamorphism, Dynamic Fault Rocks & Frictional Melts, Thermal Aureoles & Metasomatic Skarns
1. The Spectrum of Metamorphic Environments

Metamorphic rocks are classified on the basis of their texture, mineralogy, protolith chemistry, and geological setting of origin (Winter, 2010, Chapters 21 & 22; IUGS Subcommission on the Systematics of Metamorphic Rocks - SCMR). Four critical classes represent extreme end-members of physical regimes:

  • Blueschist (Subduction Zone High-P/Low-T): Metabasic rocks metamorphosed under high lithostatic pressure and low temperature ($P > 6\text{–}10\text{ kbar}, T = 200^\circ\text{–}450^\circ\text{C}$) in subducting oceanic slabs. Diagnostic sodic amphibole: glaucophane, imparting a distinctive blue color and schistose fabric.
  • Pseudotachylite (Dynamic Frictional Melt in Faults): Glassy, vitreous, or crypto-crystalline material occurring as injection veins and fault-vein fault rocks, generated by extreme localized frictional heating during rapid seismic rupture ($>0.1\text{–}1\text{ m/s}$) along fault planes.
  • Hornfels (Contact / Thermal Metamorphism): Tough, fine-grained, non-schistose rocks formed in the thermal aureoles of igneous intrusions under low confining pressures. Characterized by substantial static recrystallization and polygonal/decussate textures without preferred mineral orientation.
  • Skarn (Hydrothermal Metasomatism): Coarse-grained, zoned calc-silicate rocks resulting from intense chemical mass transfer (metasomatism) between silicate magmas/hydrothermal fluids and carbonate host rocks (limestones or dolomites), richly packed with Ca-Fe-Mg-Mn silicates (garnet, pyroxene, epidote).
2. Authoritative Textbook Definitions (Winter, Ch 22, pp. 500–502)
Blueschist: "A blue-amphibole-bearing metamorphosed mafic rock. Glaucophane, the characteristic blue amphibole, indicates high pressures and low temperatures typical of subduction zones... Most blueschists are foliated (schistose) due to directed tectonic stress during subduction accretion." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., p. 501)
Pseudotachylite: "A dark, very fine-grained to glassy rock that typically occurs as thin veins or intrusive veinlets in fault zones. It is formed by frictional melting of the wall rock during rapid seismic slip events on faults. When the melt chills against the cold country rock, it forms a vitreous or cryptocrystalline matrix with rounded host clasts." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., p. 502)
Hornfels: "A type of granofels that is typically fine-grained, non-schistose, and occurs in contact metamorphic aureoles. Hornfelses are tough and splintery, exhibiting substantial recrystallization into equant grains without preferred orientation because deformation is absent during thermal baking." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., p. 500)
Skarn: "A calc-silicate rock resulting from contact metamorphism and metasomatism of carbonate rocks by hydrothermal fluids derived from an igneous intrusion... Characterized by Ca-Fe-Mg-Mn silicates (such as grossular-andradite garnet, diopside-hedenbergite, epidote, wollastonite) that develop pronounced compositional zones and bands." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., p. 501)
II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 7
Question Statement
Q.7: Match List-I with List-II:
List-I (Rocks)
A. Blueschist
B. Pseudotachylite
C. Hornfels
D. Skarn
List-II (Descriptions)
1. Ultrafine-grained vitreous looking material occurring as thin veins
2. Na-amphibole containing schistose rock
3. A non-schistose substantial recrystallization
4. A metasomatic Ca-Fe-Mg-Mn-silicate rock with compositional zones and bands
✓
Correct Match: A – 2,   B – 1,   C – 3,   D – 4
Blueschist is a Na-amphibole schist; Pseudotachylite is vitreous fault melt; Hornfels is non-schistose thermal recrystallization; Skarn is metasomatic Ca-Fe-Mg-Mn silicate rock.
Exhaustive Pairwise Critical Analysis
A → 2: Blueschist ⟷ Na-amphibole containing schistose rock

Why this match is conclusive: Blueschists are defined mineralogically by the predominance of the blue sodium amphibole glaucophane ($Na_2(Mg_3Al_2)Si_8O_{22}(OH)_2$) or crossite/riebeckite, formed by high-pressure, low-temperature metamorphism of basaltic oceanic crust in subduction zones. Because they undergo intense non-coaxial deformation within the subduction accretionary wedge, blueschists almost universally possess a pronounced tectonic foliation and are thus true schistose rocks.

B → 1: Pseudotachylite ⟷ Ultrafine-grained vitreous looking material occurring as thin veins

Why this match is conclusive: Pseudotachylites are fossil earthquakes: they represent rock melt generated by extreme frictional dissipation along fault surfaces during seismic rupture. The generated silicate melt is violently injected into surrounding fractures and rapidly quenched against cold host rock, solidifying into an ultrafine-grained, glassy (vitreous) or cryptocrystalline black material occurring as sharp, thin veins.

C → 3: Hornfels ⟷ A non-schistose substantial recrystallization

Why this match is conclusive: Hornfels is the classic product of thermal contact metamorphism in the static aureoles surrounding igneous plutons. Because differential stress and directed tectonic shearing are minimal or absent during thermal baking, the preexisting rock undergoes substantial static recrystallization to form an interlocking, equigranular polygonal or decussate mosaic that is entirely non-schistose (lacking foliation).

D → 4: Skarn ⟷ A metasomatic Ca-Fe-Mg-Mn-silicate rock with compositional zones and bands

Why this match is conclusive: Skarns (also known as tactites) are formed by intense metasomatic mass exchange driven by hydrothermal fluids infiltrating between a silicate magma (providing Si, Fe, Al) and carbonate rocks (providing Ca, Mg, CO₂). This reactive fluid flow produces a complex suite of Ca-Fe-Mg-Mn silicates (grossular-andradite, diopside-hedenbergite, wollastonite, epidote, bustamite) arranged in sharp, distinct compositional zones and reaction bands.

III

8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Metamorphic Rock Nomenclature
1. Glaucophane, the defining sodium amphibole in blueschists, belongs to which mineral group formula?
(a) Ca₂(Mg,Fe)₅Si₈O₂₂(OH)₂
(b) Na₂Mg₃Al₂Si₈O₂₂(OH)₂
(c) (Mg,Fe)₇Si₈O₂₂(OH)₂
(d) KAl₂Si₃AlO₁₀(OH)₂
Answer: (b) — Glaucophane is a sodic amphibole with the end-member formula Na₂Mg₃Al₂Si₈O₂₂(OH)₂.
2. Pseudotachylites are distinguished from mylonites primarily because pseudotachylites form by:
(a) Ductile crystal-plastic dislocation creep
(b) Frictional melting during rapid seismic slip
(c) Slow pressure solution mass transfer
(d) Static thermal annealing in an aureole
Answer: (b) Frictional melting during rapid seismic slip — Mylonites form by ductile plastic flow without melting, whereas pseudotachylites are generated by flash frictional melting during fast seismic slip events.
3. Contact metamorphic aureoles surrounding shallow granitic intrusions typically produce which non-foliated rock from pelitic shale protoliths?
(a) Mylonite    (b) Pelitic Hornfels    (c) Blueschist    (d) Gneiss
Answer: (b) Pelitic Hornfels — Shales baked under static conditions in thermal aureoles transform into dense, fine-grained, non-schistose hornfelses (spotted hornfels with cordierite/andalusite).
4. An "exoskarn" refers to a metasomatic skarn developed within:
(a) The intrusive igneous body itself
(b) The sedimentary carbonate host rock adjoining the pluton
(c) Ocean floor basaltic pillow lavas
(d) Mantle peridotite xenoliths
Answer: (b) — Exoskarn forms in the surrounding sedimentary carbonate country rock, whereas endoskarn forms by replacement within the igneous intrusion itself.
5. Under polarizing petrographic microscope, the glassy matrix of an unaltered pseudotachylite appears:
(a) Strongly pleochroic    (b) Completely isotropic (dark under crossed polars)    (c) Highly birefringent    (d) Lamellar twinned
Answer: (b) Completely isotropic (dark under crossed polars) — True volcanic and frictional glass lacks crystalline order and is optically isotropic.
6. Which diagnostic mineral pair in blueschists indicates the complete breakdown of plagioclase at high pressure?
(a) Albite + Anorthite    (b) Jadeite + Quartz    (c) Calcite + Quartz    (d) Chlorite + Biotite
Answer: (b) Jadeite + Quartz — Albite breaks down at high pressure via the univariant reaction: Albite = Jadeite + Quartz, a definitive high-pressure index equilibrium.
7. The decussate texture often observed in hornfelses is characterized by:
(a) Parallel aligned mica flakes defining continuous schistosity
(b) Randomly oriented, interlocking elongate or platy crystals lacking preferred orientation
(c) Alternating felsic and mafic compositional banding
(d) Rounded porphyroclasts with recrystallized tails
Answer: (b) — Decussate texture consists of interlocking prismatic or platy grains (e.g., micas, amphiboles) oriented completely at random due to absence of deviatoric stress.
8. Skarn deposits are world-class economic sources for which metals?
(a) Fe, Cu, W, Sn, Au, Zn, Pb    (b) Exclusively alkali metals like Li and Na    (c) Noble gases    (d) Halite and sylvite
Answer: (a) Fe, Cu, W, Sn, Au, Zn, Pb — Metasomatic skarns host major hydrothermal ore deposits of iron (magnetite skarn), copper, tungsten (scheelite skarn), gold, and lead-zinc.

DGMS 2019 · METAMORPHIC PETROLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Metamorphism of Siliceous Carbonates: The CMS System & Protolith Diagnosis

Authoritative Reference: John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 29: Metamorphism of Calcareous & Ultramafic Rocks)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Metamorphism of Carbonate Rocks
The CaO–MgO–SiO₂–H₂O–CO₂ (CMS-HC) System, Decarbonation Sequences & T-X(CO₂) Phase Equilibria
1. The Chemical System of Carbonate Metamorphism

Sedimentary carbonate rocks range from pure limestone ($\text{CaCO}_3$) and dolostone ($\text{CaMg(CO}_3)_2$) to siliceous dolomitic limestones and marls containing varying amounts of detrital quartz ($\text{SiO}_2$) and clay minerals (illite, smectite).

The phase equilibria of these rocks are modeled within the fundamental five-component system $\text{CaO} - \text{MgO} - \text{SiO}_2 - \text{H}_2\text{O} - \text{CO}_2$ (CMS-HC). When quartz is present, progressive metamorphism drives a series of dramatic decarbonation and mixed-volatile reactions, liberating gaseous $\text{CO}_2$ (and $\text{H}_2\text{O}$) and forming a sequence of calcium-magnesium silicates.

"Metamorphosed calcareous rocks are derived from carbonate-rich sedimentary precursors... Metamorphosed siliceous dolomites are particularly interesting because the reactions that occur during progressive metamorphism produce a sequence of Ca-Mg silicates that serve as sensitive indicators of metamorphic grade and fluid composition." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 29, p. 670)
2. The Progressive Mineral Sequence in Siliceous Dolomites

As temperature rises from greenschist through amphibolite to granulite facies, the initial sedimentary assemblage Calcite + Dolomite + Quartz undergoes sequential reactions as portrayed in Winter's $T-X_{\text{CO}_2}$ phase diagram:

Reaction 1: Appearance of Diopside (Medium-to-High Grade)
At amphibolite facies conditions, dolomite reacts with quartz (or earlier tremolite + calcite) to generate the calcic pyroxene diopside: Dolomite + 2 Quartz = Diopside + 2 CO₂ Tremolite + 3 Calcite + 2 Quartz = 5 Diopside + 3 CO₂ + H₂O
Reaction 2: Appearance of Forsterite (High Grade)
In quartz-depleted, dolomite-rich bulk compositions, diopside and dolomite react to form Mg-rich olivine (forsterite): Diopside + 3 Dolomite = 2 Forsterite + 4 Calcite + 2 CO₂

Notice that forsterite in marbles coexists stably with calcite, but is strictly isolated from free quartz by the intervening diopside tie-line!

Reaction 3: Appearance of Wollastonite (Highest Grade / Granulite Facies)
At peak temperatures and low $X_{\text{CO}_2}$ (or very high T in granulites), calcite and quartz directly react to produce the pyroxenoid wollastonite: Calcite + Quartz = Wollastonite + CO₂
Reaction 4: K-Feldspar from Illitic Clays
In impure dolomites containing minor detrital illite/potassium clay, phlogopite forms at lower grades and breaks down at granulite facies to generate K-feldspar: Phlogopite + 3 Calcite + 6 Quartz = K-feldspar + 3 Diopside + 3 CO₂ + H₂O

Therefore, an assemblage containing calcite + dolomite + quartz + diopside + wollastonite + K-feldspar + forsterite represents a classic, high-grade, partially equilibrated siliceous dolomitic limestone (carbonate rock protolith) undergoing granulite-facies metamorphism!

II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 8
Question Statement
Q.8: Presence of calcite, dolomite, quartz, diopside, wollastonite, K-feldspar, forsterite in a regionally metamorphosed rock, under granulite facies suggests which one of the following types of protoliths?
(a) Mafic
(b) Pelitic
(c) Carbonate rock
(d) Ultramafic
✓
Correct Answer: (c) Carbonate rock
The combination of carbonate minerals (calcite, dolomite) with calcium-magnesium silicates (diopside, wollastonite, forsterite) uniquely fingerprints an impure siliceous dolomitic limestone protolith.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(c) Carbonate rock CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: The mineral assemblage listed in the question is the textbook signature of a siliceous dolomitic limestone or marl (Winter, 2010, Chapter 29; Bucher & Grapes, 2011; Yardley, 1989). Primary sedimentary calcite ($\text{CaCO}_3$), dolomite ($\text{CaMg(CO}_3)_2$), and detrital quartz ($\text{SiO}_2$) react during progressive metamorphism to yield diopside ($\text{CaMgSi}_2\text{O}_6$), forsterite ($\text{Mg}_2\text{SiO}_4$), and wollastonite ($\text{CaSiO}_3$). Minor clay/shale impurities (illite) provide potassium and aluminum to generate K-feldspar via phlogopite breakdown. No other geological protolith possesses this combination of abundant Ca-Mg carbonates and calc-silicate phases.
(a) Mafic INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A mafic protolith (metabasite such as basalt or gabbro) under granulite facies conditions produces a "two-pyroxene granulite" dominated by orthopyroxene + clinopyroxene + calcic plagioclase ± garnet ± quartz (as examined in Question 5). Mafic rocks have low CO₂ contents; primary calcite and dolomite do not exist in equilibrium in peak mafic granulites, nor does wollastonite form because calcium is strongly partitioned into plagioclase and clinopyroxene.
(b) Pelitic INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: Pelitic protoliths (shales, mudstones) are characterized by very high Al₂O₃ and FeO/MgO, with very low CaO. Under granulite facies, metapelites form assemblages of sillimanite + garnet (almandine) + cordierite + biotite + K-feldspar + plagioclase + quartz. Pelites completely lack the calcium required to form calcite, dolomite, diopside, or wollastonite.
(d) Ultramafic INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: While ultramafic rocks (peridotites, serpentinites) contain abundant Mg-olivine (forsterite) and minor pyroxene, they are extremely depleted in CaO and SiO₂. They never contain free quartz in equilibrium with forsterite, nor do they develop wollastonite ($\text{CaSiO}_3$) or significant calcite/dolomite in regional granulite facies terranes.

8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Carbonate Metamorphism & Protolith Diagnosis
1. The reaction Calcite + Quartz = Wollastonite + CO₂ is an example of which type of metamorphic reaction?
(a) Continuous hydration    (b) Discontinuous decarbonation    (c) Polymorphic inversion    (d) Oxidation reaction
Answer: (b) Discontinuous decarbonation — It is a univariant devolatilization reaction releasing CO₂ gas.
2. Why does wollastonite form at much lower temperatures in the presence of an H₂O-rich metamorphic fluid than in pure CO₂?
(a) H₂O increases the activation energy
(b) Dilution of the fluid phase by H₂O lowers the activity of CO₂ ($a_{\text{CO}_2}$), driving decarbonation to lower temperatures
(c) H₂O transforms wollastonite into tremolite
(d) H₂O reacts with quartz to form coesite
Answer: (b) — Reducing the mole fraction of CO₂ ($X_{\text{CO}_2}$) reduces the partial pressure and chemical potential of CO₂, shifting the equilibrium boundary toward lower temperatures.
3. In a pure dolomitic marble undergoing metamorphism, what mineral appears before diopside under moderate temperatures if quartz is present?
(a) Tremolite    (b) Sillimanite    (c) Kyanite    (d) Omphacite
Answer: (a) Tremolite — The reaction 5 Dolomite + 8 Quartz + 1 H₂O = Tremolite + 3 Calcite + 7 CO₂ introduces tremolite at lower amphibolite grade before diopside appears.
4. The presence of forsterite in a metamorphic marble indicates that:
(a) The rock was originally an aluminum-rich bauxite
(b) The protolith was dolomite-rich and quartz was completely consumed by earlier reactions
(c) The metamorphism occurred at 0°C
(d) The rock was subjected to hydrothermal gold mineralization
Answer: (b) — Forsterite forms by reaction between diopside and excess dolomite; it cannot coexist with quartz.
5. A metamorphosed rock consisting essentially of garnet (almandine-pyrope), sillimanite, cordierite, quartz, and biotite is derived from which protolith?
(a) Basalt    (b) Pelite (shale)    (c) Limestone    (d) Dunite
Answer: (b) Pelite (shale) — High Al and K with low Ca produce sillimanite, cordierite, and almandine garnet.
6. Which chemical component distinguishes a siliceous dolomite from a pure limestone?
(a) Abundance of both MgO and SiO₂    (b) Total absence of CaO    (c) High Na₂O    (d) High P₂O₅
Answer: (a) Abundance of both MgO and SiO₂ — Dolomite provides Mg and Ca, while detrital sand/chert provides silica.
7. The chemical formula of wollastonite is:
(a) CaSiO₃    (b) CaMgSi₂O₆    (c) Mg₂SiO₄    (d) Ca₂Al₃Si₃O₁₂(OH)
Answer: (a) CaSiO₃ — Wollastonite is the calcium metasilicate pyroxenoid.
8. What is the role of potassium in the formation of K-feldspar in impure calc-silicate rocks?
(a) It comes from detrital illite/muscovite and forms phlogopite, which breaks down to K-feldspar at high grades
(b) It replaces silicon in the quartz lattice
(c) It forms liquid native potassium metal
(d) It acts as a catalyst without entering any mineral
Answer: (a) — Illite clay supplies potassium to form phlogopite at medium grades; at higher grades, phlogopite + calcite + quartz react to form K-feldspar + diopside.
Metamorphic Petrology / Question 9
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · METAMORPHIC PETROLOGY & MINERALOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Eclogite Facies Reactions, Plagioclase Breakdown & Grossular Garnet

Authoritative References: John D. Winter (Ch 25: Metamorphic Facies) & Dexter Perkins (Ch 13: Garnet Group Mineralogy)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Eclogite Facies & Garnet Crystal Chemistry
High-Pressure Metabasic Paragenesis, The Breakdown of Anorthite, and the Pyralspite vs. Ugrandite Series
1. Eclogite Facies Petrogenesis & The Destruction of Plagioclase

The eclogite facies represents the pinnacle of high-pressure crustal and mantle metamorphism ($P > 1.2\text{–}1.5\text{ GPa}$, equivalent to depths greater than 40–50 km, and temperatures from 500°C to over 900°C). Eclogites are classically defined as bimineralic metabasic rocks consisting essentially of omphacite (a jadeite-diopside solid solution sodium-calcium clinopyroxene) and pyrope-almandine-grossular garnet, without plagioclase!

"Plagioclase is unstable at the high pressures of the eclogite facies, and its breakdown reactions mark the transition from amphibolite or granulite facies to eclogite facies... Ultimately, anorthite breaks down in the absence of other mineral phases via the reaction: 3 Anorthite = Grossular + 2 Kyanite + Quartz." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 25, p. 579)
The Fundamental High-Pressure Breakdown Reactions of Plagioclase
Because plagioclase feldspar contains both albite ($NaAlSi_3O_8$) and anorthite ($CaAl_2Si_2O_8$), high lithostatic pressure forces both components to collapse into denser, octahedral-aluminum phases: Albite Breakdown:      NaAlSi₃O₈ = NaAlSi₂O₆ (Jadeite) + SiO₂ (Quartz) Anorthite Breakdown:   3 CaAl₂Si₂O₈ = Ca₃Al₂Si₃O₁₂ (Grossular) + 2 Al₂SiO₅ (Kyanite) + SiO₂ (Quartz)

Both reactions have positive $dP/dT$ Clapeyron slopes, with the dense product assemblages (lower molar volume) favored at elevated pressures.

2. Mathematical & Stoichiometric Proof of the Reaction

The reaction given in the exam question is: $$\mathbf{3\text{ Anorthite} = \text{Grossular} + 2\text{ Kyanite} + \text{Quartz}}$$

Let us perform a rigorous element-by-element atomic inventory to demonstrate that grossular must have the stoichiometric formula $\text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$:

Element Reactant: 3 × Anorthite ($3 \times \text{CaAl}_2\text{Si}_2\text{O}_8$) Product: 2 × Kyanite ($2 \times \text{Al}_2\text{SiO}_5$) Product: 1 × Quartz ($1 \times \text{SiO}_2$) Residual Required for Grossular Grossular Atom Count ($\text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$)
Calcium (Ca) $3 \times 1 = \mathbf{3}$ 0 0 $3 - 0 = \mathbf{3}$ Ca₃
Aluminum (Al) $3 \times 2 = \mathbf{6}$ $2 \times 2 = 4$ 0 $6 - 4 = \mathbf{2}$ Al₂
Silicon (Si) $3 \times 2 = \mathbf{6}$ $2 \times 1 = 2$ $1 \times 1 = 1$ $6 - (2+1) = \mathbf{3}$ Si₃
Oxygen (O) $3 \times 8 = \mathbf{24}$ $2 \times 5 = 10$ $1 \times 2 = 2$ $24 - (10+2) = \mathbf{12}$ O₁₂

The stoichiometric subtraction yields precisely: $\text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$.

3. Crystal Chemistry of the Garnet Group (Dexter Perkins, Chapter 13)

Garnets are orthosilicates (nesosilicates) belonging to the isometric (cubic) crystal system (space group $Ia\bar{3}d$, point group $4/m\bar{3}2/m$). Their structural formula is written as: $$X_3 Y_2 (\text{SiO}_4)_3 \quad \text{or} \quad X_3 Y_2 \text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$$ where:

  • $X$-site: Divalent cations in 8-fold (dodecahedral) coordination: $\text{Ca}^{2+}, \text{Mg}^{2+}, \text{Fe}^{2+}, \text{Mn}^{2+}$.
  • $Y$-site: Trivalent cations in 6-fold (octahedral) coordination: $\text{Al}^{3+}, \text{Fe}^{3+}, \text{Cr}^{3+}$.
  • Tetrahedral site: $\text{Si}^{4+}$ in independent $[\text{SiO}_4]^{4-}$ tetrahedra.
"Mineralogists divide garnets into two series: the pyralspites (pyrope, almandine, spessartine) and the ugrandites (uvarovite, grossular, andradite)... Grossular is the Al end member of the ugrandite series, Ca₃Al₂Si₃O₁₂. Like all garnets, grossular forms equant dodecahedral or trapezohedral crystals and has no cleavage." — Dexter Perkins, Mineralogy (3rd Ed., Chapter 13, pp. 410, 412)
II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 9
Question Statement
Q.9: 3 Anorthite = Grossular + 2 Kyanite + Quartz is a reaction which marks the eclogite facies in metabasic rocks. What will be the composition of grossular in the reaction given above?
(a) Ca₃Al₂Si₃O₁₂
(b) CaSiO₃
(c) (Ca, Mg)Si₂O₆
(d) CaCO₃
✓
Correct Answer: (a) Ca₃Al₂Si₃O₁₂
Grossular is the calcium-aluminum end-member of the ugrandite garnet series with the exact stoichiometric formula Ca₃Al₂Si₃O₁₂.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(a) Ca₃Al₂Si₃O₁₂ CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In mineralogy and petrology (Perkins, 2014, p. 412; Winter, 2010, p. 579), grossular is universally established as the calcic aluminous end-member garnet with chemical formula $\text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$. As proven by the mass balance of $3\text{ Anorthite} = 3\text{ CaAl}_2\text{Si}_2\text{O}_8 \rightarrow \text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12} + 2\text{ Al}_2\text{SiO}_5 + \text{SiO}_2$, this formula satisfies stoichiometry exactly.
(b) CaSiO₃ INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: $\text{CaSiO}_3$ is the chemical formula of wollastonite, a calcium inosilicate (pyroxenoid) typical of thermally metamorphosed limestones. Wollastonite lacks aluminum entirely and is structurally and chemically distinct from garnet.
(c) (Ca, Mg)Si₂O₆ INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: $(\text{Ca, Mg})\text{Si}_2\text{O}_6$ represents diopside (specifically $\text{CaMgSi}_2\text{O}_6$), a monoclinic clinopyroxene. Diopside contains magnesium (absent in pure anorthite) and has a single-chain inosilicate crystal structure with a $(\text{Si}:\text{O})$ ratio of $1:3$, whereas garnet is an orthosilicate with an isolated $(\text{SiO}_4)$ ratio of $1:4$.
(d) CaCO₃ INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: $\text{CaCO}_3$ is the formula for the anhydrous carbonate minerals calcite and aragonite. Carbonates contain carbon and lack silicon and aluminum altogether.

8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Eclogite Facies & Garnet Mineralogy
1. In the structural formula of garnet $X_3 Y_2 \text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$, what are the coordination numbers of the $X$, $Y$, and $\text{Si}$ sites?
(a) 4, 6, 8    (b) 8, 6, 4    (c) 6, 6, 6    (d) 12, 8, 4
Answer: (b) 8, 6, 4 — The divalent $X$-site is in 8-fold (dodecahedral) coordination, the trivalent $Y$-site is in 6-fold (octahedral) coordination, and $Si$ is in 4-fold (tetrahedral) coordination.
2. Which clinopyroxene is the diagnostic phase defining the eclogite facies in metabasites?
(a) Diopside    (b) Hedenbergite    (c) Omphacite    (d) Aegirine
Answer: (c) Omphacite — Omphacite is an intermediate solid solution between augite/diopside and jadeite, characteristic of eclogites.
3. The albite breakdown reaction marking the onset of high-pressure blueschist/eclogite conditions is:
(a) Albite = Nepheline + Quartz
(b) Albite = Jadeite + Quartz
(c) Albite + Calcite = Anorthite + CO₂
(d) Albite = Glaucophane + H₂O
Answer: (b) Albite = Jadeite + Quartz — This classic univariant reaction represents the breakdown of sodic plagioclase at high pressure.
4. The green color of the ugrandite garnet uvarovite is caused by the presence of:
(a) Titanium    (b) Chromium ($\text{Cr}^{3+}$) in the octahedral site    (c) Nickel    (d) Copper
Answer: (b) Chromium — Uvarovite has the formula $\text{Ca}_3\text{Cr}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$, imparting a vibrant emerald-green color.
5. Coesite and microdiamonds found as inclusions in eclogitic garnets provide proof of:
(a) Very low temperature    (b) Ultra-high pressure (UHP) metamorphism    (c) Contact aureole baking    (d) Hydrothermal alteration
Answer: (b) Ultra-high pressure (UHP) metamorphism — Coesite requires $P > 2.5\text{–}3.0\text{ GPa}$ (depths $>90\text{ km}$), proving subduction of continental/oceanic crust into the mantle.
6. In garnet nomenclature, the name "ugrandite" is a portmanteau derived from:
(a) Ural, Granite, Diorite
(b) Uvarovite, Grossular, Andradite
(c) Ultramafic, Granulite, Diopside
(d) Underground Anorthite Diopside
Answer: (b) Uvarovite, Grossular, Andradite — The three calcium-bearing end-members form the ugrandite series.
7. In the reaction $3\text{ Anorthite} = \text{Grossular} + 2\text{ Kyanite} + \text{Quartz}$, why does volume decrease ($-\Delta V$)?
(a) Garnet and kyanite have much denser, closely packed atomic structures with higher coordination numbers than plagioclase
(b) Gas is liberated during the reaction
(c) Quartz contracts under heat
(d) Aluminum evaporates
Answer: (a) — High pressure favors dense phases where Al enters 6-fold coordination (kyanite and garnet) rather than 4-fold coordination (anorthite).
8. What is the composition of the garnet andradite?
(a) $\text{Ca}_3\text{Fe}_2^{3+}\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$    (b) $\text{Fe}_3^{2+}\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$    (c) $\text{Mg}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$    (d) $\text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$
Answer: (a) $\text{Ca}_3\text{Fe}_2^{3+}\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$ — Andradite is the calcium-ferric iron end-member of the ugrandite series.
Metamorphic Petrology & Textures / Question 10
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · METAMORPHIC PETROLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Granoblastic Polygonal Texture, Annealing & Grain Boundary Migration

Authoritative Reference: John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 23: Structures and Textures of Metamorphic Rocks)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Metamorphic Textures & Recrystallization
Recovery, Dynamic vs. Static Recrystallization, Interfacial Surface Free Energy & 120° Triple Junctions
1. The Thermodynamic Driving Force for Recrystallization

In metamorphic geology, recrystallization is defined as the structural rearrangement of mineral grains without necessarily changing their bulk chemical composition. When rocks undergo deformation, strain energy accumulates within the crystal lattice in the form of dislocations, subgrains, and high-energy curved grain boundaries.

When deformation ceases but elevated temperature persists—a process known as static annealing—the rock seeks thermodynamic equilibrium by minimizing two primary sources of excess internal free energy:

  1. Lattice Strain Energy: Minimized during initial recovery through subgrain rotation and dislocation climb/glide.
  2. Interfacial Surface Free Energy ($\gamma$): Grains possess excess energy along their mutual contact boundaries. Thermodynamics dictates that the system must reduce total boundary surface area per unit volume: $$\Delta G_{\text{surface}} = \sum \gamma_i A_i$$ To minimize this surface free energy, high-angle grain boundaries migrate toward centers of curvature, eliminating small strained grains, straightening boundary segments, and achieving an optimal geometric packing.
"In cases of advanced recrystallization, grain boundary area reduction straightens the boundaries further, eventually producing a granoblastic polygonal texture that is completely recrystallized (annealed). The grain boundaries meet at approximately 120° angles, representing the lowest interfacial energy configuration (foam texture)." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 23, p. 510)
2. The 120° Triple Junction & Foam Texture Analogy

In an isotropic, monomineralic aggregate (such as pure quartzite or calcite marble), all grain boundaries possess identical interfacial free energy ($\gamma_{11} = \gamma_{12} = \gamma_{13}$). At any line where three grains meet—termed a triple junction—interfacial surface tensions balance like forces in mechanical vector equilibrium: $$\vec{\gamma}_1 + \vec{\gamma}_2 + \vec{\gamma}_3 = 0$$ Because the magnitudes are equal, the three dihedral angles must be identically: $$\theta = \frac{360^\circ}{3} = \mathbf{120^\circ}$$

This arrangement is mathematically identical to a soap bubble froth (foam structure). A rock exhibiting equigranular, polygonal crystal outlines with planar boundaries intersecting at approximately 120° is universally designated as having a granoblastic polygonal texture.

II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 10
Question Statement
Q.10: Granoblastic polygonal texture is an example of:
(a) Texture of recrystallization
(b) Texture of crystallization
(c) Texture of metasomatism
(d) Texture of mylonitization
✓
Correct Answer: (a) Texture of recrystallization
Granoblastic polygonal texture is the quintessential product of solid-state static recrystallization (thermal annealing) driven by the minimization of grain boundary surface energy.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(a) Texture of recrystallization CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In metamorphic geology and petrology (Winter, 2010, Chapter 23, pp. 510–512; Passchier & Trouw, 2005; Vernon, 2004), granoblastic polygonal texture (also called polygonal mosaic texture or foam texture) is universally recognized as the hallmark texture of solid-state recrystallization. During static thermal annealing following deformation (or during contact metamorphism in hornfelses and marbles), grain-boundary migration eliminates irregular, strained boundaries to minimize surface free energy, causing grain contacts to straighten into planar boundaries that meet at characteristic ~120° triple junctions.
(b) Texture of crystallization INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A "texture of crystallization" specifically refers to primary igneous crystallization directly from a liquid silicate melt (producing textures such as ophitic, subophitic, porphyritic, trachytic, or cumulate textures with euhedral crystal faces) or primary sedimentary precipitation from an aqueous solution. Granoblastic polygonal texture forms strictly in the solid state by post-depositional/post-magmatic recrystallization.
(c) Texture of metasomatism INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: Textures of metasomatism are characterized by active, open-system chemical mass transfer driven by hydrothermal fluids. They produce replacement textures, reactionary overgrowths, compositional zoning, atoll structures, and vein fillings (such as in skarns and greisens), rather than equigranular, stable 120° polygonal mosaics.
(d) Texture of mylonitization INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: Mylonitization is a high-strain dynamic deformation process operating in ductile shear zones. It produces strong foliation, grain size reduction, ribbon quartz, rotated porphyroclasts ($\sigma$- and $\delta$-type mantles), and core-and-mantle structures. Mylonitic textures represent high accumulated strain, whereas granoblastic polygonal texture represents the complete thermal destruction (annealing) of such strain!
III

Genetic Texture Category Primary Physical Mechanism Defining Textural Features Representative Rock Types
Texture of Recrystallization (Annealing) Static solid-state grain boundary migration minimizing surface free energy Granoblastic polygonal, 120° triple junctions, straight boundaries, equant grains Marble, Quartzite, Granulite, Hornfels
Texture of Dynamic Deformation (Mylonitization) Crystal-plastic dislocation creep and dynamic recrystallization during shear Ribbon quartz, core-and-mantle, rotated porphyroclasts, ultramylonitic foliation Mylonite, Ultramylonite, Phyllonite

8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Metamorphic Textures & Recrystallization
1. The dihedral angle of 120° at triple junctions in granoblastic polygonal textures results directly from:
(a) Uniaxial compression
(b) Balance of equal interfacial surface energies among three identical adjacent grains
(c) Rapid quenching from magma
(d) Dissolution along cleavage planes
Answer: (b) — In monomineralic aggregates, vector balance of equal interfacial surface tensions produces dihedral angles of 120°.
2. Which term describes the process where strained grains are replaced by new, strain-free grains during prolonged exposure to elevated temperatures without active deformation?
(a) Cataclasis    (b) Static annealing (recrystallization)    (c) Dynamic fracturing    (d) Exsolution
Answer: (b) Static annealing (recrystallization) — Post-kinematic heating allows grain boundaries to migrate and eliminate dislocations.
3. A monomineralic metamorphic rock composed entirely of interlocking equant quartz crystals displaying 120° triple junctions is classified texturally as:
(a) Nematoblastic    (b) Granoblastic polygonal quartzite    (c) Lepidoblastic schist    (d) Mylonitic ribbon
Answer: (b) Granoblastic polygonal quartzite — Granoblastic describes equant grains; polygonal denotes the straight-sided 120° geometry.
4. The term "foam texture" is frequently used as a synonym for which metamorphic texture?
(a) Granoblastic polygonal    (b) Slaty cleavage    (c) Amygdaloidal    (d) Porphyroclastic
Answer: (a) Granoblastic polygonal — Soap foam cells naturally meet at 120° angles to minimize surface tension, matching annealed crystals.
5. In contrast to static recrystallization, dynamic recrystallization occurs:
(a) During active tectonic deformation and stress
(b) At absolute zero temperature
(c) Only in the presence of molten sulfur
(d) Exclusively on meteorites
Answer: (a) During active tectonic deformation and stress — Dynamic recrystallization proceeds synchronously with differential stress via subgrain rotation and bulging.
6. When unequal mineral phases (e.g., quartz and biotite) meet at a boundary, why do dihedral angles deviate from 120°?
(a) Interfacial energies between different mineral phases ($\gamma_{\text{Qtz-Bt}}$) differ from those between identical phases ($\gamma_{\text{Qtz-Qtz}}$)
(b) The rock ceases to be metamorphic
(c) Chemical equilibrium is impossible
(d) Gravity distorts the crystal lattice
Answer: (a) — Interfacial free energy depends on lattice misfit across differing mineral species, shifting dihedral angles away from 120°.
7. Which of the following rocks is most characteristically dominated by a granoblastic polygonal texture?
(a) Pure Calcite Marble    (b) Graphitic Slate    (c) Chlorite Schist    (d) Ultramylonite
Answer: (a) Pure Calcite Marble — Pure calcite recrystallizes readily under thermal metamorphism to form uniform granoblastic polygonal mosaics.
8. What happens to grain size during progressive static grain growth (Ostwald ripening) in an annealed metamorphic rock?
(a) Mean grain size increases as larger grains consume smaller grains to reduce total grain boundary area
(b) Grain size reduces continuously to sub-micron size
(c) Grains dissolve into pore gas
(d) Grains convert to glass
Answer: (a) — Grain growth driven by surface energy reduction causes larger grains to grow at the expense of smaller grains with higher surface-to-volume ratios.

Calc-Silicate Metamorphism & Sillimanite-Equivalent High-Grade Facies

Authoritative Reference: John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Chapter 29: Metamorphism of Calcareous & Ultramafic Rocks)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Regional Metamorphism of Calc-Silicate Rocks
Marly Protoliths, Mixed-Volatile Phase Equilibria & Correlation with Barrovian Pelitic Zones
1. Calc-Silicate Rocks & Cross-Facies Correlation

Calc-silicate rocks are metamorphosed marls or calcareous mudstones/sandstones containing significant amounts of both carbonate components ($\text{CaCO}_3, \text{CaMg(CO}_3)_2$) and silicate detritus (quartz, feldspar, illite/kaolinite clays providing Al, Si, and K). Because they contain both calcium and aluminum, calc-silicates develop distinctive Ca-Al-Mg silicates (zoisite/clinozoisite, grossular, anorthite, diopside, wollastonite) that are exceptionally sensitive to metamorphic grade.

In a landmark classic study, W. Q. Kennedy (1949) established the direct correlation between the progressive mineral zones in calc-silicate rocks and the Barrovian zones in associated pelitic schists of the Scottish Highlands:

  • Biotite Zone Equivalent: Zoisite + Calcite + Biotite + Albite + Quartz
  • Garnet Zone Equivalent: Clinozoisite + Calcite + Hornblende + Oligoclase
  • Staurolite / Kyanite Zone Equivalent: Diopside + Anorthite + Calcite ± Hornblende
  • Sillimanite Zone Equivalent (Peak Grade): Wollastonite + Grossular (+ Diopside + Anorthite)
2. Petrologic Reactions Generating Wollastonite + Grossular

Under high amphibolite-to-granulite facies conditions equivalent to the pelitic sillimanite zone ($T > 650^\circ\text{–}750^\circ\text{C}$), two quintessential decarbonation reactions occur:

Reaction 1: The Wollastonite-Forming Decarbonation Reaction
Calcite reacts with excess quartz to eliminate the Cal-Qtz tie-line and produce wollastonite: Calcite + Quartz = Wollastonite + CO₂      (CaCO₃ + SiO₂ = CaSiO₃ + CO₂)

In regional terranes buffered to moderate-to-high $X_{\text{CO}_2}$, this reaction requires high temperatures ($> 650^\circ\text{–}720^\circ\text{C}$), precisely matching the sillimanite zone.

Reaction 2: The Grossular-Forming Decarbonation Reaction
Calcic plagioclase (anorthite) reacts with remaining calcite and quartz to produce calcic grossular garnet: Anorthite + 2 Calcite + Quartz = Grossular + 2 CO₂ CaAl₂Si₂O₈ + 2 CaCO₃ + SiO₂ = Ca₃Al₂Si₃O₁₂ + 2 CO₂

Alternatively, at high pressures, zoisite breaks down: 2 Zoisite + Calcite + Quartz = 3 Grossular + CO₂ + H₂O.

Because both wollastonite and grossular require the complete breakdown of calcite with quartz and anorthite under peak thermal conditions, the assemblage Wollastonite + Grossular serves as the definitive high-grade calc-silicate marker equivalent to the sillimanite zone of pelites.

II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 11
Question Statement
Q.11: In metamorphism of calc-silicate rocks, which assemblage can be considered equivalent to the sillimanite zone of pelitic rocks?
(a) Anorthite–pyroxene
(b) Zoisite–calcite–biotite
(c) Diopside–anorthite
(d) Wollastonite–grossular
✓
Correct Answer: (d) Wollastonite–grossular
The high-temperature decarbonation assemblage of wollastonite + grossular defines the peak calc-silicate metamorphic grade, directly equivalent to the sillimanite zone in pelites.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(d) Wollastonite–grossular CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In metamorphic petrology and regional metamorphic zonations (Winter, 2010, Chapter 29; Kennedy, 1949; Bucher & Grapes, 2011), the stable coexistence of wollastonite ($\text{CaSiO}_3$) and grossular ($\text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$) requires high temperatures ($> 650^\circ\text{–}750^\circ\text{C}$) to drive the univariant decarbonation reactions $\text{Cal} + \text{Qtz} = \text{Wo} + \text{CO}_2$ and $\text{An} + 2\text{Cal} + \text{Qtz} = \text{Grs} + 2\text{CO}_2$. This peak assemblage represents the highest calc-silicate zone in regional metamorphic terranes and contact aureoles, directly equivalent to the upper amphibolite-to-granulite facies (the sillimanite zone of pelitic rocks).
(a) Anorthite–pyroxene INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: While anorthite and pyroxene (diopside) can occur across a broad temperature range in calc-silicate rocks, "anorthite–pyroxene" is more typically the characteristic signature of mafic granulites. In calc-silicates, anorthite and diopside form at intermediate grades (staurolite/kyanite zone); at the sillimanite grade, anorthite breaks down with calcite to produce grossular!
(b) Zoisite–calcite–biotite INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: In Kennedy's (1949) classic zonal correlation, the assemblage zoisite + calcite + biotite + albite is explicitly the diagnostic marker of the biotite zone (low-grade greenschist facies, T < 400°C–450°C). Zoisite is a hydrous epidote-group mineral that is completely unstable at the high temperatures of the sillimanite zone.
(c) Diopside–anorthite INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: The diopside–anorthite assemblage corresponds to the middle amphibolite facies (kyanite zone equivalent). Under progressive metamorphism, diopside and anorthite form before wollastonite and grossular appear. Only when temperatures rise further into the sillimanite zone does quartz react with calcite to generate wollastonite and anorthite react with calcite to yield grossular.

8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Calc-Silicates & Metamorphic Zonation
1. In Kennedy's (1949) zonal correlation of metamorphic rocks in the Scottish Highlands, which calc-silicate assemblage corresponds to the biotite zone of pelites?
(a) Wollastonite + Grossular    (b) Zoisite + Calcite + Biotite    (c) Diopside + Forsterite    (d) Periclase + Brucite
Answer: (b) Zoisite + Calcite + Biotite — Low-grade marly rocks develop zoisite and biotite in equilibrium with calcite, matching the pelitic biotite isograd.
2. What is the fundamental decarbonation reaction that introduces wollastonite into high-grade calc-silicates?
(a) Calcite + Quartz = Wollastonite + CO₂
(b) Dolomite + Quartz = Diopside + 2 CO₂
(c) Tremolite + Calcite = Diopside + CO₂ + H₂O
(d) Grossular + Quartz = Anorthite + Wollastonite
Answer: (a) Calcite + Quartz = Wollastonite + CO₂ — The classic univariant decarbonation equilibrium.
3. The presence of grossular garnet in calc-silicate rocks requires the presence of which element in the sedimentary protolith?
(a) Titanium    (b) Aluminum (from detrital clay/shale)    (c) Zirconium    (d) Phosphorus
Answer: (b) Aluminum — Pure carbonate lacks Al; grossular ($\text{Ca}_3\text{Al}_2\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}$) requires aluminum supplied by detrital clay minerals.
4. In contact metamorphic aureoles of limestone (e.g., Crestmore, California), the innermost, highest-temperature zone adjacent to the pluton is typically the:
(a) Forsterite zone    (b) Monticellite zone    (c) Grossular–Wollastonite–Diopside zone    (d) Chlorite zone
Answer: (c) Grossular–Wollastonite–Diopside zone — The innermost zone represents peak temperatures where silica metasomatism and intense decarbonation yield grossular, wollastonite, and diopside.
5. On an isobaric $T-X_{\text{CO}_2}$ diagram, why do devolatilization reaction curves that liberate both H₂O and CO₂ (e.g., tremolite formation) exhibit a parabolic maximum?
(a) Due to changes in gravity
(b) Because the reaction produces mixed volatiles, reaching maximum thermal stability at a specific fluid composition matching the stoichiometric product ratio ($X_{\text{CO}_2} = n_{\text{CO}_2} / (n_{\text{CO}_2} + n_{\text{H}_2\text{O}})$)
(c) Because calcite melts at low temperatures
(d) Because quartz dissolves into the gas phase
Answer: (b) — Reactions producing both H₂O and CO₂ reach their peak temperature on a $T-X_{\text{CO}_2}$ diagram where the fluid composition equals the ratio in which the gases are evolved.
6. Which calc-silicate mineral is an epidote-group sorosilicate with formula $\text{Ca}_2\text{Al}_3\text{Si}_3\text{O}_{12}\text{(OH)}$?
(a) Zoisite/Clinozoisite    (b) Wollastonite    (c) Diopside    (d) Forsterite
Answer: (a) Zoisite/Clinozoisite — Zoisite (orthorhombic) and clinozoisite (monoclinic) are hydrated Ca-Al silicates characteristic of low-to-medium grade calc-silicates.
7. In high-grade regional calc-silicate rocks, scapolite commonly forms by replacement of:
(a) Plagioclase feldspar in the presence of $\text{CO}_2$- and $\text{Cl}$-rich fluids    (b) Olivine    (c) Biotite    (d) Pyrite
Answer: (a) — Scapolite (meionite/marialite solid solution) is an alumosilicate containing volatile anions ($CO_3^{2-}, Cl^-, SO_4^{2-}$) that replaces plagioclase at high metamorphic temperatures.
8. The transition from the diopside–anorthite zone to the wollastonite–grossular zone in calc-silicate rocks coincides with which pelitic reaction?
(a) Chlorite = Biotite
(b) Muscovite + Quartz = Sillimanite + K-feldspar + H₂O (or melt)
(c) Smectite = Illite
(d) Kaolinite = Pyrophyllite
Answer: (b) — The second sillimanite isograd (muscovite breakdown to sillimanite + Kfs) occurs at roughly $650^\circ\text{–}700^\circ\text{C}$, matching the formation of wollastonite and grossular in calc-silicates.
Structural Geology & Tectonics / Question 12
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · STRUCTURAL GEOLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Fold Anatomy, Hinge Line Geometry & Curvature Analysis

Authoritative Reference: Ben A. van der Pluijm & Stephen Marshak, Earth Structure: An Introduction to Structural Geology and Tectonics (2nd Ed., Chapter 10: Folds and Folding)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Anatomical Elements of Folds
Curvature Distribution, Hinge Points vs. Inflection Points, Cylindricity & Axial Surfaces
1. Geometric Elements of a Folded Surface

Folds are curved geological surfaces resulting from ductile bending or buckling of planar layers. In modern structural geology (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Chapter 10; Ramsay & Huber, 1987; Fossen, 2016), the anatomy of a fold is defined rigorously by mathematical and geometric parameters:

  • Hinge Point: On a profile section of a single folded surface, the point where the radius of curvature is at a minimum, which means the curvature ($\kappa = 1/R$) is at a maximum.
  • Hinge Line (or Hinge): The continuous three-dimensional line connecting points of maximum curvature on a single folded layer. The hinge line is a real, physical line on the bed that can be curved or straight, plunging or horizontal.
  • Fold Limb: The section of the fold between two adjacent hinge lines. In profile, limbs are the domains of relatively lower curvature.
  • Inflection Point / Line: The point or line on a limb where the curvature changes sense (from convex outward to concave inward), where curvature $\kappa = 0$. The inflection points mark the boundary between antiforms and synforms.
  • Axial Surface: The two-dimensional surface formed by connecting the hinge lines of successive stacked stratigraphic layers. When planar, it is termed the axial plane.
"The hinge line (or simply the hinge) is the line of maximum curvature on a folded surface. In a profile view, the hinge is a point (the hinge point). When traced through three dimensions across the bed, these points define the hinge line. Limbs are the sides of the fold that have lower curvature." — Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Earth Structure (2nd Ed., Chapter 10, p. 250)
2. Critical Distinctions: Hinge Line vs. Fold Axis vs. Crest Line

Examinations frequently test whether students confuse the physical hinge line with other mathematical or topographic lines:

Structural Term Rigorous Geometric Definition Physical Reality Applicability
Hinge Line Line of maximum curvature in a folded bed Real, tangible line on the rock surface Applies to ALL folds (cylindrical & non-cylindrical)
Fold Axis An imaginary line of fixed orientation that, when moved parallel to itself, generates the fold Mathematical generating vector (not a specific line on a bed) Applies ONLY to cylindrical folds
Crest Line Line connecting the highest structural/topographic points on a fold Topographic/elevation dependent Coincides with hinge line only in upright, non-plunging folds!
Trough Line Line connecting the lowest structural points in a fold Elevation dependent Coincides with synformal hinge line only in upright folds
II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 12
Question Statement
Q.12: The hinge line of a fold is:
(a) A line that divides the fold into symmetrical parts
(b) A line that is always parallel to fold axis
(c) A line of maximum curvature in a folded bed
(d) A line of minimum curvature in a folded bed
✓
Correct Answer: (c) A line of maximum curvature in a folded bed
By standard international definition in structural geology, the hinge line is the locus of points of maximum curvature across a folded surface.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(c) A line of maximum curvature in a folded bed CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In all authoritative textbooks of structural geology (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, 2004, p. 250; Ramsay & Huber, 1987; Fossen, 2016, p. 240; Billings, 1972), the hinge line of a fold is explicitly defined as "the line connecting points of maximum curvature on a folded surface." In two-dimensional profile view, this is the hinge point where the radius of curvature $R$ reaches its minimum, meaning the rate of change of orientation along the profile arc ($\kappa = d\theta / ds$) is at its absolute maximum.
(a) A line that divides the fold into symmetrical parts INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A line (or surface) that divides a fold into two mirror-image symmetrical halves is the axial surface of a symmetric fold. In asymmetric folds (which constitute the vast majority of natural tectonic folds), the axial surface and hinge line do NOT divide the fold into symmetrical parts; one limb is longer and dips differently than the other. Thus, dividing a fold symmetrically is not a defining characteristic of a hinge line.
(b) A line that is always parallel to fold axis INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: The hinge line is only parallel to the fold axis in cylindrical folds. Non-cylindrical folds (such as conical folds, dome-and-basin structures, and sheath folds) have curved hinge lines whose plunge and trend change continuously along their length; such folds do not possess a fold axis at all. Therefore, a hinge line cannot be defined as "always parallel to fold axis."
(d) A line of minimum curvature in a folded bed INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: The line of minimum curvature on a folded profile is the inflection line (where curvature is zero, $\kappa = 0$) or the planar fold limbs. Defining the hinge line as "minimum curvature" is the exact inverse of its true physical nature.

8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Fold Geometry & Structural Analysis
1. The point on a folded profile where the sense of curvature changes from convex to concave is termed the:
(a) Hinge point    (b) Inflection point    (c) Crest point    (d) Culmination
Answer: (b) Inflection point — Inflection points have zero curvature ($\kappa = 0$) and mark the geometric boundary between adjacent antiforms and synforms.
2. A fold that can be generated geometrically by moving a straight line parallel to itself in space is defined as:
(a) Non-cylindrical    (b) Cylindrical    (c) Conical    (d) Ptygmatic
Answer: (b) Cylindrical — Cylindrical folds possess a unique, constant fold axis orientation across the entire folded surface.
3. Under what structural condition does the crest line of an antiform coincide exactly with its hinge line?
(a) In an inclined, plunging fold
(b) In an upright, horizontal (non-plunging) fold
(c) In an overturned fold
(d) In a recumbent fold
Answer: (b) In an upright, horizontal (non-plunging) fold — When the axial plane is vertical, the line of maximum curvature coincides with the structurally highest line (crest). In inclined folds, the crest shifts away from the hinge onto the upper limb.
4. The surface that connects the hinge lines of successive stacked layers in a multi-layered folded sequence is the:
(a) Axial surface    (b) Cleavage plane    (c) Fault mirror    (d) Bedding slip plane
Answer: (a) Axial surface — The axial surface connects hinge lines through depth across all affected layers.
5. A sheath fold is an extreme example of which category of folds?
(a) Strictly cylindrical fold    (b) Highly non-cylindrical fold with a strongly curved hinge line    (c) Monocline    (d) Chevron fold
Answer: (b) — Sheath folds develop in high-strain shear zones where hinge lines rotate towards the shear direction, forming tubular, non-cylindrical geometries.
6. In Ramsay's fold classification based on dip isogons, Class 1B folds are:
(a) Similar folds    (b) Parallel (concentric) folds of uniform layer thickness    (c) Folds with thinned hinges    (d) Ptygmatic folds
Answer: (b) Parallel (concentric) folds of uniform layer thickness — Class 1B folds maintain constant orthogonal bed thickness ($t = \text{constant}$).
7. The interlimb angle of an "isoclinal" fold is:
(a) 120° – 70°    (b) 70° – 30°    (c) 30° – 10°    (d) 0° – 10° (limbs are essentially parallel)
Answer: (d) 0° – 10° — Fleuty's (1964) classification defines isoclinal folds by interlimb angles between 0° and 10°, where both limbs dip in the same direction at equal angles.
8. What is the orientation of axial planar cleavage relative to the hinge line of a non-plunging fold?
(a) Perpendicular to the hinge line
(b) Parallel to the hinge line, with the cleavage intersecting bedding along a line parallel to the hinge
(c) At a random 45° angle
(d) Completely unoriented
Answer: (b) — The intersection lineation between axial planar cleavage ($S_1$) and bedding ($S_0$) is parallel to the fold hinge line ($L_1 \parallel \text{hinge}$).
Structural Geology & Tectonics / Question 13
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · STRUCTURAL GEOLOGY & PLATE TECTONICS · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Oceanic Strike-Slip Faults, Transform Boundaries & Fracture Zones

Authoritative Reference: Ben A. van der Pluijm & Stephen Marshak, Earth Structure (2nd Ed., Chapters 8, 14 & 19)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Strike-Slip Faults & Transform Boundaries
Wilson's Revolutionary Concept, Mechanics of Ridge-Ridge Offsets & Oceanic Fracture Zones
1. J. Tuzo Wilson & The Formulation of Transform Faults

Prior to 1965, all strike-slip faults were interpreted as simple transcurrent faults that cut across preexisting geological features. In 1965, the Canadian geophysicist J. Tuzo Wilson published a revolutionary paper titled "A new class of faults and their bearing on continental drift" (Nature), recognizing that oceanic strike-slip faults that offset mid-ocean ridges represent an entirely new mechanical and kinematic category: transform faults.

"In the preferred sense, transform faults are plate boundaries at which lithosphere is neither created nor destroyed... A transform fault links two other plate boundaries (divergent or convergent) and accommodates the relative horizontal motion between the separating plates. Seismicity along ridge-ridge transform faults is strictly confined to the active segment connecting the two ridge crests." — Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Earth Structure (2nd Ed., Table 8.2, p. 171 & Chapter 19, p. 450)
2. Diagnostic Distinctions: Transform Faults vs. Other Strike-Slip Faults

In structural geology (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Chapter 8, Table 8.2), strike-slip faults are subdivided into distinct genetic classes based on their tectonic scale and boundary terminations:

Strike-Slip Fault Type Tectonic Nature & Termination Displacement Distribution Seismic Activity Pattern
Transform Fault Major lithospheric plate boundary; terminates abruptly at other plate boundaries (ridges, trenches) Constant displacement along the active plate boundary segment Earthquakes strictly confined to segment between ridge crests; fossil extensions (fracture zones) are aseismic
Transcurrent (Wrench) Fault Intraplate strike-slip fault; dies out into rock within a single plate at tip lines Displacement is maximum at center and decreases to zero at both tips Earthquakes distributed along the entire length of the fault plane
Tear Fault Local strike-slip fault bounding a thrust sheet or graben segment to accommodate differential advance Limited to the moving thrust nappe Shallow, local seismicity during thrust emplacement
Scissor Fault Rotational fault where displacement changes sign across a pivotal axis of zero slip Variable along strike (rotational hinge) Local block tilting
II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 13
Question Statement
Q.13: In ocean, regional strike-slip faults associated with lithospheric plate boundaries are known as:
(a) Transform faults
(b) Tear faults
(c) Wrench faults
(d) Scissor faults
✓
Correct Answer: (a) Transform faults
Regional strike-slip faults that offset oceanic ridges and function as conservative lithospheric plate boundaries are universally designated as transform faults.
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(a) Transform faults CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In global tectonics and structural geology (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Table 8.2, p. 171; Wilson, 1965; Kearey, Klepeis & Vine, 2009), transform faults are explicitly defined as regional strike-slip faults that constitute primary conservative plate boundaries connecting segments of mid-ocean ridges or subduction zones. Across the global ocean floor (such as the Romanche and Charlie-Gibbs Fracture Zones in the Atlantic, and the Mendocino and Clarion Transforms in the Pacific), these faults accommodate horizontal motion between major tectonic plates without generating or destroying lithosphere.
(b) Tear faults INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A tear fault is a localized intraplate strike-slip fault that develops within a single thrust sheet or hanging wall block, accommodating differential forward motion or lateral termination of thrust nappes. Tear faults are minor structural features confined to individual fold-thrust belts and are not regional lithospheric plate boundaries.
(c) Wrench faults INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: Wrench fault (or transcurrent fault) is an older descriptive term for large, high-angle strike-slip faults that occur within continental plates (e.g., the Great Glen Fault in Scotland). Crucially, transcurrent/wrench faults terminate at tip lines within intact crust rather than ending at plate boundary junctions, and their slip diminishes toward both ends.
(d) Scissor faults INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A scissor fault (or pivotal fault) is a fault characterized by differential rotation around an axis perpendicular to the fault plane, such that displacement increases in opposite directions away from a neutral pivot point. It is not an oceanic plate boundary.
III
Synthesis Reference Tables, Diagnostic Criteria & High-Yield Practice
Plate Tectonic Fault Classification & 8 Exam-Grade Multiple-Choice Questions
Classification of Strike-Slip Faults by Scale and Geological Setting
Classification Primary Geodynamic Setting Boundary Terminations Motion Relative to Spreading Representative World Examples
Oceanic Transform Fault Ocean basins, lithospheric plate boundaries Spreading ridge axes or subduction zones Constant slip across active segment Romanche, Vema, Charlie-Gibbs Transforms
Continental Transform Fault Continental lithospheric plate boundaries Triple junctions or oceanic ridge/trench systems Accommodates full relative plate motion San Andreas Fault (California), Alpine Fault (NZ)
Transcurrent (Wrench) Fault Intracontinental strike-slip Dies out into ductile shear zones or tip lines Variable along strike; zero at tips Great Glen Fault, Altyn Tagh Fault
Tear Fault Fold-and-thrust belts, accretionary wedges Terminates at floor/roof thrusts Compartmentalization of thrust nappes Jura Mountains tear faults, Moine thrust tears
8 High-Yield Practice Questions on Transform Faults & Strike-Slip Tectonics
1. Who first proposed the existence and mechanics of transform faults in 1965?
(a) Alfred Wegener    (b) Harry Hess    (c) J. Tuzo Wilson    (d) Fred Vine
Answer: (c) J. Tuzo Wilson — Wilson published the foundational paper demonstrating that transform faults link divergent and convergent plate boundaries.
2. Along an oceanic ridge-ridge transform fault, where do earthquakes occur?
(a) Across the entire length of the fracture zone extending across the whole ocean basin
(b) Exclusively along the active segment between the two offsetting ridge crests
(c) Only at the exact center of the fracture zone
(d) Earthquakes never occur on transform faults
Answer: (b) — Between the ridge segments, adjacent lithospheric plates move in opposite directions, generating seismicity; outside this active offset, plates move in the same direction at equal speeds, forming aseismic fracture zones.
3. If a mid-ocean ridge trending north-south is stepped to the right (dextral apparent offset), what is the actual sense of slip along the active transform fault?
(a) Dextral (right-lateral)    (b) Sinistral (left-lateral)    (c) Pure dip-slip normal    (d) Pure thrust
Answer: (b) Sinistral (left-lateral) — In a ridge-ridge transform, spreading from the two offset ridges forces the block between them to slide in the opposite sense (sinistral) to the apparent geographic offset of the ridge crests.
4. The San Andreas Fault in California is classified tectonically as a:
(a) Mid-plate intraplate fault
(b) Continental transform fault connecting the East Pacific Rise with the Mendocino Triple Junction
(c) Local tear fault in a fold belt
(d) Subduction megathrust
Answer: (b) — The San Andreas Fault is a classic right-lateral continental transform plate boundary connecting spreading ridges and triple junctions.
5. Oceanic fracture zones extending beyond the active ridge segments represent:
(a) Active subduction trenches
(b) Aseismic scars marking juxtaposed lithosphere of different ages and thermal subsidence histories
(c) Active mid-ocean ridges
(d) Collisional mountain belts
Answer: (b) — Fracture zones are fossil, inactive traces of transforms where crust of different ages (and therefore different depths) are juxtaposed.
6. Which term describes a regional strike-slip fault that terminates within a single tectonic plate without connecting other plate boundaries?
(a) Transform fault    (b) Transcurrent (wrench) fault    (c) Spreading center    (d) Benioff zone
Answer: (b) Transcurrent (wrench) fault — Transcurrent faults are intraplate features whose slip dies out toward both tips.
7. In a pull-apart basin formed along a strike-slip fault system, what type of stepover geometry is required in a right-lateral (dextral) fault?
(a) Left stepover (releasing bend)    (b) Right stepover (releasing bend)    (c) Planar continuous trace    (d) Perpendicular intersection
Answer: (b) Right stepover (releasing bend) — A right step on a right-lateral fault creates local crustal extension and subsidence, forming a rhomboidal pull-apart basin.
8. What is the fundamental boundary condition that distinguishes conservative plate boundaries from convergent and divergent boundaries?
(a) Lithosphere is created
(b) Lithosphere is consumed
(c) Lithospheric surface area is conserved (neither created nor destroyed)
(d) Rocks are converted to diamond
Answer: (c) — Conservative boundaries (transforms) preserve total surface area of oceanic/continental lithosphere.

DGMS 2019 · STRUCTURAL GEOLOGY · CHAPTER MASTERCLASS

Salt Tectonics, Halokinesis & Diapiric Piercement Folds

Authoritative Reference: Ben A. van der Pluijm & Stephen Marshak, Earth Structure (2nd Ed., Chapters 2 & 10: Salt Structures & Special Fold Geometries)
I
Comprehensive Chapter Masterclass: Halokinesis & Diapiric Folding
Rheology of Rock Salt, Gravitational Instability, Piercement Mechanics & Diapiric Folds
1. Physical Properties of Rock Salt & Halokinesis

Halokinesis (salt tectonics) refers to the autonomous, gravity-driven flow and intrusion of rock salt (predominantly halite, $\text{NaCl}$) beneath sedimentary basins. The extraordinary ability of rock salt to flow and intrude overlying rock sequences is governed by three fundamental physical properties (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Chapter 2):

  1. Incompressible Low Density: Halite has a fixed density of $\sim 2.16\text{ g/cm}^3$ that remains nearly constant regardless of burial depth. In contrast, common terrigenous sediments (muds, shales, sands) deposit at lower densities ($1.8\text{–}2.0\text{ g/cm}^3$) but undergo irreversible mechanical compaction, exceeding $2.2\text{–}2.6\text{ g/cm}^3$ below ~1 km of burial. This establishes a profound Rayleigh-Taylor gravitational density inversion where a buoyant, low-density salt layer is trapped beneath a heavier overburden.
  2. Extremely Low Yield Strength: Salt behaves as a non-Newtonian fluid under geologic strain rates, undergoing pervasive crystal-plastic dislocation creep at temperatures as low as 50°C–100°C.
  3. Differential Loading: Tectonic extension, lateral sediment thickness variations, or prograding sedimentary wedges create pressure gradients that trigger buoyant upward ascent of mobile salt.
"Table 2.5 — Terminology of Salt Structures: Salt Diapir: A salt structure that pierces bedding in overlying strata... In a diapir fold (or piercement fold), ductile, incompetent material (such as rock salt, gypsum, or overpressured mud) has broken through the more brittle overlying rock layers and occupied the core of the fold." — Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Earth Structure (2nd Ed., Table 2.5, p. 28)
2. The Piercement Mechanism & Fold Core Occupation

When an anticlinal fold develops above an ascending salt ridge, the ductile core material exerts strong tensile and upward shear forces on the overlying rocks:

  • Concordant Stage (Salt Dome / Pillow): The overlying strata bend upward smoothly in conformity with the salt swell without breaking.
  • Piercement Stage (Diapir Fold / Piercement Fold): The stretching capacity of the brittle roof is exceeded. Faulting and tensile rupture occur, allowing the mobile salt plug to punch through (pierce) the brittle carapace and occupy the central core of the fold. Strata on the flanks are dragged into subvertical, overturned, or faulted attitudes adjacent to the diapir walls.
II
Official Exam Question Statement & In-Depth Critical Analysis
Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) 2019 · Junior Scientific Officer Exam · Question 14
Question Statement
Q.14: In a fold, rock salt has broken through the more brittle overlying rocks and occupied its core. The structure is known as:
(a) Disharmonic fold
(b) Drag fold
(c) Box fold
(d) Diapir fold
✓
Correct Answer: (d) Diapir fold
A fold in which mobile, ductile material (such as rock salt) has actively pierced through the more brittle overlying rock carapace to occupy the core is defined as a diapir fold (or piercement fold).
Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(d) Diapir fold CORRECT
Why it is uniquely correct: In structural geology and salt tectonics (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, 2004, Table 2.5, p. 28; Billings, 1972, p. 101; Davis & Reynolds, 1996), a diapir fold (also known as a piercement fold or diapiric fold, from the Greek diapeirein = to pierce through) is explicitly defined as a fold whose core is composed of mobile, incompetent material (such as rock salt, gypsum, or shale) that has ruptured and broken through the more brittle overlying rock layers.
(a) Disharmonic fold INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A disharmonic fold refers to a layered sequence where adjacent layers exhibit markedly different fold profiles, wavelengths, or amplitudes due to sharp contrasts in rheological competence (e.g., incompetent shale folding disharmonically between competent sandstone beds). In disharmonic folding, layers remain stratigraphically intact and do not pierce or punch through each other.
(b) Drag fold INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A drag fold is a minor, asymmetric parasitic fold formed in incompetent beds adjacent to a fault plane due to frictional dragging of the wall rocks during fault displacement, or by interlayer shear during flexural-slip folding. Drag folds do not involve diapiric piercement of rock salt through a brittle roof.
(c) Box fold INCORRECT
Why it is incorrect: A box fold (conjugate fold) is a profile geometry characterized by two distinct hinge lines and axial planes that converge downward, creating a broad, flat-topped crest and steep limbs. It is a geometric fold shape, not an intrusive piercement structure.
