DGMS 2019 · Question 19 · Masterclass Guide

Magma Series & Geodynamic Environments: The Calc-Alkaline Subduction Hallmark & Petrogenetic Evolution

Comprehensive Igneous Petrology Guide · Authoritative Reference: John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., Ch. 8, 16 & 17)
I
THEORETICAL FOUNDATION: MAGMA SERIES & DISCRIMINATION DIAGRAMS
Alkaline vs. Subalkaline, Tholeiitic vs. Calc-Alkaline, AFM Trends & Subduction Zone Petrogenesis

1. The Concept of Magma Series & The Two Major Splits

A magma series (or association, lineage, clan) is defined as a group of igneous rocks that share distinctive chemical and mineralogical characteristics and exhibit a coherent, continuous evolutionary trend on variation diagrams, reflecting descent from a common parental magma type via systematic differentiation:

The Two Classical Branching Points:
  1. First Split (TAS Diagram — Total Alkalis vs. Silica): Magmas split into Alkaline (silica-undersaturated, high $(\text{Na}_2\text{O} + \text{K}_2\text{O})$) and Subalkaline (silica-saturated to oversaturated). They are separated by the "thermal divide" across the basalt tetrahedron.
  2. Second Split (Subalkaline Division — AFM & Miyashiro Diagrams): Subalkaline magmas further bifurcate into two fundamental evolutionary paths:
    • Tholeiitic Series: Characterized by strong initial iron enrichment ($FeO^*$) at nearly constant $\text{SiO}_2$.
    • Calc-Alkaline Series: Characterized by continuous silica enrichment without iron enrichment.
"First, calc-alkaline magmas are essentially restricted to subduction-related plate tectonic processes. This must carry some genetic significance and will certainly be a factor in any model for subduction zone magma genesis. Recognition of calc-alkaline characteristics in the composition of ancient volcanic rocks may help determine their tectonic framework."
— John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd Ed., 2010), Chapter 8: Major and Minor Elements in Magmas, p. 161.

2. Discrimination Diagrams: AFM and Miyashiro ($FeO^*/MgO$)

The standard triangular AFM diagram ($A = \text{Na}_2\text{O} + \text{K}_2\text{O}$, $F = \text{FeO} + 0.8998\,\text{Fe}_2\text{O}_3$, $M = \text{MgO}$) provides the classic graphical separation between tholeiitic and calc-alkaline magmas:

Figure 1 (Winter Fig. 14, p. 160): AFM diagram showing the contrasting differentiation trends. The solid line divides the upper Tholeiitic field from the lower Calc-Alkaline field (Irvine and Baragar, 1971). Tholeiitic suites (such as the Skaergaard intrusion, Mid-Atlantic Ridge MORB, and Thingmuli volcano, Iceland—solid circles) arch strongly toward the $F$ apex (iron enrichment). Calc-alkaline suites (such as the Cascade volcanic arc and Crater Lake, Oregon—open circles) bypass the $F$ apex, trending directly from the $M$ corner toward the alkali $A$ apex.
The Physical Cause of the Calc-Alkaline Trend: Oxygen Fugacity ($f_{\text{O}_2}$) & Water
Why does the Calc-Alkaline series avoid iron enrichment?

• Tholeiitic Differentiation (Anhydrous / Low $f_{\text{O}_2}$): Under dry, reducing mantle conditions (e.g. MORB), fractional crystallization of olivine ($\text{Mg}_2\text{SiO}_4$) and plagioclase removes $\text{Mg}^{2+}$, $\text{Ca}^{2+}$, and $\text{Al}^{3+}$. Iron ($\text{Fe}^{2+}$) does not enter plagioclase and is rejected into the melt. Magnetite cannot crystallize early because oxygen is low. Thus, the residual liquid undergoes dramatic iron accumulation ($FeO^*/MgO$ spikes).

• Calc-Alkaline Differentiation (Hydrous / High $f_{\text{O}_2}$): In subduction zones, water and oxidizing species ($\text{SO}_4^{2-}, \text{Fe}^{3+}$) released from the down-going slab elevate $P_{\text{H}_2\text{O}}$ and oxygen fugacity in the mantle wedge. High $f_{\text{O}_2}$ forces iron into the ferric state ($\text{Fe}^{3+}$), triggering early precipitation of titanomagnetite ($\text{Fe}_3\text{O}_4$):
$6\,\text{Fe}_2\text{SiO}_4\text{ (Fayalite)} + \text{O}_2 \longrightarrow 4\,\text{Fe}_3\text{O}_4\text{ (Magnetite)} + 6\,\text{SiO}_2$
Early magnetite removal strips iron out of the melt, driving liquid composition directly toward silica enrichment ($\text{SiO}_2 > 56\%$, andesite $\to$ dacite $\to$ rhyolite).
Figure 2 (Miyashiro Diagram): Plot of $FeO^*/MgO$ vs. $\text{SiO}_2$ (Miyashiro, 1974). Tholeiitic magmas show a steep positive slope ($FeO^*/MgO$ increases rapidly with little silica increase). Calc-alkaline magmas show a gentle, flat slope because iron is simultaneously removed by early Fe-Ti oxide fractionation.

3. Petrogenesis in Subduction Zones: Why Subduction is Essential

Winter's subduction zone model demonstrates why calc-alkaline magmatism cannot occur at mid-ocean ridges or intraplate hotspots:

Figure 3 (Winter Cross-Section of Subduction Magmatism): Dehydration of the subducting oceanic plate at $80–120\text{ km}$ depth releases aqueous fluids containing mobile LILE ($\text{Cs, Rb, Ba, Sr, K}$) and boron into the overlying mantle wedge (peridotite). The addition of water dramatically depresses the mantle peridotite solidus (flux melting), generating primary hydrous calc-alkaline picritic and basaltic melts that ascend into the island arc or continental crust.
"Because the calc-alkaline series is restricted mostly to convergent plate boundaries in complex settings, we shall set it aside [when modeling oceanic basalt generation at mid-ocean ridges and intraplate islands]."
— John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology, Chapter 10, p. 191.

4. Cross-Correlation: Magma Series vs. Plate Tectonic Settings

The following matrix compares the four magma series across all major global tectonic regimes:

Magma Series Subduction Zones (Island & Continental Arcs) Divergent Margins (MORB / Mid-Ocean Ridges) Oceanic Intraplate Hotspots (OIB / Hawaii) Continental Intraplate Rifts & Flood Basalts
Calc-Alkaline
★ Found ONLY in Subduction		 DOMINANT & DIAGNOSTIC (Andes, Cascades, Japan, Aleutians). ABSENT (No water/fluxing at ridges). ABSENT (Anhydrous plume melting). ABSENT (Unless contaminated by ancient subduction-modified crust).
Tholeiitic
Option (b)		 Present in immature island arc tholeiites (IAT). DOMINANT (Nearly 100% of normal MORB is tholeiitic). DOMINANT (Huge shield-building lavas of Hawaii, Iceland, Galapagos). DOMINANT (Continental Flood Basalts: Deccan Traps, Karoo, Columbia River).
Alkaline
Option (c)		 Rare / Minor (Late back-arc or post-collisional shoshonites). Very Rare (Only localized off-axis seamounts or early embryonic rifts). COMMON & CHARACTERISTIC (Post-shield rejuvenated stages: alkali basalt, phonolite). COMMON & CHARACTERISTIC (Continental rifts: East African Rift, Oslo Graben).
Komatiitic
Option (d)		 Absent in modern subduction zones. Absent (Requires mantle temperatures $>1600^\circ\text{C}$). Absent in modern plate tectonics (Archean mantle plume origin, $>2.5\text{ Ga}$). Archean greenstone belts (e.g., Barberton, Abitibi, Dharwar).
II
EXAM QUESTION & RIGOROUS OPTION-BY-OPTION DISSECTION
DGMS 2019 Examination · Official Question Statement, Verified Answer & Detailed Distracter Analysis
DGMS 2019 · QUESTION 19
Which one of the following magma series is found only in subduction zones?
(a) Calc-alkaline
(b) Tholeiitic
(c) Alkaline
(d) Komatiite
✓
Correct Option: (a) Calc-alkaline
Verified via John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous & Metamorphic Petrology (Ch. 8, p. 161; Ch. 10, p. 191; Ch. 16 & 17)
Correct Answer Option (a): Calc-alkaline

Detailed Validation: The calc-alkaline magma series is universally recognized in igneous petrology as being uniquely and exclusively generated at subduction-related convergent plate margins (island arcs and active continental margins, such as the Japanese Arc, Aleutians, and the Andes). The genesis of calc-alkaline magmas fundamentally requires high volatile ($H_2O$) content and elevated oxygen fugacity ($f_{O_2}$), which are provided solely by dehydration fluids expelled from the subducting lithospheric slab into the overlying mantle wedge. Under these conditions, early crystallization of Fe-Ti oxides (titanomagnetite) prevents iron enrichment and drives liquid differentiation toward silica-rich andesites, dacites, and rhyolites. Calc-alkaline magmatism does not occur at mid-ocean ridges, intraplate oceanic hotspots, or continental flood basalt provinces.

Incorrect Option (b): Tholeiitic

Detailed Refutation: The tholeiitic series is the most widespread and ubiquitous magma type on Earth. Far from being restricted to subduction zones, tholeiitic basalt constitutes virtually $100\%$ of Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalts (MORB—the largest magmatic province on the planet at divergent margins), dominates oceanic island shield volcanoes (e.g., Mauna Loa and Kilauea in Hawaii, Iceland), and forms massive Continental Flood Basalt provinces (e.g., Deccan Traps, Columbia River Basalts). Because tholeiites occur in nearly every tectonic environment, they cannot be diagnostic of subduction zones.

Incorrect Option (c): Alkaline

Detailed Refutation: The alkaline magma series is characteristically associated with intraplate tectonic settings, specifically continental rifts (e.g., the East African Rift System, Rio Grande Rift) and late-stage oceanic island hotspots (e.g., the post-shield and rejuvenated stages of Hawaii, Tahiti, Tristan da Cunha). Alkaline magmas are generated by low degrees of partial melting of deep, garnet-bearing asthenospheric mantle in intraplate regimes, not in subduction zones.

Incorrect Option (d): Komatiite

Detailed Refutation: Komatiites are ultra-magnesian ($>18\text{ wt}\% MgO$), high-temperature ($>1600^\circ\text{C}$) volcanic rocks displaying spinifex textures. They were generated almost exclusively during the Archean Eon ($>2.5\text{ Ga}$) by exceptionally high-degree ($>40\%$) partial melting of deep mantle plumes. Komatiites are not a feature of modern plate tectonics or subduction zones.

III
SYNTHESIS & HIGH-YIELD PRACTICE QUESTIONS
Curated Problems Covering AFM Projections, Miyashiro Plots, Slab Fluids & Arc Petrochemistry

High-Yield Exam Practice Questions

Practice Q1 (Differentiation Trends): AFM Path Divergence

On an AFM ternary diagram, why does the Skaergaard intrusion liquid trend curve sharply upward toward the $F$ apex, whereas the Crater Lake (Cascades) liquid trend moves straight toward the $A$ apex?

Solution & Rationale:
Skaergaard is an anhydrous, reducing tholeiitic system where plagioclase + olivine fractional crystallization leaves iron to concentrate in the residual melt (Fenner trend of iron enrichment). Crater Lake is a hydrous, oxidized calc-alkaline arc magma where elevated $f_{\text{O}_2}$ triggers early crystallization of titanomagnetite ($\text{Fe}_3\text{O}_4$), immediately removing iron and steering the remaining melt straight toward silica and alkalis ($\text{Na}_2\text{O} + \text{K}_2\text{O}$).
Practice Q2 (Subduction Geochemistry): The Tonalite-Trondhjemite-Granodiorite (TTG) Suite

In Archean subduction zones, what calc-alkaline plutonic suite formed the primary continental crust, and what mineral residue was required in the slab source?

Answer: The TTG suite (Tonalite–Trondhjemite–Granodiorite). It was formed by partial melting of hot subducting oceanic crust in the garnet amphibolite / eclogite facies, leaving a garnet + rutile residue that caused heavy REE depletion and high $\text{Sr/Y}$ ratios.
Practice Q3 (Miyashiro Discriminant): Slope Significance

In Miyashiro's (1974) plot of $FeO^*/MgO$ versus $\text{SiO}_2$, what mathematical criterion separates tholeiitic suites from calc-alkaline suites?

Answer: The slope of the differentiation trend ($\Delta(FeO^*/MgO) / \Delta\text{SiO}_2$). Tholeiitic suites show a steep slope ($> 0.1$), indicating rapid increase in iron relative to magnesium with minimal silica change. Calc-alkaline suites have a shallow slope ($< 0.1$), indicating that silica increases rapidly as magnesium decreases, without an iron spike.
Practice Q4 (Subduction Volatiles): Slab Dehydration Mechanism

Which metamorphic mineral reactions in the down-going oceanic slab are primarily responsible for releasing the water that fluxes the mantle wedge at $100\text{ km}$ depth?

Answer: The breakdown of hydrous phyllosilicates and amphiboles during the transition from blueschist to eclogite facies—specifically the dehydration of serpentine, chlorite, lawsonite, and glaucophane into anhydrous garnet + omphacite + quartz.
Practice Q5 (Trace Element Signature): Arc Spidergrams

What is the most diagnostic geochemical trace element fingerprint of calc-alkaline subduction zone magmas on a primitive-mantle-normalized spidergram?

Answer: Enrichment in Large-Ion Lithophile Elements (LILE: $\text{Cs, Rb, Ba, Sr, Pb}$) coupled with pronounced negative anomalies in High Field Strength Elements ($\text{Nb, Ta, Ti}$). This "subduction signature" reflects the mobility of LILE in slab-derived aqueous fluids and the retention of $\text{Nb-Ta-Ti}$ in residual slab rutile.
Practice Q6 (Petrography): Dominant Rock Types

What volcanic rock type is volumetrically dominant in calc-alkaline continental volcanic arcs (e.g., the Andes), contrasting with tholeiitic mid-ocean ridges where basalt dominates?

Answer: Andesite ($\text{SiO}_2 \approx 57–63\text{ wt}\%$), accompanied by dacite and rhyolite. The worldwide abundance of andesite at convergent margins gave the "Andes" its name and defines the "Andesite Line" around the Pacific Rim.
Practice Q7 (Thermal Divide): Alkaline vs. Subalkaline Immutability

Why can a subalkaline magma (tholeiitic or calc-alkaline) never differentiate into an alkaline magma (e.g., phonolite) during low-pressure fractional crystallization?

Answer: Because of the presence of the thermal divide (the plane of silica saturation: Forsterite–Diopside–Albite) in the basalt tetrahedron. Magmas on the silica-saturated side of the divide evolve toward the silica-oversaturated minimum (quartz-normative rhyolite), while magmas on the undersaturated side evolve toward the feldspathoid minimum (nepheline-normative phonolite). Crystallization cannot cross a thermal divide.
Practice Q8 (Shoshonitic Series): Arc Evolution

In mature island arcs and post-collisional settings, an ultra-potassic magma series often appears behind the volcanic front. What is this series called?

Answer: The Shoshonitic Series (or high-K calc-alkaline to potassic alkaline series). It represents magmas derived from deep, metasomatized mantle lithosphere enriched in potassium and LILE during waning or terminated subduction.

Comprehensive Magma Series Summary Matrix

Magma Series Parental Magma Differentiation Trend Volatiles & Redox State Tectonic Setting
Calc-Alkaline (The Answer) Hydrous High-Alumina Basalt Silica enrichment without Fe enrichment (Andesite $\to$ Dacite $\to$ Rhyolite) Hydrous ($>2–6\text{ wt}\% \text{H}_2\text{O}$); High $f_{\text{O}_2}$ (NNO to HM buffer) Exclusively Subduction Zones (Island Arcs & Active Continental Margins)
Tholeiitic Olivine Tholeiite Basalt Iron enrichment (Fenner trend) followed by silica enrichment Anhydrous ($<0.5\text{ wt}\% \text{H}_2\text{O}$); Low $f_{\text{O}_2}$ (QFM buffer) Mid-Ocean Ridges (MORB), Ocean Islands (OIB), Continental Flood Basalts, Back-Arcs
Alkaline Alkali Olivine Basalt / Basanite Silica-undersaturated trend (Trachybasalt $\to$ Trachyte $\to$ Phonolite) Variable, enriched in $\text{CO}_2$ and alkalis Intraplate Hotspots (Hawaii, Tahiti), Continental Rifts (East African Rift)
Komatiitic Peridotitic / Ultramafic Komatiite Olivine fractionation from ultra-high-Mg liquids ($>18\text{ wt}\% \text{MgO}$) Anhydrous, extremely high temperature ($>1600^\circ\text{C}$) Archean Greenstone Belts (Archean Mantle Plumes, $>2.5\text{ Ga}$)
Isotope Geochemistry / Question 20
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · Question 20 · Masterclass Guide

Isotope Geochemistry: The Rb-Sr Geochronometer, Isochrons & Planetary Reservoir Evolution

Comprehensive Geochemistry Guide · Authoritative References: K. C. Misra (2012, Ch. 10) & William M. White (2005, Ch. 8)
I
THEORETICAL FOUNDATION: THE Rb-Sr SYSTEM & CRUSTAL RESERVOIRS
Radioactive Decay, The Isochron Equation, Planetary Differentiation & The Continental Crust Rb/Sr Ratio

1. The Rb-Sr Decay Scheme & Isochron Equation

Rubidium ($\text{Rb}$) is an alkali metal (Group 1) that substitutes for $\text{K}^+$ in minerals like biotite, muscovite, and K-feldspar. Strontium ($\text{Sr}$) is an alkaline earth metal (Group 2) that substitutes for $\text{Ca}^{2+}$ in plagioclase, apatite, and calcite. The isotope $^{87}\text{Rb}$ decays to stable radiogenic $^{87}\text{Sr}$ via negative $\beta^-$ decay:

The Rb-Sr Radioactive Decay & Isochron Formulation
$^{87}_{37}\text{Rb} \longrightarrow {}^{87}_{38}\text{Sr} + \beta^- + \bar{\nu} + Q$

• Decay constant: $\lambda_{\text{Rb}} = 1.42 \times 10^{-11}\text{ yr}^{-1}$
• Half-life: $t_{1/2} = \frac{\ln(2)}{\lambda} = 48.8 \times 10^9\text{ years}$ ($48.8\text{ Ga}$)

Normalizing to non-radiogenic, stable reference isotope $^{86}\text{Sr}$:
$\left(\frac{^{87}\text{Sr}}{^{86}\text{Sr}}\right)_{\text{present}} = \left(\frac{^{87}\text{Sr}}{^{86}\text{Sr}}\right)_{\text{initial}} + \left(\frac{^{87}\text{Rb}}{^{86}\text{Sr}}\right) \cdot (e^{\lambda t} - 1)$

Since $\lambda t \ll 1$, using Taylor series approximation $e^{\lambda t} - 1 \approx \lambda t$:
$y = c + m \cdot x \implies \left(\frac{^{87}\text{Sr}}{^{86}\text{Sr}}\right) = \left(\frac{^{87}\text{Sr}}{^{86}\text{Sr}}\right)_0 + \lambda t \cdot \left(\frac{^{87}\text{Rb}}{^{86}\text{Sr}}\right)$

On an isochron diagram ($^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ vs. $^{87}\text{Rb}/^{86}\text{Sr}$), cogenetic samples define a straight line whose slope yields the geological age ($t$) and whose y-intercept defines the initial strontium ratio ($(^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr})_0$).

2. Textbook Evidence: Isochrons & Crust-Mantle Divergence

Figure 10.14 from Misra models the disparate evolution of strontium isotopes over Earth's history, driven by geochemical differentiation between the mantle and continental crust:

Figure 1 (Misra Fig. 10.14, p. 246): Evolution of terrestrial $^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ over geologic time. Line ABD represents closed-system evolution of the bulk mantle (with average $\text{Rb/Sr} = 0.027$) from the initial BABI ratio ($0.69899$) to present-day ($0.702–0.704$). Line BC represents the evolution of a batch of continental crust extracted from the mantle at $2.9\text{ Ga}$, having a markedly higher $\text{Rb/Sr} \approx 0.15–0.16$ (closest exam value: 0.158). Line BE represents the resulting depleted mantle residue.
"It can be calculated that to produce a ratio of 0.704 from the BABI value of 0.699 over 4.55 billion years (the Earth's age) would require an average Rb/Sr ratio of 0.027. The Rb/Sr ratio of the mantle must have decreased as a function of time because of preferential enrichment of Rb in the partial melts extracted from the mantle... In contrast, the extracted continental crust has a substantially higher Rb/Sr ratio (~0.15–0.16)."
— Kula C. Misra, Introduction to Geochemistry: Principles and Applications (2012), Chapter 10: Radiogenic Isotopes, p. 246.

3. Why Continental Crust is Enriched in Rb/Sr

The stark difference in $\text{Rb/Sr}$ ratios between the mantle ($0.027$) and the continental crust ($0.158$) is a direct thermodynamic consequence of their trace element incompatibility during mantle partial melting:

Partitioning Mechanics during Magma Generation:
  • Rubidium ($\text{Rb}^+$): Large ionic radius ($1.52\text{ \AA}$) prevents it from entering mantle olivine and pyroxenes. It is highly incompatible ($D_{\text{bulk}} \approx 0.001–0.01$). When the mantle partially melts, $\text{Rb}$ enters the melt almost entirely.
  • Strontium ($\text{Sr}^{2+}$): Smaller ionic radius ($1.18\text{ \AA}$) allows it to substitute readily for $\text{Ca}^{2+}$ in clinopyroxene and especially plagioclase. It is moderately incompatible ($D_{\text{bulk}} \approx 0.1–0.2$).
  • Resulting Fractionation: Because $\text{Rb}$ is far more incompatible than $\text{Sr}$ ($D_{\text{Rb}} \ll D_{\text{Sr}}$), every partial melting event extracts $\text{Rb}$ into the magma far more efficiently than $\text{Sr}$. As continental crust accumulates from mantle melts, it becomes heavily enriched in $\text{Rb}$ relative to $\text{Sr}$, raising its bulk $\text{Rb/Sr}$ ratio from $0.027$ up to 0.158!

4. Terrestrial and Cosmochemical Reference Reservoirs

Geochemists use specific geochemical benchmarks to interpret isotopic data across planetary reservoirs:

Geochemical Reservoir Representative $\text{Rb/Sr}$ Ratio Initial or Present $^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ Significance & Geochemical Meaning
BABI (Basaltic Achondrite Best Initial) $\sim 0.0001$ $0.69899 \pm 0.00005$ Primordial solar system ratio at $4.55\text{ Ga}$, measured from pristine achondrite meteorites.
Bulk Silicate Earth / Mantle (BSE / UR) $0.027–0.030$ $0.7025–0.7045$ Undifferentiated primitive mantle before continental crust extraction.
Depleted Mantle (DM / MORB Source) $< 0.010$ ($0.005$) $0.7022–0.7030$ Mantle residue that has experienced ancient melt extraction. Low Rb/Sr prevents radiogenic $^{87}\text{Sr}$ growth.
Average Continental Crust
★ Correct Answer		 0.158 (Rudnick & Gao, 2003; Taylor & McLennan, 1985; Misra, p. 246) $0.710–0.725+$ Bulk continental crust (upper + middle + lower). Elevated $\text{Rb/Sr}$ drives rapid radiogenic growth of $^{87}\text{Sr}$.
Upper Continental Crust $\sim 0.32–0.38$ $> 0.720$ Highly evolved granites, shales, and sediments. Extremely high Rb/Sr.
II
EXAM QUESTION & RIGOROUS OPTION-BY-OPTION DISSECTION
DGMS 2019 Examination · Official Question Statement, Verified Answer & Detailed Distracter Analysis
DGMS 2019 · QUESTION 20
Average continental crust reservoir is represented by which one of the following Rb/Sr values of radiogenic parent-daughter pairs?
(a) 0.124
(b) 0.158
(c) 0.182
(d) 0.242
✓
Correct Option: (b) 0.158
Verified via K. C. Misra (Introduction to Geochemistry, p. 246) & Rudnick & Gao (Treatise on Geochemistry, 2003)
Incorrect Option (a): 0.124

Detailed Refutation: The value $0.124$ underestimates the average rubidium-to-strontium ratio of the bulk continental crust. While some mafic granulitic lower-crustal models estimate lower local values ($\sim 0.05–0.10$), the internationally standardized global average for the entire continental crust (integrating upper, middle, and lower crust) is substantially higher ($0.158$).

Correct Answer Option (b): 0.158

Detailed Validation: 0.158 is the internationally recognized benchmark value for the $\text{Rb/Sr}$ weight ratio of the average bulk continental crust. In classic crustal compositions established by Taylor & McLennan (1985, 1995) and revised in the authoritative Treatise on Geochemistry (Rudnick & Gao, 2003, Table 1), the bulk continental crust contains approximately $49\text{ ppm Rb}$ and $320\text{ ppm Sr}$, yielding $\text{Rb/Sr} = 49 / 320 \approx \mathbf{0.153–0.158}$ (modeled as $\sim 0.15–0.16$ in Misra, Fig. 10.14). This value reflects the integrated enrichment of incompatible Rb during multi-stage crustal extraction from the mantle.

Incorrect Option (c): 0.182

Detailed Refutation: The value $0.182$ is too high for the average bulk continental crust; it falls in the intermediate transition zone between the bulk crust ($0.158$) and the felsic upper continental crust ($>0.32$). It does not correspond to any accepted global planetary reservoir benchmark.

Incorrect Option (d): 0.242

Detailed Refutation: The value $0.242$ is significantly too radiogenic for the bulk continental crust, approaching the value of the highly differentiated upper continental crust or post-Archean Australian shale (PAAS). The presence of the mafic, restitic lower crust reduces the whole-crust average to $0.158$.

III
SYNTHESIS & HIGH-YIELD PRACTICE QUESTIONS
Curated Problems Covering Isochrons, Crustal Contamination, BABI & Geochronology

High-Yield Exam Practice Questions

Practice Q1 (Initial Ratios): Detecting Crustal Contamination

A basaltic lava flow erupts through continental lithosphere. Mass spectrometry reveals an initial strontium isotope ratio of $(^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr})_0 = 0.7125$. Does this basalt represent a pure, uncontaminated mantle melt?

Solution & Rationale:
No. Modern uncontaminated mantle-derived melts (MORB and pristine OIB) possess initial $^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ ratios strictly between $0.7020$ and $0.7045$. An initial ratio $> 0.706$ (and here $0.7125$) is definitive evidence that the ascending magma assimilated ancient continental crust, which has had billions of years to accumulate radiogenic $^{87}\text{Sr}$ from its elevated $\text{Rb/Sr}$ ratio.
Practice Q2 (Isochron Mechanics): Slope and Age

A suite of granitic whole-rock samples defines an isochron on a plot of $^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ vs. $^{87}\text{Rb}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ with a slope of $m = 0.0355$. Given $\lambda_{\text{Rb}} = 1.42 \times 10^{-11}\text{ yr}^{-1}$, calculate the crystallization age of the granite.

Solution & Rationale:
$\text{Slope } m = e^{\lambda t} - 1 \approx \lambda t$.
$t = \frac{m}{\lambda} = \frac{0.0355}{1.42 \times 10^{-11}\text{ yr}^{-1}} = 2.50 \times 10^9\text{ years} = \mathbf{2.50\text{ Ga}}$ (Neoarchean).
Practice Q3 (Mantle Depletion): Why DM has Low Rb/Sr

Why does the Depleted Mantle (the source reservoir for N-MORB) have a lower $\text{Rb/Sr}$ ratio ($< 0.010$) than the Bulk Silicate Earth ($0.027$)?

Answer: Over geologic time, repeated episodes of partial melting extracted incompatible elements to build the continental crust. Because $\text{Rb}$ is much more incompatible than $\text{Sr}$ ($D_{\text{Rb}} \ll D_{\text{Sr}}$), $\text{Rb}$ was extracted into the crust with much higher efficiency, leaving the residual mantle progressively starved of rubidium relative to strontium.
Practice Q4 (BABI Concept): Solar System Primordial Reference

What is BABI, what is its numerical value, and from which specific meteorite class was it determined?

Answer: Basaltic Achondrite Best Initial (BABI) is the primordial $^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ ratio of the early Solar System ($4.55\text{ Ga}$), established by Papanastassiou & Wasserburg (1969) as $0.69899 \pm 0.00005$ ($\approx 0.699$) from basaltic achondrite meteorites (eucrites).
Practice Q5 (Mineral Isochrons vs. Whole-Rock): Metamorphic Resetting

During a high-grade metamorphic event, which isochron is reset to give the age of metamorphism: a whole-rock isochron or a mineral isochron (biotite-feldspar-apatite)?

Answer: Mineral isochron. At metamorphic temperatures ($>300–500^\circ\text{C}$), strontium diffuses rapidly across mineral grain boundaries, re-homogenizing the $^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ ratio among coexisting minerals and resetting the clock. On a hand-specimen scale (tens of centimeters), the whole rock remains a closed system, so whole-rock isochrons preserve the original igneous crystallization age.
Practice Q6 (Opposite Geochemical Behavior): Rb-Sr vs. Sm-Nd

Why do geochemists frequently pair the $\text{Rb-Sr}$ isotope system with the $\text{Sm-Nd}$ isotope system when studying mantle and crustal evolution?

Answer: Because the two systems have exactly opposite parent-daughter partitioning behavior during crust extraction: In $\text{Rb-Sr}$, the parent ($\text{Rb}$) is more incompatible than the daughter ($\text{Sr}$), giving the crust a high $\text{Rb/Sr}$ and high radiogenic $^{87}\text{Sr}/^{86}\text{Sr}$. In $\text{Sm-Nd}$, the daughter ($\text{Nd}$) is more incompatible than the parent ($\text{Sm}$), giving the crust a low $\text{Sm/Nd}$ and low radiogenic $^{143}\text{Nd}/^{144}\text{Nd}$!
Practice Q7 (Feldspar Partitioning): K-Feldspar vs. Plagioclase

In a granitic rock, which mineral will have the highest $^{87}\text{Rb}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ ratio, and which will have the lowest?

Answer: Biotite and K-feldspar have the highest $^{87}\text{Rb}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ (abundant $\text{K}^+$ hosting $\text{Rb}$, virtually no $\text{Ca}^{2+}$). Plagioclase and apatite have the lowest $^{87}\text{Rb}/^{86}\text{Sr}$ (rich in $\text{Ca}^{2+}$ hosting $\text{Sr}$, but lacking $\text{K}^+$).
Practice Q8 (Analytical Challenges): Volatile Mobility

Why is the Rb-Sr system more vulnerable to secondary weathering and hydrothermal disturbance than the Sm-Nd or U-Pb systems?

Answer: Both $\text{Rb}^+$ (alkali) and $\text{Sr}^{2+}$ (alkaline earth) are large, low-valence ions that dissolve readily in low-temperature aqueous fluids (they are fluid-mobile LILE). In contrast, $\text{Sm}, \text{Nd}, \text{U}$, and $\text{Pb}$ are far less soluble and more tightly bound in resistant accessory minerals like zircon and monazite.

Comprehensive Reservoir Geochemical Summary

Reservoir Parameter Bulk Silicate Earth (BSE) Depleted Mantle (DM) Bulk Continental Crust (The Answer) Upper Continental Crust
