Igneous Petrology & Geochemistry / Question 25
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 · Igneous Petrology & Geochemistry · Chapter Masterclass

Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalts (MORB): Petrogenesis, Mantle Reservoirs & Incompatible Trace Element Systematics

Comprehensive Textbook Study Guide & Complete Worked Solution for DGMS 2019 Exam (Question 25)
Grounded in John D. Winter (2010), William M. White (2005/2013), Kula C. Misra (2012), and Hugh Rollinson (1993)
📌 Executive Summary & Key Geochemical Takeaways

Question 25 examines the foundational petrogenetic classification of Mid-Oceanic Ridge Basalts (MORB) and the chemical heterogeneity of the sub-oceanic upper mantle. The global ocean ridge system produces the largest volume of magma on Earth (~21 km³/yr), yet these basalts are not chemically uniform:

  • N-MORB ("Normal" MORB): Derived from a previously depleted, incompatible element-poor mantle reservoir known as DMM (Depleted MORB Mantle). Because the continental crust extracted incompatible elements over geologic time, the residue is depleted in light rare earth elements (LREE), Cs, Rb, Ba, Th, U, and K, yielding characteristically low (La/Sm)N < 1.0 (positive REE slope) and low K2O < 0.10 wt.%.
  • E-MORB / P-MORB ("Enriched" or "Plume" MORB): Taps an incompatible element-richer mantle associated with deep mantle plumes or enriched, fertile mantle domains (e.g., Iceland, Azores, Galapagos), yielding (La/Sm)N > 1.8 (negative REE slope) and high K2O > 0.10 wt.%.
  • T-MORB ("Transitional" MORB): Represents intermediate or hybrid compositions ((La/Sm)N ≈ 0.8–1.8) generated by mixing between N-MORB and E-MORB parental magmas during ascent and shallow ridge magma chamber processing.
  • VAB (Volcanic Arc Basalts): Formed exclusively at subduction zones (convergent plate margins) via hydrous slab-fluid flux melting; not a mid-ocean ridge basalt at all. Characterized by strong LILE enrichment and prominent HFSE depletion (marked negative Nb–Ta anomaly).
Definitive Answer: (a) N-MORB is derived from an incompatible element-poor (depleted) mantle.
PART I
Theoretical Foundation & Direct Textbook Analysis
Comprehensive exploration of MORB magmatism, trace element fractionation, mantle reservoir isotopic taxonomy, and decompression melting mechanics
1. Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalts (MORB): Tectonic Framework & Primary vs. Derivative Melts

Mid-ocean ridges represent the divergent boundaries where oceanic lithosphere is created by the upwelling and partial melting of the underlying asthenospheric mantle. Oceanic crust is structured into a classic stratified sequence (recognized on land as ophiolites): Layer 1 (pelagic sediment), Layer 2A (pillow basalts), Layer 2B (sheeted dolerite dikes), Layer 3A (isotropic and foliated gabbros), and Layer 3B (layered gabbroic cumulates), underlain by residual mantle peridotite (harzburgite and depleted lherzolite).

"The great quantity and chemical uniformity of MORBs led early workers to conclude they were all primary melts delivered directly to the surface. As discussed above, the vast majority of, if not all, MORBs are now believed to be derivative liquids that have undergone at least some degree of fractional crystallization and magma mixing, as first proposed by O'Hara (1968a, 1977, 1982). Because the composition of most MORBs approximate a low-pressure cotectic for olivine + plagioclase + clinopyroxene + liquid, the fractional crystallization must have taken place near the surface, probably in shallow magma chambers. Primary magmas must be in equilibrium with mantle phases, and therefore must have Mg# around 70... Thus few, if any, of the MORB liquids that reach the surface are primary." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology (2nd ed.), Chapter 13, p. 299–301

Because near-surface fractional crystallization modifies the concentrations of major oxides (reducing MgO, raising FeO, and concentrating incompatible elements), petrologists evaluate mantle source variations by comparing primitive samples with equal Mg# [where Mg# = 100 × Mg / (Mg + Fe²⁺)] or by using trace element ratios and radiogenic isotopes that do not fractionate during crystal removal.

2. Chemical Heterogeneity of MORB: N-MORB, E-MORB (P-MORB), and T-MORB

When primitive MORBs with similar Mg# are compared along ridge segments, substantial differences in incompatible elements (K, Ti, P, LREE) become immediately apparent. This proves that the sub-oceanic mantle is not a single, homogeneous reservoir:

"Figure 10 shows the variation in K₂O with Mg# for the MAR data set of Schilling et al. (1983). Note the tremendous variation in K₂O, even for constant Mg# values between 65 and 70, indicating considerable variation in MORB parental liquids. Similar variations independent of Mg# are found for TiO₂ and P₂O₅... Because K₂O, TiO₂, and P₂O₅ are incompatible elements, their concentration is unlikely to be affected greatly by fractional crystallization processes after separation from the mantle. The concentrations of these elements are more likely to reflect characteristics inherited from the mantle source. This suggests, although subtly, that there is an incompatible-rich and an incompatible-poor source region (at least) for MORB magmas in the mantle beneath the ridges... We can thus distinguish between N-MORB ('normal' MORB), which taps the depleted, or incompatible-poor mantle, and E-MORB ('enriched' MORB, also called P-MORB for 'plume'), which taps an incompatible-richer mantle. N-MORBs with Mg# > 65 have K₂O < 0.10, TiO₂ < 1.0, whereas E-MORBs have K₂O > 0.10 and TiO₂ > 1.0 for the same Mg#." — John D. Winter, Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology, Chapter 13, p. 300
Winter Figure 13-10: Variation in K₂O versus Mg# for Mid-Atlantic Ridge basalts (Schilling et al., 1983). For any given primitive Mg# (e.g., 65–70), K₂O varies from <0.05 wt.% (incompatible-depleted N-MORB) to >1.5 wt.% (incompatible-enriched E-MORB/P-MORB associated with plume plateaus like Iceland and the Azores).

Rare Earth Element (REE) Patterns: Distinguishing N-MORB from E-MORB and T-MORB

The Rare Earth Elements (REEs, Lanthanides: La to Lu) exhibit progressive decreases in ionic radii from La³⁺ (largest, most incompatible) to Lu³⁺ (smallest, least incompatible). Therefore, chondrite-normalized REE diagrams provide an unmistakable fingerprint of mantle source depletion:

  • N-MORB: Exhibits a positive slope in the Light REE (LREE) segment. La is more incompatible than Sm, so prior melt extraction depletes La much more severely than Sm, resulting in (La/Sm)N < 1.0 (typically < 0.7) and depleted absolute LREE concentrations (~2–4× chondrite).
  • E-MORB (P-MORB): Exhibits a negative slope in the LREE segment. The source is fertile or enriched, so incompatible LREEs are elevated relative to HREEs, resulting in (La/Sm)N > 1.8 (up to 5×) and LREE concentrations (~50–80× chondrite).
  • T-MORB (Transitional MORB): Possesses intermediate slopes with (La/Sm)N between 0.8 and 1.8. Geochemical modeling shows T-MORBs do not represent a third mantle layer, but rather the continuous hybrid mixing between N-MORB and E-MORB melts.
Winter Figure 13-11: Chondrite-normalized REE patterns for Mid-Atlantic Ridge basalts (Schilling et al., 1983). Solid triangles represent N-MORBs derived from the depleted (incompatible-poor) mantle with a positive slope (depleted in LREE). Open squares represent E-MORBs derived from an enriched mantle with a negative slope (enriched in LREE). Notice the identical Heavy REE (HREE) levels, demonstrating that source differences reside in the highly incompatible elements.
Winter Figure 13-12: La/Sm ratio versus Mg# for Mid-Atlantic Ridge MORBs (Schilling et al., 1983). Solid triangles = N-MORB ((La/Sm)N < 1.0); Open squares = E-MORB ((La/Sm)N > 1.8); Gray circles = T-MORB (Transitional MORB, 1.0 < La/Sm < 1.8) forming a continuous hybrid mixing spectrum.
3. Incompatible Trace Element Multi-Element Diagrams & The Origin of the Depleted Mantle

When trace elements are arranged on an extended rare earth diagram (spidergram) in order of increasing compatibility during mantle melting (from Cs, Rb, Ba, Th, U on the far left to Y, Yb, Lu on the far right), the incompatible element depletion of MORB is remarkably clear.

"Figure 11.18 compares the incompatible element abundances in MORB and typical OIB alkali basalt in a spider diagram, in which elements are ordered according to their incompatibility and normalized to primitive mantle values... The incompatible element depletion of MORB is apparent. OIB are generally incompatible-element enriched... In the view of most mantle geochemists and geophysicists, MORB are derived from the asthenosphere, which is that part of the upper mantle that directly underlies the lithosphere and is characterized by relatively low seismic velocities... Oceanic island basalts are thought to be derived from mantle plumes that rise from a thermal boundary layer at either the 660 km discontinuity or the core-mantle boundary." — William M. White, Geochemistry, Chapter 11 (The Mantle and Core), p. 491
White Figure 11.18: Primitive mantle-normalized incompatible trace element spider diagram for Average MORB tholeiite compared with typical OIB alkali basalt (Sun, 1980). Note the profound depletion of MORB in the most incompatible elements (Cs, Rb, Ba, Th, U, Nb, Ta, K) dropping to near or below 1× primitive mantle, in stark contrast to OIB which reaches 50–150× primitive mantle.

The Hofmann (1988) Complementary Crust–Mantle Paradigm

Why is the upper mantle depleted in incompatible elements? The definitive geochemical answer was articulated by Albrecht Hofmann (1988): the Depleted MORB Mantle (DMM) is the complementary residue left behind by the extraction of the Earth's continental crust.

"Hofmann (1988) argued that the compositional relationship between continental crust and oceanic crust could be explained by a simple two-stage model of first extracting continental crust from the primitive mantle, and then extracting oceanic crust by remelting of the residual (depleted) mantle... Fig. 12.15 shows complementary patterns exhibited by concentrations of elements in the bulk continental crust and 'normal-type' mid-ocean ridge basalts (N-MORB), both normalized to the estimated values for the primitive mantle (Sun and McDonough, 1989). The continental crust is enriched in incompatible elements... whereas N-MORB is depleted in these elements." — Kula C. Misra, Introduction to Geochemistry: Principles and Applications, Chapter 12, p. 308
Misra Figure 12.15: Complementary trace element patterns of bulk continental crust (open circles) and N-MORB (solid circles), normalized to Primitive Mantle (Hofmann, 1988; Sun & McDonough, 1989). Incompatible elements strongly partitioned into the continental crust (yielding enrichment factors of 30–80×) leave the residual upper mantle severely depleted, directly explaining why N-MORB has normalized ratios < 1 for Rb, Ba, and Th.
White Figure 11.21: The Three-Reservoir Model of the Earth's mantle and crust (DePaolo, 1980; White, 2005). The extraction of the continental crust (enriched in incompatible elements) leaves the Depleted Mantle (source of MORB, characterized by εNd = +10 and low ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr), underlain by an undepleted Primitive Mantle or deep plume source.
4. Radiogenic Isotope Fingerprinting of Mantle Reservoirs (DMM, BSE, HIMU, EMI, EMII)

Because radiogenic isotopic ratios (e.g., ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr, ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd) do not change during partial melting or fractional crystallization, they reflect the time-integrated parent/daughter ratio of the mantle source over hundreds of millions of years:

  • Rb–Sr System: Rb is more incompatible than Sr (DRb < DSr). Extraction of continental crust depleted the upper mantle in Rb, leaving a low Rb/Sr ratio. Over 2–3 billion years, little radiogenic ⁸⁷Sr grew, so DMM has very low ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr (< 0.7025).
  • Sm–Nd System: Nd is more incompatible than Sm (DNd < DSm). Continental crust extraction extracted more Nd than Sm, leaving the residual upper mantle with an elevated Sm/Nd ratio. Decay of ¹⁴⁷Sm to ¹⁴³Nd produced high radiogenic ¹⁴³Nd, so DMM has high ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd (> 0.5131) and positive εNd (+10 to +12).
Winter Figure 13-13: ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd versus ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr for Atlantic and Pacific N-MORBs compared with E-MORBs (Winter, 2010; Ito et al., 1987). N-MORB forms a tight cluster in the upper-left quadrant (high ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd > 0.5130, low ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr < 0.7032), characteristic of long-term incompatible element depletion. E-MORBs extend downward and rightward toward Bulk Earth (BSE) and enriched mantle components.
Winter Table 14-5: Approximate isotopic ratios of principal mantle reservoirs (Rollinson, 1993; Winter, 2010). Note that DM / DMM (Depleted Mantle) exhibits the lowest ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr (0.7015–0.7025) and highest ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd (0.5133–0.5136), defining the source of N-MORB.
5. Decompression Melting Mechanics & Plume–Ridge Interaction

Melting beneath mid-ocean ridges is driven by adiabatic decompression of upwelling mantle peridotite (lherzolite/harzburgite). Because the dry peridotite solidus has a steeper dP/dT slope than the mantle adiabat (~0.3°C/km), upward flow causes the mantle to cross its solidus at depths of ~60–80 km (P ≈ 1.5–2.5 GPa):

Winter Figure 13-14: Schematic model for the rise and decompression melting of different mantle source regions beneath mid-ocean ridges (after Zindler et al., 1984; Wilson, 1989; Winter, 2010). The shallow, convecting upper mantle above 660 km consists of Depleted Mantle (source of N-MORB). Deep mantle plumes ascending from the lower mantle tap Enriched Mantle (source of E-MORB/P-MORB). Where plumes interact with spreading ridges (e.g., Iceland on MAR), melt mixing produces T-MORB.

Melting proceeds by incremental polybaric near-fractional melting, where small melt fractions (~1%) segregate continuously as the mantle rises through a triangular melting regime (Langmuir et al., 1992), focusing into the axial magma chamber to form the oceanic crust.

6. Distinct Petrogenetic Setting of Volcanic Arc Basalts (VAB)

Option (d) in the question refers to VAB (Volcanic Arc Basalts). It is essential to recognize that VABs are completely distinct from Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalts in tectonic regime, melting mechanism, and geochemical signature:

  • Tectonic Setting: VAB erupts at convergent plate boundaries (island arcs and active continental margins above subduction zones), whereas MORB erupts at divergent spreading ridges.
  • Melting Trigger: MORB is generated by dry adiabatic decompression melting. VAB is generated by hydrous flux melting: aqueous fluids released by the dehydration of subducting serpentinite, altered basalts, and pelagic sediments lower the solidus of the overlying mantle wedge.
  • Geochemical Signature: Subduction fluids selectively transport soluble Large Ion Lithophile Elements (LILE: Cs, Rb, Ba, Sr, Pb) into the mantle wedge, while High Field Strength Elements (HFSE: Nb, Ta, Zr, Hf, Ti) are retained in residual refractory titanate phases (such as rutile) in the subducting slab. This produces the universally diagnostic negative Nb–Ta anomaly and positive Pb anomaly in VAB, which is entirely absent in MORB.
PART II
DGMS 2019 Examination: Question & Worked Solution
Exhaustive option-by-option critical appraisal and definitive textbook-grounded answer
❓ Original Question — DGMS 2019 (Junior Scientific Officer)

Q.25. Which one of the following Mid-Oceanic Ridge Basalts (MORB) is derived from an incompatible element-poor mantle?

(a) N-MORB
(b) P-MORB
(c) T-MORB
(d) VAB
Source: DGMS Junior Scientific Officer Exam 2019, Question 25. Reference Screenshot: Screenshot from 2026-10-12 23-14-41.png.
✓
CORRECT OPTION: (a) N-MORB
N-MORB ("Normal" MORB) is derived from the Depleted MORB Mantle (DMM), an incompatible element-poor upper mantle reservoir left behind after ancient extraction of continental crust.
🔬 Exhaustive Option-by-Option Critical Analysis
(a) N-MORB (Normal Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalt) CORRECT

Why it is correct: "N" stands for Normal. N-MORB constitutes the vast majority (~85%) of volcanic rock generated along oceanic spreading ridges globally. It is derived by adiabatic decompression partial melting of the Depleted MORB Mantle (DMM)—the convecting upper asthenospheric mantle above ~660 km depth.

  • Trace Element Depletion: The DMM experienced previous melt extraction events (most notably the progressive formation of the continental crust throughout the Archean and Proterozoic). Because incompatible elements preferentially enter the liquid phase during melting, the residual DMM was left severely depleted in highly incompatible trace elements (Cs, Rb, Ba, Th, U, Nb, Ta, K, La, Ce).
  • Rare Earth Fingerprint: N-MORB possesses a characteristic positive LREE slope on chondrite-normalized diagrams, with normalized (La/Sm)N < 1.0 (typically < 0.7), demonstrating that the source had already lost its light rare earths.
  • Isotopic Depletion: High time-integrated Sm/Nd and low Rb/Sr in the depleted mantle result in highly depleted isotopic compositions: low ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr (< 0.7025) and high ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd (> 0.5131, εNd = +10 to +12).

Winter (2010, p. 300) explicitly writes: "We can thus distinguish between N-MORB ('normal' MORB), which taps the depleted, or incompatible-poor mantle, and E-MORB ('enriched' MORB, also called P-MORB for 'plume'), which taps an incompatible-richer mantle." Thus, (a) is unequivocally the correct answer.

(b) P-MORB (Plume-type Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalt / E-MORB) INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: "P" stands for Plume (also termed E-MORB for Enriched MORB). P-MORBs occur along ridge segments that are situated near or directly influenced by deep-seated mantle plumes and hotspots (e.g., the Mid-Atlantic Ridge near Iceland, the Azores, or Tristan da Cunha, and the Galapagos Spreading Center).

P-MORBs are derived from an incompatible element-rich (fertile or metasomatized) mantle source, not an incompatible element-poor mantle. They have elevated concentrations of K₂O (>0.10 wt.%), TiO₂ (>1.0 wt.%), P₂O₅, and enriched LREE profiles with negative slopes where (La/Sm)N > 1.8. Their radiogenic isotopes plot toward enriched reservoirs (higher ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr ~ 0.7030–0.7045 and lower ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd). Therefore, P-MORB represents the opposite of an incompatible element-poor melt.

(c) T-MORB (Transitional Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalt) INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: "T" stands for Transitional. T-MORBs exhibit geochemical characteristics intermediate between N-MORB and E-MORB/P-MORB, with (La/Sm)N ratios typically ranging between 0.8 and 1.8.

As documented by Schilling et al. (1983) and Winter (2010, p. 301), chemical and isotopic data demonstrate that T-MORBs do not represent a unique, distinct third mantle layer, but are formed by the mixing of depleted N-MORB melts with enriched E-MORB plume melts during magma ascent and storage in axial magma chambers. Because T-MORBs involve significant contributions from the enriched mantle component, they are not derived from a purely incompatible element-poor mantle source.

(d) VAB (Volcanic Arc Basalts) INCORRECT

Why it is incorrect: VAB stands for Volcanic Arc Basalts. This option is a double category error:

  1. Not a MORB: VAB is erupted at convergent plate boundaries (subduction zones), such as island arcs and continental volcanic arcs (e.g., the Marianas, Aleutians, Andes). The question specifically asks for a Mid-Oceanic Ridge Basalt (MORB).
  2. Not incompatible element-poor: VABs are strongly enriched in Large Ion Lithophile Elements (LILE: Cs, Rb, Ba, Sr, Pb) and LREE derived from hydrous slab fluids fluxing the mantle wedge. Furthermore, they display a profound depletion in HFSE (Nb, Ta, Ti), producing the classic subduction-zone negative Nb–Ta anomaly.
PART III

🎯 High-Yield Curated Practice Questions (DGMS, UPSC GSI, GATE & IIT-JAM)
  • Practice Question 1 (UPSC Geologist 2022 Pattern):
    Which of the following rare earth element ratios is globally utilized as the primary diagnostic slope index to discriminate between LREE-depleted N-MORB and LREE-enriched E-MORB?
    Answer: (La/Sm)N (Chondrite-normalized Lanthanum-to-Samarium ratio).
    Explanation: Because HREEs (Tb to Lu) have virtually overlapping, flat concentrations in both N-MORB and E-MORB (controlled by garnet/spinel melting physics), the overall slope ratio (La/Lu)N or (La/Yb)N can be misleading if residual garnet is involved. In contrast, (La/Sm)N directly measures the slope across the light rare earth elements where incompatible element fractionation is most pronounced: (La/Sm)N < 1.0 defines N-MORB, while (La/Sm)N > 1.8 defines E-MORB.
  • Practice Question 2 (GATE Geology 2021 Pattern):
    In a diagram of ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd versus ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr, the Depleted MORB Mantle (DMM) source plots in which quadrant relative to the Bulk Silicate Earth (BSE)?
    Answer: Upper-left quadrant (high ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd, low ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr).
    Explanation: Continental crust formation extracted incompatible elements over 3.8+ Ga, depleting the upper mantle in Rb relative to Sr (low Rb/Sr) and in Nd relative to Sm (high Sm/Nd). Over geologic time, low Rb/Sr yielded minimal ⁸⁷Sr accumulation (⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr < 0.7025), whereas high Sm/Nd yielded substantial ¹⁴³Nd accumulation (¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd > 0.5131, εNd = +10 to +12).
  • Practice Question 3 (IIT-JAM Geology Pattern):
    According to the complementary crust–mantle evolution model of Hofmann (1988), which planetary process was primarily responsible for depleting the N-MORB mantle source in incompatible elements?
    Answer: The irreversible, episodic extraction of the continental crust from the primitive mantle.
    Explanation: Incompatible trace elements (Cs, Rb, Ba, Th, U, K, LREE) have bulk solid/liquid distribution coefficients D << 1. During partial melting, they strongly partition into the magma. Because low-density, buoyant felsic continental crust cannot be subducted back into the convective mantle, its growth sequestered these elements, leaving the upper convecting asthenosphere as a depleted, incompatible-poor residue (DMM).
  • Practice Question 4 (UPSC GSI Mains Analytical Question):
    Explain how Volcanic Arc Basalts (VAB) can be distinguished from Mid-Ocean Ridge Basalts (MORB) on multi-element spidergrams using High Field Strength Elements (HFSE).
    Answer: Presence of a marked negative Nb–Ta anomaly and positive Pb anomaly in VAB.
    Explanation: In subduction zones, aqueous fluids from the slab transport water-soluble LILE (Cs, Rb, Ba, Sr, Pb) into the mantle wedge, but cannot transport insoluble HFSE (Nb, Ta, Zr, Hf, Ti), which remain bound in residual titanate minerals (rutile) in the slab. Consequently, VAB displays very high Ba/Nb (>30) and low Nb/U (<10), manifesting as a sharp trough at Nb–Ta on spidergrams. In MORBs (both N-MORB and E-MORB), no negative Nb–Ta anomaly exists (Nb/U ≈ 47).
  • Practice Question 5 (DGMS Mining / Geology 2018 Pattern):
    What is the primary physical mechanism of mantle melting that produces parental magmas at mid-ocean spreading centers?
    Answer: Decompression melting (adiabatic upwelling of mantle peridotite).
    Explanation: Plates pull apart at divergent boundaries, causing underlying asthenospheric mantle peridotite to ascend passively. Because the ascent is rapid relative to conductive thermal loss, the path is adiabatic (~0.3°C/km). The solidus slope (dP/dT) of peridotite is much steeper than the adiabat, so the upwelling rock intersects the dry solidus at depths between 60 and 80 km, generating basaltic melts without any external heat or water addition.
  • Practice Question 6 (Petrology Competitive Exam):
    Along the Mid-Atlantic Ridge near Iceland, basalts with flat REE patterns and intermediate incompatible element abundances ((La/Sm)N ≈ 1.0–1.6) are classified as:
    Answer: T-MORB (Transitional MORB).
    Explanation: Where deep mantle plumes impinge on oceanic spreading centers (e.g., the Iceland hotspot on the Mid-Atlantic Ridge), ascending enriched plume diapir melts mix with ambient depleted N-MORB melts within sub-ridge melting regimes and shallow magma chambers, producing hybrid transitional lavas termed T-MORBs.
  • Practice Question 7 (Isotope Geochemistry Specialist Exam):
    Why do both N-MORB and OIB share identical canonical ratios of Nb/U = 47 ± 10 and Ce/Pb = 25 ± 5, despite vastly different absolute abundances?
    Answer: Because Nb and U (and Ce and Pb) have identical bulk partition coefficients during purely oceanic mantle melting and differentiation.
    Explanation: Hofmann et al. (1986) demonstrated that DNb ≈ DU and DCe ≈ DPb during igneous processes in the oceanic mantle. Fractionation only occurs when continental crust or subduction fluids are involved, where aqueous fluids fractionate Pb from Ce (producing Ce/Pb < 5 in arcs and crust) and rutile fractionates Nb from U (producing Nb/U < 10 in continental crust).
  • Practice Question 8 (Advanced Igneous Petrology):
    During fractional crystallization of a parental MORB magma in a shallow crustal axial magma chamber, which pair of trace elements will decrease rapidly in the liquid?
    Answer: Nickel (Ni) and Chromium (Cr).
    Explanation: Ni has a very high partition coefficient in olivine (DNiol/liq >> 1, typically 5–15), and Cr is strongly compatible in magnesiochromite and clinopyroxene (DCr >> 1). Consequently, early fractional crystallization of olivine + spinel immediately depletes the derivative MORB melt in Ni and Cr, while incompatible elements (Zr, Ti, Y, K) steadily increase.

DGMS 2019 • SECTION D • QUESTION 26

Mantle Reservoirs & Isotopic Geochemistry: The Bulk Silicate Earth (BSE) and Chondritic Uniform Reservoir (CHUR)

Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) Junior Scientific Officer Examination 2019 | Standard Petrology & Geochemistry Reference Guide

📋 Question Statement

Q.26. Which one of the following geochemical reservoirs is defined by the Nd-isotopic composition of chondritic meteorites?
a (a) DM (Depleted Mantle)
b (b) BSE (Bulk Silicate Earth)
c (c) PREMA (Prevalent Mantle)
d (d) HIMU (High-µ Mantle)
✅ Correct Answer: (b) BSE (Bulk Silicate Earth)

📖 Official Textbook Figures & Mantle Reservoir Diagrams

Figure 1: Winter (2010), Chapter 14, Figure 14-8. 143Nd/144Nd versus 87Sr/86Sr systematics for ocean basalts (MORB and OIB), showing the mantle reservoirs proposed by Zindler and Hart (1986). Notice the exact central placement of BSE (Bulk Silicate Earth / bulk Earth) at εNd = 0 (143Nd/144Nd = 0.512638) and 87Sr/86Sr ≈ 0.7045–0.7050.
Figure 2: Winter (2010), Chapter 14, Table 14-5. Approximate Isotopic Ratios of Various Mantle Reservoirs. Note the entry for BSE: 143Nd/144Nd = 0.51264 (the exact chondritic meteorite value), 87Sr/86Sr = 0.7052, 206Pb/204Pb = 18.4.
Figure 3: William M. White (2005), Chapter 8, Figure 8.9. (a) Nd isotope evolution in mantle and crust over 4.56 Ga. The bold horizontal reference line demonstrates the evolution of the bulk earth or CHUR (chondritic uniform reservoir). (b) Evolution of bulk earth, crust, and mantle in εNd notation, where Bulk Earth/CHUR is locked permanently at εNd ≡ 0.
Figure 4: William M. White (2005), Chapter 8, Figure 8.11. Sr and Nd isotope ratios in major geochemical reservoirs. The composition of the bulk Earth (BSE) lies exactly at the intersection of the horizontal datum line at εNd = 0 and 87Sr/86Sr ≈ 0.705.

🔍 In-Depth Option Evaluation & Geochemical Breakdown

(b) BSE (Bulk Silicate Earth) — [CORRECT]

Definition: The Bulk Silicate Earth (BSE), also termed the Primitive Mantle (PUM), represents the bulk composition of the Earth's silicate portion (crust + mantle) prior to any differentiation into continental crust and residual depleted mantle, but after core formation.

Why Chondritic Nd Isotopes Define BSE: Both Samarium (Sm) and Neodymium (Nd) are Refractory Lithophile Elements (RLE).

  • Refractory: They possess extremely high condensation temperatures in the solar nebula (> 1400 K), meaning they did not volatilize during planetary accretion.
  • Lithophile: They do not dissolve in metallic iron and thus did not enter the Earth's core during planetary core segregation.

Because neither element was fractionated by volatility nor core separation, the whole silicate Earth must have the exact same Sm/Nd ratio as chondritic meteorites. Consequently, the Nd-isotopic evolution of the Bulk Silicate Earth is modeled by the Chondritic Uniform Reservoir (CHUR) (DePaolo & Wasserburg, 1976). Today, CHUR and BSE have an identical 143Nd/144Nd value of 0.512638, defining εNd ≡ 0!

(a) DM (Depleted Mantle / DMM) — [INCORRECT]

Characteristics: Depleted Mantle (DM) is the residue left behind in the upper mantle after the extraction of continental crust over geological time. It is the source reservoir for normal mid-ocean ridge basalts (N-MORB).

Isotopic Signature: During partial melting of the primitive mantle, Nd is more incompatible than Sm (DNd < DSm), so Nd partitioned preferentially into the crustal melt. This left the residual mantle (DM) with a higher Sm/Nd ratio than chondrites. Over 2–3 billion years of 147Sm decay, DM evolved to higher 143Nd/144Nd ratios (0.5131–0.5136), corresponding to strongly positive εNd values (+10 to +12). Hence, DM is distinctly non-chondritic!

(c) PREMA (Prevalent Mantle) — [INCORRECT]

Characteristics: Proposed by Zindler and Hart (1986), PREMA represents a common focal component toward which many oceanic island basalt (OIB) arrays converge from different geographic sectors.

Isotopic Signature: PREMA has 143Nd/144Nd ≈ 0.5128–0.5130 (εNd ≈ +4 to +6), intermediate between DM and BSE, and 87Sr/86Sr ≈ 0.7033. It represents a mildly depleted, well-mixed mantle component (possibly lower mantle or entrained plume material), not the chondritic reservoir.

(d) HIMU (High-µ Mantle) — [INCORRECT]

Characteristics: HIMU derives its name from "high µ" (where µ = 238U/204Pb). It was created by ancient subduction and mantle storage (~1.5–2.0 Ga) of recycled altered oceanic crust (basaltic slabs) that lost Pb during subduction dehydration, resulting in an exceptionally elevated U/Pb ratio.

Isotopic Signature: Characterized by extremely radiogenic lead isotopes (206Pb/204Pb > 20.5–21.5), very low 87Sr/86Sr (~0.7028), and intermediate 143Nd/144Nd (0.5128–0.5129, εNd ≈ +4). It has no genetic or isotopic equivalence to chondritic meteorites.

⚛️ Theoretical Foundations: The Sm-Nd Isotopic System & CHUR

1. Radioactive Decay Scheme

The Sm-Nd isotopic system is based on the alpha decay of 147Sm to 143Nd:

Sm-Nd Alpha Decay Law:
147Sm → 143Nd + α (decay constant λ = 6.54 × 10-12 yr-1, half-life t1/2 = 106 Ga)

(143Nd / 144Nd)present = (143Nd / 144Nd)initial + (147Sm / 144Nd) × (eλt - 1)

2. The CHUR (Chondritic Uniform Reservoir) Concept

In 1976, Donald DePaolo and Gerald Wasserburg introduced the Chondritic Uniform Reservoir (CHUR) model. They analyzed carbonaceous and ordinary chondritic meteorites and demonstrated that their Sm/Nd ratios are remarkably uniform:

Present-Day CHUR Standard Parameters:
(147Sm / 144Nd)CHUR0 = 0.1967
(143Nd / 144Nd)CHUR0 = 0.512638 (normalized to 146Nd/144Nd = 0.7219)

3. The Epsilon Neodymium (εNd) Notation

Because differences in 143Nd/144Nd occur only in the 4th and 5th decimal places, DePaolo and Wasserburg defined parts per 10,000 deviations from CHUR/BSE:

Epsilon Nd Equation:
εNd = [ ((143Nd/144Nd)sample / (143Nd/144Nd)CHUR) - 1 ] × 10,000

• If εNd = 0 → Sample is identical to Bulk Silicate Earth (BSE / CHUR).
• If εNd > 0 → Sourced from Depleted Mantle (DM), higher Sm/Nd ratio (LREE-depleted).
• If εNd < 0 → Sourced from Enriched Reservoir / Continental Crust, lower Sm/Nd ratio (LREE-enriched).

📊 Comprehensive Comparison of Zindler & Hart (1986) Mantle Reservoirs

Reservoir Full Name & Origin 143Nd/144Nd εNd 87Sr/86Sr 206Pb/204Pb Chondritic Relationship
BSE Bulk Silicate Earth (Primitive Mantle / PUM) 0.512638 0 0.7045–0.7052 18.40 Identical to Chondritic Meteorites (CHUR)
DM (DMM) Depleted Mantle (MORB source, crust residue) 0.5131–0.5136 +10 to +12 0.7015–0.7025 15.5–17.7 Non-chondritic (High Sm/Nd, depleted in Nd)
EM-I Enriched Mantle I (Subducted lower crust / pelagic sed) < 0.5112–0.5124 -12 to 0 0.7045–0.7055 17.6–17.8 Non-chondritic (Low Sm/Nd, enriched in Nd)
EM-II Enriched Mantle II (Subducted upper crust / terrigenous sed) 0.5110–0.5122 -15 to -2 > 0.707–0.722 16.3–17.3 Non-chondritic (Very high Rb/Sr, crustal)
HIMU High-µ Mantle (Recycled dehydrated oceanic crust) 0.5128–0.5129 +4 to +5 0.7028–0.7030 > 20.5–21.8 Non-chondritic (Extreme U/Pb fractionation)
PREMA Prevalent Mantle (Common focal mixing component) 0.5128–0.5130 +4 to +6 0.7033–0.7035 18.2–18.5 Non-chondritic (Intermediate depleted)
FOZO Focus Zone (Deep primitive/plume mantle, high 3He/4He) 0.5128–0.5130 +4 to +6 0.7030–0.7040 18.5–19.5 Non-chondritic (Deep lower mantle component)

📚 Direct Textbook Quotations & Citations

John D. Winter (2010), Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology, Chapter 14 (Oceanic Intraplate Volcanism), p. 275:

"A second reservoir may be a non-depleted mantle, called BSE (bulk silicate Earth) by Zindler and Hart (1986) and primitive upper mantle (PUM) or primary uniform reservoir by others. BSE reflects the isotopic signature of the primitive mantle as it would evolve to the present without any subsequent fractionation of radioactive elements (which presumably were extracted to form continental and oceanic crust)... In Table 14-5, BSE has 143Nd/144Nd = 0.51264 and 87Sr/86Sr = 0.7052."
— Winter (2010), p. 275 & Table 14-5
William M. White (2005), Geochemistry, Chapter 8 (Radiogenic Isotope Geochemistry), p. 332:

"The Earth as a whole has a 147Sm/144Nd ratio identical to that of chondrites (0.1967), and the initial 143Nd/144Nd of the Earth is also the same as that of chondrites. Thus the bulk Earth evolves along the same line as chondrites. Because at any time, εNd is the deviation from chondritic 143Nd/144Nd, the bulk Earth, as well as chondritic meteorites always have εNd = 0."
— White (2005), p. 332
Kula C. Misra (2012), Introduction to Geochemistry: Principles and Applications, Chapter 12 (Radiogenic Isotopes), p. 312:

"The concept of CHUR (Chondritic Uniform Reservoir) is based on the assumption that the Earth's mantle has evolved with a Sm/Nd ratio equal to that of chondritic meteorites... The present-day 143Nd/144Nd ratio of CHUR (0.512638) defines the Bulk Silicate Earth (BSE)."
— Misra (2012), p. 312

🎯 Exam Traps & Rapid Elimination Strategy

⚡ Rule of Thumb for UPSC Geo-Scientist & DGMS:
  1. Whenever a question connects chondritic meteorites / CHUR to a mantle reservoir, immediately look for BSE (Bulk Silicate Earth) or PUM (Primitive Upper Mantle). They are synonymous in isotope geochemistry.
  2. DM (Depleted Mantle) is always associated with N-MORB, high positive εNd (+10 to +12), and low 87Sr/86Sr.
  3. HIMU is always associated with high 206Pb/204Pb (> 20.5) and recycled oceanic crust.
  4. EM-I / EM-II are always associated with negative εNd, high 87Sr/86Sr, and recycled continental crust / sediments.
  5. PREMA is the common convergence point of OIB mixing arrays (εNd ≈ +4 to +6).
Igneous Petrology & Phase Equilibria / Question 27
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 • SECTION D • QUESTION 27

Equilibrium Mineral Assemblages: The Forsterite–Quartz Incompatibility & Phase Petrology

Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) Junior Scientific Officer Examination 2019 | Igneous Petrology & Phase Equilibria Guide

📋 Question Statement

Q.27. Which of the following do NOT constitute an equilibrium mineral assemblage in a rock?
a (a) Quartz and orthoclase
b (b) Forsterite and quartz
c (c) Hornblende and andesine
d (d) Labradorite and augite
✅ Correct Answer: (b) Forsterite and quartz (Chemically incompatible pair)

📖 Official Textbook Phase Diagrams (Winter 2010, Chapter 7)

Figure 1: John D. Winter (2010), Chapter 7, Figure 12. Isobaric T–X phase diagram of the binary system Forsterite–Silica (Mg2SiO4–SiO2) at 0.1 MPa (1 atm). Note the intermediate compound Enstatite (En: MgSiO3), which melts incongruently at 1557°C to Forsterite + Liquid. At equilibrium, the system is split into two mutually exclusive sub-systems: (1) Fo + En (silica-undersaturated) and (2) En + Qtz (silica-oversaturated). Forsterite and Quartz can never coexist across the intermediate Enstatite phase barrier!
Figure 2: John D. Winter (2010), Chapter 7, Figure 13. Schematic enlargement of the peritectic reaction tie-line: Forsterite + Liquid (silica-rich) ⇌ Enstatite. Any cooling magma containing excess SiO2 consumes all forsterite to form enstatite before quartz can ever crystallize.
Figure 3: John D. Winter (2010), Chapter 7, Figure 15. The Fo–SiO2 system at atmospheric pressure (bottom) and elevated pressure (top). At elevated pressures, enstatite melts congruently, creating a permanent thermal divide between Fo-bearing rocks and Qtz-bearing rocks.

🔍 In-Depth Option Evaluation & Petrographic Analysis

(b) Forsterite and quartz — [DOES NOT CONSTITUTE AN EQUILIBRIUM ASSEMBLAGE • CORRECT]

Chemical Incompatibility: Forsterite (Mg2SiO4) is a highly silica-undersaturated nesosilicate (olivine group). Quartz (SiO2) is pure silica (tectosilicate). In the MgO–SiO2 system, an intermediate chain silicate phase exists: Enstatite (MgSiO3).

The Incompatibility Reaction: Under all geological crustal and upper-mantle conditions (T < 1557°C and P < 10 GPa), forsterite and quartz react completely and irreversibly to form enstatite:

Forsterite–Quartz Breakdown Reaction:
Mg2SiO4 (Forsterite) + SiO2 (Quartz) ⟶ 2 MgSiO3 (Enstatite)
ΔG°reaction < 0 (Thermodynamically spontaneous)

Because the Gibbs free energy of this reaction is strongly negative, a rock can contain either (Forsterite + Enstatite) in silica-undersaturated compositions, or (Enstatite + Quartz) in silica-oversaturated compositions, but NEVER Forsterite + Quartz together in thermodynamic equilibrium!

Note on Fe-rich systems: While Mg-olivine (Forsterite) is incompatible with quartz, Fe-rich olivine (Fayalite, Fe2SiO4) CAN coexist with quartz in highly differentiated ferroan granites and rhyolites because pure ferrosilite (FeSiO3) is unstable at low pressures. The question explicitly specifies Forsterite, making this an absolute thermodynamic incompatibility!

(a) Quartz and orthoclase — [STABLE EQUILIBRIUM PAIR]

Rock Types: Granite, Rhyolite, Granitic Pegmatite, Aplite, Felsic Gneiss.

Phase Relations: Quartz (SiO2) and Orthoclase (KAlSi3O8) form the classic low-temperature eutectic in "Petrogeny's Residua System" (Quartz–Albite–Orthoclase). They crystallize together in mutual thermodynamic equilibrium as the terminal assemblage of granitic melts.

(c) Hornblende and andesine — [STABLE EQUILIBRIUM PAIR]

Rock Types: Diorite, Andesite, Amphibolite (metabasite).

Phase Relations: Hornblende (calcic amphibole) and Andesine (intermediate plagioclase, An30–An50) define the diagnostic mineral assemblage of intermediate calc-alkaline magmas as well as the standard amphibolite facies of regional metamorphism. They are in complete thermodynamic equilibrium.

(d) Labradorite and augite — [STABLE EQUILIBRIUM PAIR]

Rock Types: Basalt, Gabbro, Diabase / Dolerite.

Phase Relations: Labradorite (calcic plagioclase, An50–An70) and Augite (calcic clinopyroxene) form the principal cotectic mineral pair of mafic igneous rocks worldwide, coexisting stably in equilibrium to produce ophitic, subophitic, and intergranular textures.

⚛️ Petrological Foundations: Silica Saturation & Bowen's Reaction Series

1. The Concept of Silica Saturation (Shand, 1927)

Igneous rocks and mineral assemblages are divided into three fundamental categories based on their silica activity:

Category Definition Diagnostic Minerals Forbidden Minerals (Incompatible)
Silica-Oversaturated Contains free primary SiO2 Quartz, Cristobalite, Tridymite, Hypersthene Forsterite, Nepheline, Leucite, Sodalite
Silica-Saturated Exact stoichiometric silica; no free quartz, no unsaturated phases Enstatite, Diopside, Feldspars (Albite, Anorthite, Orthoclase) Quartz + Forsterite together
Silica-Undersaturated Deficient in SiO2; forms silica-poor minerals Forsterite, Nepheline, Leucite, Kalsilite, Melilite Quartz

2. Bowen's Discontinuous Reaction Series

N. L. Bowen (1928) demonstrated that during fractional crystallization of basaltic magmas:

Bowen's Discontinuous Branch:
Olivine (Fo) ⟶ Orthopyroxene (En) ⟶ Clinopyroxene (Augite) ⟶ Amphibole (Hornblende) ⟶ Biotite

• At the peritectic reaction: Forsterite + Liquid ⟶ Enstatite.
• In a silica-rich melt, all forsterite is resorbed before quartz can form.
• In a silica-poor melt, quartz never forms because all silica is consumed.

📚 Direct Textbook Quotations & Citations

John D. Winter (2010), Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology, Chapter 7, p. 121:

"Olivine crystals begin to form at 1800°C, they continue to grow with cooling, and then they begin to be consumed back into the melt as a new mineral, enstatite, forms... You might also recognize this olivine ⟶ pyroxene transition as the first step in the 'discontinuous' left side of Bowen's Reaction Series... Because enstatite forms between forsterite and silica, forsterite and quartz cannot coexist in equilibrium."
— Winter (2010), Chapter 7, p. 121
Anthony R. Philpotts & Jay J. Ague (2009), Principles of Igneous and Metamorphic Petrology, 2nd ed., Chapter 10:

"Because enstatite is intermediate in composition between forsterite and quartz, the reaction Fo + Qtz = 2 En prevents the coexistence of forsterite and quartz at equilibrium in any igneous or metamorphic rock. Rocks containing olivine and quartz are either non-equilibrium mixtures or contain iron-rich olivine (fayalite)."
— Philpotts & Ague (2009), Chapter 10

🎯 Exam Traps & Rapid Elimination Strategy

⚡ Standard Competitive Exam Incompatible Pairs (Forbidden Assemblages):
  1. Forsterite + Quartz: Always incompatible! They react to form Enstatite / Orthopyroxene (Fo + Qtz ⟶ 2 En).
  2. Nepheline + Quartz: Always incompatible! They react to form Albite (Ne + 2 Qtz ⟶ Ab).
  3. Leucite + Quartz: Always incompatible! They react to form K-feldspar (Lc + Qtz ⟶ Kfs).
  4. Kalsilite + Quartz: Always incompatible! They react to form K-feldspar (Kls + 2 Qtz ⟶ Kfs).
  5. Corundum + Quartz: Always incompatible! They react to form Al2SiO5 (Crn + Qtz ⟶ Ky/Sil/And).
Sedimentary Environments & Coastal Dynamics / Question 28
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 • SECTION E • QUESTION 28

Coastal Hydrodynamics: Wave-Dominated Coastline Zonation from Land to Sea

Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) Junior Scientific Officer Examination 2019 | Sedimentology & Coastal Processes Guide

📋 Question Statement

Q.28. In a wave-dominated coastline, the correct distribution of environment from land to sea is:
a (a) Shoaling zone – surf zone – breaker zone – swash zone
b (b) Swash zone – shoaling zone – surf zone – breaker zone
c (c) Swash zone – surf zone – breaker zone – shoaling zone
d (d) Surf zone – shoaling zone – swash zone – breaker zone
✅ Correct Answer: (c) Swash zone – surf zone – breaker zone – shoaling zone
🏖️ LANDWARD (Beach) ➔ Swash Zone ➔ Surf Zone ➔ Breaker Zone ➔ Shoaling Zone ➔ SEAWARD (Offshore) 🌊

📖 Official Textbook Figures (Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology & Stratigraphy)

Figure 1: Sam Boggs Jr. (4th/5th Ed.), Chapter 9, Figure 9.21. Generalized cross-sectional profile of the beach and nearshore zone, showing the principal zones of wave activity. Observe the seaward progression from the subaerial beach: Foreshore / Swash Zone (run-up on beach face) ⟶ Surf Zone (inner translation bores) ⟶ Breaker Zone (where waves break) ⟶ Shoaling Zone (where waves feel bottom in shoreface).
Figure 2: Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 9, Figure 9.24. Zonation of sedimentary structures and facies across the shoreface, nearshore, and foreshore.

🔍 In-Depth Option Evaluation & Spatial Logic

(c) Swash zone – surf zone – breaker zone – shoaling zone — [CORRECT]

Spatial Progression: This sequence accurately reflects the hydrodynamic and morphological zones encountered when traveling from the dry land (beach berm) outward into the open sea:

  • 1. Swash Zone (Landward-most): The intertidal beach face (foreshore) alternately covered by upswash (run-up) and backwash (gravity drainage).
  • 2. Surf Zone: The zone of high-velocity translatory bores moving landward between the breaker bar and the swash limit.
  • 3. Breaker Zone: The narrow band where steepening waves become dynamically unstable and break violently ($H/h \approx 0.78$).
  • 4. Shoaling Zone (Seaward-most): The shoreface region from fair-weather wave base ($10\text{–}15\text{ m}$) where oscillatory deep-water waves begin interacting with the seafloor.

(a) Shoaling zone – surf zone – breaker zone – swash zone — [INCORRECT DIRECTION / REVERSED TRAP]

This option represents the progression from SEA TO LAND (seaward to onshore), but it scrambles the position of the breaker and surf zones. Waves enter the shoaling zone first, break in the breaker zone, move as surf, and run up as swash. Crucially, the prompt requested FROM LAND TO SEA!

(b) Swash zone – shoaling zone – surf zone – breaker zone — [INCORRECT]

The shoaling zone is located out at sea (lower to middle shoreface). Placing it immediately adjacent to the swash zone completely violates physical coastal architecture.

(d) Surf zone – shoaling zone – swash zone – breaker zone — [INCORRECT]

Completely disorganized hydrodynamic order; swash zone is landward of surf zone, and shoaling zone is outermost.

📊 Hydrodynamic & Sedimentological Characteristics of Coastal Zones

Zone (Land ➔ Sea) Morphological Sub-environment Dominant Hydrodynamic Process Diagnostic Sedimentary Structures Grain Size & Sorting
1. Swash Zone (Landward) Foreshore / Beach Face Alternating upswash and backwash flows; thin sheet flows Low-angle planar lamination (dipping 2°–8° seaward), heavy-mineral placer laminae Medium to coarse sand, exceptionally well-sorted
2. Surf Zone Upper Shoreface / Nearshore Trough Translational bores, longshore currents, rip current generation Trough & planar cross-bedding, multidirectional wave ripples, subaqueous bar bedforms Fine to coarse sand, moderately to well-sorted
3. Breaker Zone Middle Shoreface / Breaker Bar Crest Violent wave collapse, maximum turbulent kinetic energy, high bed shear Coarse lag deposits, steep rip-channel scours, high-angle onshore-dipping cross-beds Coarse sand, granules, shells; poorly sorted lag
4. Shoaling Zone (Seaward) Lower Shoreface (Fair-weather wave base) Symmetrical & asymmetrical oscillatory bottom currents ("feeling the bottom") Symmetrical wave ripples, hummocky cross-stratification (HCS), Ophiomorpha burrows Very fine to fine sand grading seaward to silt/mud

📚 Direct Textbook Quotations & Citations

Sam Boggs Jr. (2006), Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy, 4th ed., Chapter 9, pp. 324–325:

"As waves progress farther shoreward into the shallow shoaling zone (Fig. 9.21), forward velocity of the wave slows, wave length decreases, and wave height increases. The waves eventually steepen and become unstable, collapsing in the breaker zone... In the surf zone, a high-velocity translation wave (bore) is projected up the upper shoreface... At the shoreline, the surf zone gives way to the swash zone, in which a rapid, very shallow swash flow moves up the beach."
— Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 9, pp. 324–325
H. G. Reading (1996), Sedimentary Environments: Processes, Facies and Stratigraphy, 3rd ed., Chapter 8:

"The beach profile in cross-section from the subaerial barrier or mainland to offshore comprises the swash zone on the foreshore, passing seawards into the surf zone, bounded externally by the breaker zone, which in turn grades into the offshore shoaling wave zone."
— H. G. Reading (1996), Chapter 8

🎯 Exam Traps & Rapid Elimination Strategy

⚡ Essential Directional Rule for UPSC & DGMS Geology:
  1. Read the Direction Carefully: Examiners love flipping between "from land to sea" and "from sea to land".
    • Land to Sea: Swash ➔ Surf ➔ Breaker ➔ Shoaling.
    • Sea to Land: Shoaling ➔ Breaker ➔ Surf ➔ Swash.
  2. Remember the Beach Experience: When you stand with your toes on the dry sand, water splashes your feet in the Swash zone. When you step into waist-deep water where waves are tumbling, you are in the Surf zone. Further out where surfers wait for waves to pitch and curl, that is the Breaker zone. Out where boats bob on gentle rolling swells, that is the Shoaling zone.

DGMS 2019 • SECTION E • QUESTION 29

Deltaic Environments: Subaqueous Delta Zonation, Delta Front & Prodelta Architecture

Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) Junior Scientific Officer Examination 2019 | Sedimentology & Deltaic Depositional Systems Guide

📋 Question Statement

Q.29. The uppermost part of subaqueous delta that lies at water depth down to 10 m or so, is referred to as:
a (a) Upper delta plain
b (b) Lower delta plain
c (c) Prodelta
d (d) Delta front
✅ Correct Answer: (d) Delta front (Uppermost subaqueous delta, depth 0 to ~10 m)

📖 Official Textbook Figure (Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology & Stratigraphy)

Figure 1: Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 9, Figure 9.12. Principal physiographic components and facies of a delta system. Note the major boundary between the Subaerial Delta Plain (Upper Delta Plain above high-tide level, Lower Delta Plain in the intertidal zone) and the Subaqueous Delta, which is subdivided into the Delta Front (uppermost part down to ~10 m depth; sand and silt mouth bars) and the Prodelta (deeper seaward slope down to 200–300 m; hemipelagic mud and silt).

🔍 In-Depth Option Evaluation & Petrographic Analysis

(d) Delta front — [CORRECT]

Verbatim Definition from Sam Boggs Jr. (p. 316):
"The subaqueous delta plain lies seaward of the lower deltaic plain below low-tide water level. The uppermost part of the subaqueous delta, lying at water depths down to 10 m or so, is commonly called the delta front."

Hydrodynamics & Sediments: The delta front is the high-energy seaward interface where river effluents meet standing basin water. Sediments consist dominantly of distributary-mouth-bar sands, sheet sands, and coarse silts with cross-bedding and ripple laminations, winnowed by waves and tidal currents.

(c) Prodelta — [INCORRECT]

Characteristics: The prodelta is the deeper seaward part of the subaqueous delta lying beyond the delta front. It extends from ~10–20 m down to water depths of 200–300 m where it merges into the open marine shelf.

Sediments: Lacks coarse sands; dominated by hemipelagic suspension deposition of fine clays, muds, and thin silt turbidites.

(a) Upper delta plain — [INCORRECT • SUBAERIAL]

Characteristics: Part of the subaerial delta, not the subaqueous delta. It lies completely above high-tide water level and is governed strictly by subaerial fluvial processes (braided/meandering channels, point bars, natural levees, backswamps, and freshwater marshes).

(b) Lower delta plain — [INCORRECT • INTERTIDAL SUBAERIAL]

Characteristics: Extends between the low-tide mark and the upper limit of tidal influence. While periodically inundated during high tide, it is subaerially exposed at low tide and features interdistributary bays, mangrove swamps, salt marshes, and tidal channels. It is not part of the continuously submerged subaqueous delta.

📊 Structural Architecture & Facies of Classic Delta Systems

Realm Subdivision Depth / Elevation Dominant Processes Diagnostic Facies / Sediments
Subaerial Delta (Deltaic Plain) Upper Delta Plain Above high-tide level (Landward) Fluvial channels, overbank floods, crevasse splay Fluvial sands, gravels, floodplain muds, coal/peat
Lower Delta Plain Intertidal zone (Between low & high tide) Mixed fluvial and tidal currents, marine inundation Interdistributary bay muds, tidal creek sands, mangrove peat
Subaqueous Delta (Below Low Tide) Delta Front Below low tide down to ~10 m depth River-jet deceleration, wave winnowing, longshore drift Distributary-mouth-bar sands, sheet sands, cross-bedded sandstones
Prodelta From ~10 m down to 200–300 m (Seaward slope) Low-energy suspension fallout, gravity slumps, turbidity currents Dark marine muds, clays, bioturbated silts, prodelta claystones

📚 Direct Textbook Quotations & Citations

Sam Boggs Jr. (2006), Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy, 4th ed., Chapter 9, p. 316:

"The subaqueous delta plain lies seaward of the lower deltaic plain below low-tide water level. The uppermost part of the subaqueous delta, lying at water depths down to 10 m or so, is commonly called the delta front. The remaining seaward part of the subaqueous delta is called the prodelta, or prodelta slope... The prodelta may extend to water depths as much as 200–300 m."
— Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 9, p. 316
J. M. Coleman & D. B. Prior (1982), Deltaic Sand Bodies, AAPG Continuing Education Course Note Series 15:

"The delta front environment encompasses the subtidal delta platform from the low water datum to water depths of 10–12 meters. It represents the zone of most active sand deposition, forming distributary-mouth-bar complexes."
— Coleman & Prior (1982)

🎯 Exam Traps & Rapid Elimination Strategy

⚡ Rule of Thumb for Deltaic Questions in UPSC & DGMS:
  1. Subaerial vs. Subaqueous: If the question mentions subaqueous, immediately eliminate Upper Delta Plain and Lower Delta Plain.
  2. Delta Front vs. Prodelta:
    • Delta Front: Shallow (0 to ~10 m depth), high energy, sandy, mouth bars.
    • Prodelta: Deep (10 m to 300 m depth), low energy, muddy/clayey, prodelta slope.

DGMS 2019 • SECTION E • QUESTION 30

Sedimentary Dynamics: Bedform Succession with Increasing Bed Shear Stress & Flow Regimes

Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) Junior Scientific Officer Examination 2019 | Physical Sedimentology & Hydrodynamics Guide

📋 Question Statement

Q.30. Which one of the following sequences of sedimentary structures is correct in terms of increasing bed shear?
a (a) Parting lineation, dune, flute mark, ripple
b (b) Ripple, dune, parting lineation, flute mark
c (c) Dune, parting lineation, flute mark, ripple
d (d) Flute mark, parting lineation, ripple, dune
✅ Correct Answer: (b) Ripple, dune, parting lineation, flute mark
⚡ INCREASING BED SHEAR STRESS (τ₀):
Current Ripple ➔ Subaqueous Dune ➔ Parting Lineation (Upper Plane Bed) ➔ Flute Mark (Turbulent Scour)

📖 Official Textbook Figures (Sam Boggs Jr., Principles of Sedimentology & Stratigraphy)

Figure 1: Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 4, Figure 4.9. The succession of bedforms that develops during unidirectional flow of sandy sediment (0.25–0.7 mm) as flow velocity and bed shear stress increase. From bottom to top: Ripples ➔ Dunes ➔ Upper Plane Bed (Parting Lineation) ➔ Antidunes / Chutes & Pools.
Figure 2: Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 4, Figure 4.54. Parting lineation (primary current lineation) formed on bedding planes of flat-laminated sandstones deposited under the upper flow regime at high bed shear stress.
Figure 3: Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 4, Figure 4.36. Flute casts on the sole of a turbidite sandstone bed. Flutes represent the highest bed shear and turbulent scour produced by concentrated horizontal fluid vortices eroding into cohesive mud before sand deposition.

🔍 In-Depth Option Evaluation & Hydrodynamic Progression

(b) Ripple, dune, parting lineation, flute mark — [CORRECT]

Hydrodynamic Succession with Increasing Bed Shear (τ₀ = ρ Cd U²):

  • 1. Ripples (Lowest Bed Shear): Form just above the threshold velocity for sediment motion in the Lower Flow Regime. Small-scale bedforms (λ < 20 cm, H < 4 cm) where grains saltate over the stoss side and avalanche down the lee slope.
  • 2. Dunes (Moderate Bed Shear): Form at higher velocity and stream power in the Lower Flow Regime. Large-scale bedforms (λ > 0.6–1 m, H > 5 cm) with active flow separation and wake vortices over the crest.
  • 3. Parting Lineation / Upper Stage Plane Bed (High Bed Shear): Upper Flow Regime (Froude number Fr ≈ 1). Dunes are washed out and sheared flat into flat laminae. High-speed and low-speed streaks generated by streamwise turbulent boundary-layer vortices align elongate grains into parting step lineations.
  • 4. Flute Marks (Highest Bed Shear & Extreme Turbulent Scour): Produced by hyper-turbulent erosive vortices at the head of high-energy flows (e.g., turbidity currents or catastrophic surges) scouring directly into the bed before deposition can begin. Basal shear stress exceeds even that of plane-bed deposition!

(a, c, d) Incorrect Permutations — [INCORRECT]

• (a) Starts with Parting lineation (Upper flow regime) followed by Dune (Lower flow regime) — non-monotonic shear order.
• (c) Places Ripple (lowest shear) at the very end after Flute mark (highest shear) — inverted.
• (d) Inverted sequence (from highest to lowest shear: Flute mark ➔ Parting lineation ➔ Ripple/Dune), but the question explicitly demands increasing bed shear!

📊 Comprehensive Hydrodynamic & Bedform Parameters

Bedform / Structure Flow Regime Froude Number (Fr) Relative Bed Shear (τ₀) Internal Stratification Produced
Current Ripples Lower Flow Regime Fr << 1 (0.1–0.3) Lowest (Just above critical threshold τcr) Small-scale planar and trough cross-lamination (ripple cross-laminae)
Subaqueous Dunes Lower Flow Regime Fr < 1 (0.3–0.8) Moderate Medium- to large-scale tabular and trough cross-bedding
Upper Plane Bed (Parting Lineation) Upper Flow Regime Fr ≈ 0.8–1.2 High (Dunes planed off; intense sheet flow) Parallel horizontal plane lamination, parting current lineation
Flute Marks (Turbulent Scour) Erosional High-Energy Regime Fr > 1 (Highly turbulent flow head) Highest (Violent boundary-layer vortex cavitation and erosion) Sole marks on base of thick graded turbidite beds (Ta division)

📚 Direct Textbook Quotations & Citations

Sam Boggs Jr. (2006), Principles of Sedimentology and Stratigraphy, 4th ed., Chapter 4, pp. 83–85:

"Numerous flume experiments have established that under unidirectional flow in sand, the succession of bedforms that develops as flow velocity and bed shear stress increase is: ripples ⟶ dunes ⟶ plane bed (with parting lineation) ⟶ antidunes."
— Sam Boggs Jr., Chapter 4, p. 83
J. D. Collinson & D. B. Thompson (1989), Sedimentary Structures, 2nd ed., Chapter 4 & 5:

"Flutes are produced by localized turbulent scour of a cohesive mud bed by fluid vortices generated in high-velocity, high-shear flows prior to deposition... In terms of boundary shear stress, flute erosion represents the upper boundary of flow power, exceeding the threshold required for plane-bed transport."
— Collinson & Thompson (1989)

🎯 Exam Traps & Rapid Elimination Strategy

⚡ Universal Flow Regime Rule:
  1. Always anchor the two endpoints:
    • Lowest Shear: Ripples (appear immediately when sand starts moving).
    • Highest Shear / Scour: Flute Marks (requires extreme turbulence to scour cohesive mud before sand settles).
  2. Between Ripples and Flutes: Dunes (Lower Flow Regime) come before Parting Lineation (Upper Flow Regime plane bed).
  3. Therefore, order: Ripple ➔ Dune ➔ Parting Lineation ➔ Flute Mark!
Seismology & Earth Interior / Question 31
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 • SECTION G • QUESTION 31

Global Seismology & Tectonics: Earthquake Focal Depths & The Wadati-Benioff Zone

Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) Junior Scientific Officer Examination 2019 | Seismotectonics & Plate Margins Guide

📋 Question Statement

Q.31. The deep-focused earthquakes commonly originate along:
a (a) Mid-oceanic ridge
b (b) Continental rift
c (c) Transform fault
d (d) Benioff zone
✅ Correct Answer: (d) Benioff zone (Wadati-Benioff subduction zone down to ~670 km)

📖 Official Textbook Figure (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Earth Structure)

Figure 1: B. A. van der Pluijm & S. Marshak, Chapter 17, Figure 17.6. Cross-sections showing earthquake foci defining the inclined Wadati-Benioff zone in the Western Pacific (Izu-Bonin and Mariana Arcs). Earthquakes penetrate continuously from shallow depths (< 70 km) near the trench through intermediate depths (70–300 km) down to deep-focus depths (300 to ~670 km) strictly within the cold, descending subducted lithospheric slab.

🔍 In-Depth Option Evaluation & Seismotectonic Architecture

(d) Benioff zone (Wadati-Benioff Zone) — [CORRECT]

Seismotectonic Mechanism: Named after seismologists Kiyoo Wadati and Hugo Benioff, the Wadati-Benioff zone is a dipping planar zone of seismicity tracing the trajectory of cold, dense oceanic lithosphere subducting into the warm mantle asthenosphere.

Focal Depth Distribution: Because ordinary mantle at depths > 50–70 km is too hot and ductile for brittle frictional sliding, earthquakes can only occur where cold, rigid rock is transported downwards. The subducting slab remains cold and brittle down to 670–700 km (the upper mantle transition zone), enabling deep-focus earthquakes (300–670 km) via dehydration embrittlement and transformational faulting (metastable olivine ⟶ spinel/ringwoodite phase changes). Subduction zones are the ONLY tectonic environments on Earth where deep-focus earthquakes occur!

(a) Mid-oceanic ridge — [INCORRECT • SHALLOW ONLY]

Mid-ocean ridges are divergent plate boundaries characterized by high heat flow and thin, newly formed lithosphere. Brittle failure is strictly limited to the uppermost 10–15 km (shallow-focus earthquakes only) along extensional rift grabens.

(b) Continental rift — [INCORRECT • SHALLOW ONLY]

Continental rifts (e.g., East African Rift, Baikal Rift) involve crustal thinning and elevated geothermal gradients. Earthquakes are restricted to the brittle upper crust at depths < 20–30 km (shallow-focus).

(c) Transform fault — [INCORRECT • SHALLOW ONLY]

Transform faults (e.g., San Andreas Fault, Alpine Fault, oceanic fracture zones) represent strike-slip motion between plates. Frictional locking and rupture occur exclusively in the upper seismogenic crust, rarely exceeding 15–20 km depth.

📊 Global Earthquake Focal Depth Classification & Plate Margins

Classification Focal Depth (h) Typical Tectonic Environments Dominant Rupture Mechanism
Shallow-Focus 0 – 70 km Mid-ocean ridges, continental rifts, transform faults, collision zones, subduction trenches Classic brittle frictional sliding & cataclasis (Byerlee's Law)
Intermediate-Focus 70 – 300 km Subduction zones exclusively (Wadati-Benioff zone) Dehydration embrittlement of serpentinite/hydrous minerals
Deep-Focus 300 – 670 km Wadati-Benioff zones exclusively (Subduction slabs) Transformational faulting (metastable olivine ➔ spinel/wadsleyite transition)

📚 Direct Textbook Quotations & Citations

B. A. van der Pluijm & S. Marshak (2004), Earth Structure: An Introduction to Structural Geology and Tectonics, 2nd ed., p. 217 & 434:

"Earthquakes in subduction zones define an inclined belt, called the Wadati-Benioff zone, that extends from the trench down to depths as great as 670 km... In fact, it is the distribution of earthquakes in the Wadati-Benioff zone that outlines the geometry of subducting slabs. Deep-focus earthquakes occur only where cold, rigid slabs penetrate into the mantle."
— Van der Pluijm & Marshak, p. 217 & 434
William Lowrie (2007), Fundamentals of Geophysics, 2nd ed., Cambridge University Press, Chapter 3:

"Deep-focus earthquakes (depths > 300 km) are unknown along mid-ocean ridges or transform faults. They occur solely along the Wadati-Benioff zones associated with subduction at convergent plate boundaries, terminating abruptly at the 670 km seismic discontinuity."
— William Lowrie, Fundamentals of Geophysics

🎯 Exam Traps & Rapid Elimination Strategy

⚡ Rule of Thumb for Earthquake Depths:
  1. Mid-Ocean Ridges & Transform Faults: NEVER have deep earthquakes! Always shallow (< 20 km).
  2. Continental Rifts: Always shallow (< 30 km).
  3. Deep-Focus Earthquakes (> 300 km): Found ONLY in Wadati-Benioff zones of subduction zones!
Historical Geology & Plate Tectonics / Question 32
Standalone View PDF Guide
DGMS 2019 • SECTION E • QUESTION 32

Paleogeography & Global Tectonics: Laurasia, Laurentia & Mesozoic Continental Breakup

Directorate General of Mines Safety (DGMS) Junior Scientific Officer Examination 2019 | Historical Geology & Supercontinent Architecture Guide

📋 Question Statement

Q.32. A major continent that existed before 90 Ma and comprised present-day N. America, Greenland, Europe and part of Asia is referred to as:
a (a) Superia
b (b) Gondwanaland
c (c) Laurentia
d (d) Laurasia
✅ Correct Answer: (d) Laurasia (Comprising North America, Greenland, Europe, and Asia)

📖 Official Textbook Figure (Van der Pluijm & Marshak, Earth Structure)

Figure 1: B. A. van der Pluijm & S. Marshak, Chapter 21, Figures 21.2.1 & 21.2.2. Paleogeographic and tectonic map showing the northern composite continent Laurasia (consisting of North America, Greenland, Europe, and Asia) separated by the Tethys Ocean from Gondwanaland to the south.

🔍 In-Depth Option Evaluation & Paleogeographic Analysis

(d) Laurasia — [CORRECT]

Constituents & Age: Following the initial rifting of Pangea during the Early Mesozoic (~180–150 Ma), the northern continental landmass was termed Laurasia. It comprised:

  • North America & Greenland (Laurentian craton)
  • Europe (Baltica and Avalonian terranes)
  • Asia (Siberian, Kazakh, North China, South China cratons, excluding peninsular India)

Laurasia existed as a coherent northern supercontinent until the Late Cretaceous (~90–60 Ma), when the opening of the North Atlantic Ocean and Norwegian Sea finally separated North America/Greenland from Eurasia.

(c) Laurentia — [CRITICAL EXAM DISTRACTOR / INCOMPLETE]

Distinction from Laurasia: Laurentia refers strictly to the ancestral North American cratonic shield (North America, Greenland, and parts of NW Scotland). It does NOT include Europe or Asia! Laurasia = Laurentia + Eurasia.

(b) Gondwanaland — [INCORRECT • SOUTHERN HEMISPHERE]

Gondwanaland was the southern supercontinent comprising South America, Africa, Madagascar, India, Antarctica, and Australia. It was separated from northern Laurasia by the Tethys Seaway.

(a) Superia — [INCORRECT • ARCHEAN CRATON]

Superia is an ancient Neoarchean supercraton (~2.7–2.5 Ga) formed by the temporary assembly of the Superior, Hearne, Wyoming, and Karelian cratons. It predates the Mesozoic by over 2.3 billion years!

📊 Major Global Supercontinents & Cratonic Amalgamations

Landmass Constituent Landmasses Geological Era / Age Hemisphere / Location
Laurasia N. America + Greenland + Europe + Asia (excl. India) Mesozoic (200 Ma to ~90–65 Ma) Northern Hemisphere
Laurentia North America + Greenland (+ NW Scotland) Paleoproterozoic to present (Ancient craton) North American nucleus only
Gondwanaland South America + Africa + India + Antarctica + Australia Neoproterozoic to Mesozoic (~550 to 150 Ma) Southern Hemisphere
Pangea Laurasia + Gondwanaland (All global continents assembled) Late Paleozoic to Early Mesozoic (~335 to 175 Ma) Global supercontinent
Superia Superior + Hearne + Wyoming + Karelian cratons Neoarchean (~2.7 to 2.45 Ga) Archean supercraton

📚 Direct Textbook Quotations & Citations

B. A. van der Pluijm & S. Marshak (2004), Earth Structure, Chapter 21, p. 542:

"During the Mesozoic, the supercontinent Pangea rifted along the east-west trending Tethys Ocean, separating the northern landmass comprising North America and Eurasia (called Laurasia) from the southern part of Pangea comprising South America, Africa, India, Australia, and Antarctica (called Gondwana)."
— Van der Pluijm & Marshak, p. 542

🎯 Exam Traps & Rapid Elimination Strategy

⚡ Formula for Rapid Recall:
  • Laurentia: North America + Greenland alone.
  • Eurasia: Europe + Asia.
  • Laurasia = Laurentia + Eurasia: North America + Greenland + Europe + Asia.
